November 8, 2024 iv

 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
 This report describes research and development (R&D) activities conducted during Fiscal Year 2024
 (FY24) specifically related to the Engineered Barrier System (EBS) R&D Work Package in the Spent
 Fuel and High-Level Waste Disposition Campaign supported by the United States (U.S.) Department of
 Energy (DOE). The R&D activities focus on understanding EBS component evolution and interactions
 within the EBS, as well as interactions between the host media and the EBS. The R&D team represented
 in this report consists of individuals from Sandia National Laboratories, Lawrence Berkeley National
 Laboratory (LBNL), Los Alamos National Laboratory (LANL), and Vanderbilt University. EBS R&D
 work also leverages international collaborations to ensure that the DOE program is active and abreast of
 the latest advances in nuclear waste disposal.
 High-level Purpose of this work: A primary goal of the EBS work package is to advance the
 development of process models that can be implemented directly within the Generic Disposal System
 Analysis (GDSA) platform. Improved process models can contribute to the safety case in several ways,
 such as:
 • building confidence and improving the robustness of the performance assessment,

 • providing further insight into the processes being modeled, establishing better constraints on
 parameters critical to repository performance, and

 • assessing technical readiness of EBS design components.

 A major focus of this work is to understand the processes that will impact and control the permeability
 and/or transport in the EBS. Permeability evolution in the geotechnical seal system is typically a major
 driver of repository performance, or otherwise an important scenario that needs to be evaluated in self-
 sealing host media. The interfaces of the EBS components, including the disturbed rock zone (DRZ), play
 a critical role in the near-field environment, where complex processes have a significant impact on overall
 transport and fate of radionuclides.
 Improved understanding of these interfaces requires capabilities to characterize, test, and model the EBS,
 in such a way that relevant complex phenomena are captured and represented. EBS processes are often
 coupled, e.g., thermal- hydrologic-mechanical-chemical (THMC), multi-scale (from molecular to
 continuum scale), and nonlinear (e.g., multi-phase flow).
 The FY24 EBS report includes results from modeling, analysis, and experimental work and is focused on
 several key themes:
 • Improved understanding and model representation of coupled processes, e.g., thermal-hydrologic-
 mechanical-chemical (THMC), in EBS materials, with a particular focus on swelling behavior
 and high temperature effects on bentonite buffer materials.

 • Investigation and characterization at multiple length scales – from molecular to mesoscale to
 continuum scale - to understand near-field process models for radionuclide retention and transport
 through the EBS and disturbed rock zone.

 • Field scale testing and analysis to validate and improve process modelling tools and overall
 conceptual understanding of complex processes in the in situ setting.

 These themes are investigated in various ways within the EBS work package, with projects ranging from
 molecular dynamic simulations to bench-scale testing and characterization, and all the way up to the drift-
 scale testing and demonstration in underground research laboratories.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                     v

 FY24 Accomplishments: Overall, significant progress has been made in FY24 towards developing the
 modeling and analysis tools and experimental and characterization capabilities needed to investigate the
 performance of EBS materials and the associated interactions in the drift and the surrounding near-field
 environment under a variety of conditions, including high temperature regimes. This includes the
 follwing accomplishments from the FY24 EBS work package:
 • Particpation in the the planning, implementation, and design of the HotBENT Field Test at the
 Grimsel Test Site in Switzerland.

 • Continued development of a state-of-the-art micro-oedometric experimental capability that
 utilizes the Advanced Light Source to study the role of groundwater chemistry on the structure
 and swelling behavior of smectitite clay minerals, e.g. bentonite buffer.

 • Development of a computational pipeline to generate synthetic datasets, train AI/ML models for
 molecular scale modeling of radionuclide retention by bentonites – this, in turn, can used to
 parametrize meso- and macroscopic models of radionuclide migration in a bentonite barrier to
 develop a robust safety assessment model.
 • Development of an improved model for diffusion in bentonite clays via bench-scale experiments
 to better understand diffusion-dominated transport as a function of clay interlayer cation
 composition and groundwater
 • Modeling and completion of bench-scale testing of a bentonite clay column in support of the
 HotBENT Field Test
 • Validation of THMC modelling capabilities for bentonite via validation against international URL
 field tests
 • Completion of a series of hydrothermal experiments to study EBS materials interactions (e.g., waste
 package materials, host, buffer materials) and the associated characterization and analysis.
 • Finalized DECOVALEX2023 deliverables, which consisted primarily of a modelling validation
 exercise as participants in the DECOVALEX2023, Task C
 • Development of novel, state-of-the-art workflow for analysis and characterization of chemo-
 mechanical process at EBS/host rock interfaces

 FY25 Priorities:
 • Continued particpation in HotBENT testing support, modelling, and analysis

 • Continued activity on mutli-scale bentonite studies on THMC representation, diffusion, and
 molecular-scale investigations

 • Begin transition to next phase of DECOVALEX2027 and/or other internaional activities based on
 EBS work package priorities and integration with GDSA modelling needs

 • Upscale testing of boehmite anionic sorbent materials to bench scale testing

November 8, 2024 vi

 Figure 3-1. AI-surrogates for the SCM of uranium sorption to oxides: (a) multioutput
 regressor consisting of 12 estimators (random forests), each trained to
 predict a single target value (one from 12 outputted values) based on an
 input vector containing model parameters/system description (12 input
 values), and (b) the DNN-surrogate consists of 5 hidden layers (each with
 512 neurons), as well as input and output layers (both containing 12
 neurons). The input consists of a description of the oxide surface (surface
 area, site density), the SCM parameter values, such as affinity constants
 (logKi) capacitances, and a description of solution composition (12 values).
 The output consists of charge densities, electrostatic potential in each layer,
 and concentrations of surface complexes (12 values). See Li et al. (2024) for
                      more details. ................................................................................................................... 22
 Figure 3-2. Comparison of charge densities and potentials predicted by the numerical
 solver and estimated by the DNN-surrogate – illustrated for the case when (a)
 the numerical solver converges for all pH values and (b) when the numerical
 solver fails to converge. Note that even if the numerical solver cannot find a
 unique solution, the DNN-surrogate can still provide the pH-dependent
                      titration data. Figures taken from Li et al. (2024). ....................................................... 23
 Figure 3-3. Performance of DNN-surrogate for uranium sorption on oxides. Parity plot for
 DNN-surrogate model prediction vs. numerically calculated ground truth. The
 score represents the goodness of fit of surrogate predictions to
 corresponding ground truth values. MSE represents the mean squared error
                      between prediction and target. The absolute error (|GT - Pred|) between

 EVALUATION OF ENGINEERED BARRIER SYSTEMS FY24 REPORT

 1 INTRODUCTION
 This report describes research and development (R&D) activities conducted during Fiscal Year
 2024(FY24) specifically related to the Engineered Barrier System (EBS) R&D Work Package in the
 Spent Fuel Waste Science and Technology (SFWST) Campaign supported by the United States (U.S.)
 Department of Energy (DOE). This report fulfills the deliverable M2SF-24SN010308051.

 The R&D activities focus on understanding EBS component evolution and interactions within the
 EBS, as well as interactions between the host media and the EBS. The R&D team represented in this
 report consists of individuals from Sandia National Laboratories, Lawrence Berkeley National
 Laboratory (LBNL), Los Alamos National Laboratory (LANL), and Vanderbilt University. EBS R&D work
 also leverages international collaborations to ensure that the DOE program is active and abreast of
 the latest advances in nuclear waste disposal.

 High-level Purpose of this work: A primary goal of the EBS work package is to advance the
 development of process models that can be implemented directly within the Generic Disposal
 System Analysis (GDSA) platform. Improved process models can contribute to the safety case in
 several ways, such as:
 • building confidence and improving the robustness of the performance assessment,
 • providing further insight into the processes being modeled, establishing better
 constraints on parameters critical to repository performance, and
 • assessing technical readiness of EBS designs and/or components.

 A major focus of this work is to understand the processes that will impact and control the
 permeability and/or transport in the EBS. Permeability evolution in the geotechnical seal system is
 typically a major driver of repository performance, or otherwise an important scenario that needs to
 be evaluated in self-sealing host media. The interfaces of the EBS components, including those with
 the disturbed rock zone (DRZ), play a critical role in the near-field environment, where complex
 processes have a significant impact on overall transport and fate of radionuclides.
 Improved understanding of these interfaces requires capabilities to characterize, test, and model the
 EBS, in such a way that relevant complex phenomena are captured and represented. EBS process
 are often coupled, e.g., thermal- hydrologic-mechanical-chemical (THMC), multi-scale (from molecular
 to continuum scale), and nonlinear (e.g., multi-phase flow).
 The FY23 EBS report includes results from modeling, analysis, and experimental work and is focused
 on several key themes:
 • Improved understanding and model representation of coupled processes, e.g., thermal-
 hydrologic-mechanical-chemical (THMC), in EBS materials, with a particular focus on swelling
 behavior and high temperature effects on bentonite buffer materials.
 • Investigation and characterization at multiple length scales – from molecular to
 mesoscale to continuum scale - to understand near-field process models for radionuclide
 retention and transport through the EBS and disturbed rock zone.
 • Field scale testing and analysis to validate and improve process modelling tools and
 overall conceptual understanding of complex processes in the in-situ setting.
 These themes are investigated in various ways within the EBS work package, with projects ranging
 from molecular dynamic simulations to bench-scale testing and characterization, and all the way up
 to the drift-scale testing and demonstration in underground research laboratories.
 The contents of this reports are summarized in detail below:

November 8, 2024                                                                                               1

 • Chemical Controls of Montmorillonite Structure and Swelling Pressure (Section 2)
 Section 2 presents the progress of research activity combining microscopic experimental and
 simulation studies to address the mechanical-chemical coupling in bentonite. A series of laboratory
 experiments were conducted to determine the influence of water chemistry and hydration time on
 the clay swelling and microstructural changes. We applied an μ- oedometer system equipped with X-
 ray transparent cells holding compacted montmorillonite. This system allowed us to determine the
 chemically- and time-resolved swelling pressure and microstructural changes using the SAXS
 technique. Our SAXS measurement profiles show the presence of mixed interlayer hydration states
 (0w – 4w) and osmotic swelling. Generally, the interlayer crystalline basal and osmotic swelling
 spacing (d001) increases with decreasing water ionic strength and varies with cation type (Na+ vs.
 K+), hydration time, and clay density. Specifically, (i) the hydration state is dominated by 3w at NaCl
 solutions vs. 2w dominates at KCl solutions. This indicates that K+ leads to lower swelling pressures;
 (ii) we observed the osmotic swelling peak with an intermediate spacing d001 = 40.3 Å at 0.001 M
 NaCl, which decreases to 35.7 Å at 0.01 M NaCl and 34.3 Å at 0.1 M NaCl; (iii) we also observed a
 mixture of 4w, 3w, and 2w layer hydrates and a strong osmotic peak (45.5 Å) at 0.001 M KCl, which
 leads to a d001 spacing larger than that observed at 0.001 M NaCl, suggesting the hydration time is
 an important factor given the difference in hydration times (14 days for 0.001 M KCl clay cell vs. 7
 days for the 0.001 M NaCl), and (iv) the hydrate and osmotic swelling peaks are stronger at lower
 clay density. This is the first work experimentally determined the clay osmotic swelling spacing for
 compacted montmorillonite using unique SAXS measurements.

 • Modeling of Radionuclide Retention by Bentonite (Section 3)
 Section 3 presents an approach to implement thermodynamic models of U(III) and U(V) hydrolysis and
 estimated, for the first time, the free energies and corresponding equilibrium constants for eight
 reactions of U(III) and U(V) in water, leading to various type of hydroxy complexes. The new chemistries
 were combined with the already known speciation characteristics of U(VI) and U(IV) to develop a more
 comprehensive description of uranium aqueous speciation at varying pH and redox conditions.
 Additionally, a uranium retention model was applied to carbonate sediments with different degrees of
 compaction. This work incorporated equilibrium speciation and sorption model with the overlapping
 electrical double layers theory. The molecular dynamics simulations of the distant-dependent dielectric
 constant of the solution in contact with the calcite surface also assisted the model parametrization.

 • Experimental Investigations of Bentonite and Other Clay-based Materials (Section 4)
 Section 4 presents the results of experimental and modeling studies of the diffusion and adsorption
 of Se. Diffusion experiments revealed that an increase in the Ca/Na ratio in solution and on clay
 surfaces leads to a rise in HTO, Se(VI), and Se(IV) diffusivity in montmorillonite samples. The increase
 of HTO diffusivity can be explained by a change in clay microstructure wherein the presence of
 exchangeable Ca promotes the formation of thicker layer stacks compared to Na, which in turn
 fosters the presence of larger interparticle pores. These microstructural changes will affect the
 transport of all radionuclides through clay barriers, including Se. In addition, the aqueous speciation
 of Se(VI) has a significant effect on Se transport. Se(VI) diffusion is limited by anion exclusion due to
 the formation of the negatively charged species SeO42-. However, in the presence of Ca2+, forming
 CaSeO4 neutral ion pairs, which can access a more significant portion of the total porosity, increases
 selenate diffusivity. We provide constraints on model parameters that can be used to predict a range
 of Se(VI) transport in engineered barrier systems. The results also emphasize the need to
 understand the overall chemical evolution of repository conditions as changes in aqueous speciation
 due to many factors (e.g., mineral dissolution/precipitation or groundwater penetration) can change
 the mobility of essential species.

 • Coupled Microbial-Abiotic Processes in EBS and Host Rock Materials (Section 5)

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   2

 Section 5 presents the study of microbial-abiotic processes in EBS and host rock materials.
 experiments were conducted on FEBEX materials collected to explore the potential microbial activity.
 These materials encompassed different temperature zones, including high heat, intermediate, and
 control zones. The objective was to test the hypothesis that even after long-term emplacement,
 viable organisms capable of various metabolic processes, including H2 utilization, persist in the
 system. Samples were incubated in non-enriched minimal conditions, and experiments were
 designed to determine if these materials possess microbial communities with the ability to
 metabolize H2 or other substrates and how the FEBEX treatment impacted those capabilities. The
 findings indicate that there is still a considerable metabolic potential within the microbial
 communities, underscoring the significant role of microbial metabolism in long-term nuclear waste
 disposal repositories employing clayey materials as a barrier.

 • Heating and Hydration Column Test on Bentonite (Section 6)
 Section 6 presents the benchtop-scale high-temperature column experiments, HotBENT-Lab, in
 support of the HotBENT field tests. HotBENT Lab #2 test using granulated Wyoming bentonite has
 been completed, and analysis of the clays is ongoing. Additional analysis of the coupon surface
 changes and interpretation of the XRD analysis is needed. The metals analysis will be re-done for
 washed/dried clay to remove pore water. Plans for collecting cation exchange capacity will also aid
 interpretation. Meanwhile, HotBENT #3, using Czech bentonite, is in the planning stage. This
 bentonite was used to pack a section of the NAGRA field test heaters. Due to problems with our XCT
 scanner, this experiment has been delayed. If the CT scanner is not replaced within a reasonable
 time frame, the experiment will be completed without this monitoring tool but will include ERT
 monitoring and additional sensors (such as humidity sensors) to monitor water content in the clay.
 Some column inlet and outlet design modifications are needed to prevent clogging experienced in
 HotBENT #2. This project is significant because the bulk of the nuclear waste design work in the US
 is done with modeling, and this experiment provides an opportunity to test and measure the
 parameters needed for the modeling efforts.

 • Modeling the Concrete/Bentonite Interface: Task 12 in the SKB EBS Task Force (Section 7)
 Section 7 presents a modeling effort as part of the Task 12 of SKB EBS Task Force to simulate the
 bentonite/concrete interaction. The overall goal of all modeling teams involved in this task is to
 collaboratively simulate long-term reactions occurring at cement-bentonite interfacial regions,
 considering mineralogical, chemical, and textural changes. In this section, we document the results
 of FY24 LBNL’s activities focused on simulations of the benchmark problem identified in Task 12 of
 the SKB EBS task force.

 • Understanding the THMC Evolution of Bentonite using Large-scale Field Experiments: HotBENT
 (Section 8)
 Section 8 presents the endeavor of using the large-scale field experiment HotBENT to understand the
 THMC evolution of bentonite under high temperature. The HotBENT field test is currently running at
 the Grimsel Test Site in Switzerland under the leadership of NAGRA and several international partners,
 including DOE. The HotBENT Project continued to progress in FY23 and FY24: all the heaters have
 reached their respective target temperatures, and the heating phase continues. Data is collected
 through the extensive sensor network, and data from different sectors are shared with the partners.
 LBNL actively participates in the HotBENT Modeling Platform, and the preliminary 2D TH model has
 been extended to a 3D TH model and a 2D THM model simulating the field test. The 3D TH model
 predictions match the trends observed in thermal and hydrodynamic behavior in the EBS. However,
 the current model underpredicts the temperature results in sectors surrounding the heaters with
 bentonite fill. The 2D THM model better captures the hydration behavior within the buffer,
 incorporating updated porosity introduced by the mechanical model.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                3

 • Hydrothermal Alteration of EBS Interfaces (Section 9)
 Section 9 summarizes hydrothermal experiments reacting EBS materials in an analogue crystalline
 host rock environment, specifically through experiments that include combinations of Wyoming
 bentonite + Grimsel granodiorite + Grimsel granodiorite synthetic groundwater ± stainless/low carbon
 steel ± OPC or low-pH cement. Results from these experiments include: 1) SEM images and EDS data,
 2) QXRD data, 3) clay mineral XRD data, 4) electron microprobe data for major mineral phases, and 5)
 aqueous geochemistry data.

 • Hydrothermal Testing to Support the HOTBENT Field Test (Section 10)
 Section 10 presents hydrothermal experiments were designed to understand geochemical and
 mineralogical changes that may occur in the EBS at the HotBENT conditions. Experiments completed
 in FY23 included different combinations of the two bentonites (FE and BCV) plus cement with
 groundwater (synthetic Grimsel granodiorite groundwater). In FY22, an experiment was completed that
 included Grimsel granodiorite wall rock as a reactant in addition to BCV bentonite and LCS.

 • Updated Thermal-Hydrologic Simulations of a Full-scale Heater Experiment in Opalinus Clay
 (Section 11)
 Section 11 presents preliminary thermal-hydrologic simulations of Task C, Step 1 were conducted
 using PFLOTRAN numerical code and a 3-D geometry. The simulations included blind predictions using
 parameter values developed in Step 0 of Task C, as well as calibration predictions with varied
 parameter values. Temperature evolution results at selected observation points were close to
 experimental data. Blind predictions of opalinus clay pressure values were much lower than
 experimental. Predicted pressure evolution at selected observation points for the calibration runs were
 very close to experimental data.

 • Development of Modified Boehmite Materials as Anion Sorbent Additive in EBS Design (Section
 12)
 Section 12 presents investigations into development of anionic sorbent material that could be used
 as an additive to bentonite buffer or as part of the plugging/sealing systems in the EBS. Anionic
 species (e.g., Iodine -129) typically drive repository performance, as transport of these species is not
 impeded as efficiently (relative to cationic species) by traditional EBS components. This effort is
 focused on modifying layered AlOOH (boehmite) to develop hybrid materials whose internal and
 external surfaces can be altered for high selectivity for specific radionuclides. Molecular modeling
 was used to evaluate glycoboehmite (GB) structural and spectroscopic properties as a function of
 butanediol loading, and batch sorption experiments were used to evaluate iodide uptake capacity of
 ion-exchanged GB.

 • Chemo-Mechanical Characterization of Cement-Rock Interfaces (Section 13)
 Section 13 presents research that: (i) characterizes chemical and mechanical changes within the
 alteration zones of both the cements and the minerals after controlled aging processes; (ii) identifies
 structural and chemical features that affect the fate of radionuclides at the interface of dissimilar
 materials; and (iii) provides a basis to verify predicted changes in major mineral phase composition
 and distribution that affect the diffusion-controlled transport of tracer constituents using reactive
 transport modeling.

 The above activities are integrated with the SFWST 2019 Roadmap. Please see Appendix A for an
 evaluation of progress towards addressing EBS-related SFWST Roadmap priorities.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  4

 2 CHEMICAL CONTROLS ON MONTMORILLONITE STRUCTURE AND
 SWELLING PRESSURE
 2.1 Introduction
 Bentonite is a clay material that is planned to be used in engineered and natural barrier systems
 such as potential U.S. Department of Energy (DOE) spent fuel and high-level radioactive waste
 repository, underground hydrogen (H2) and carbon dioxide (CO2) storage, oil and gas recovery and
 environmental remediation (Anderson et al., 2010; Birkholzer et al., 2012; Harvey & Lagaly, 2006;
 Saiyouri et al., 2004; SKB, 2011; Tournassat et al., 2015; Zheng et al., 2023). Because spent fuel
 and high-level radioactive waste contains fission products (e.g., 99Tc, 79Se, 135Cs) and transuranic
 elements (e.g., 238U, 239Pu) with long half-life radioactivity (Peterson et al., 2017), it needs to be
 safely isolated from human environments for several tens of thousands of years (Takahashi et al.,
 2015). Montmorillonite is the primary mineral phase of bentonite that has been proposed as a buffer
 and backfilling material in engineered barrier systems (EBS) for long-term geological repositories of
 high-level radioactive waste. Its ability to swell upon being hydrated by groundwater from host rock is
 a critical process for bentonite to perform its crucial safety function for sealing the repositories,
 which limits the transport to and from the bentonite barrier boundaries.
 The montmorillonite swelling-shrinking behavior is a dynamic process affected by geochemical and
 hydraulic changes that can lead to microstructural change. Determining the influence of water
 chemistry and saturation conditions on the microstructural change is thus essential for
 understanding and predicting the clay properties of swelling pressure, diffusivity, and permeability.
 This information is essential for ensuring the safety of those applications. Clay swelling occurs in two
 regimes, crystalline and osmotic, depending on the d001 spacing of the clay particles. The d001
 spacing is defined for clay particles as the sum of the interlayer distance and the thickness of one
 clay layer. Crystalline swelling (interlayer hydration) occurs when the d001 spacing increases from 10
 to 22 Å, and osmotic swelling occurs when the d001 spacing swells beyond 22 Å (Chen et al., 2022;
       Rao et al., 2013).
 X-ray powder diffraction (XRD) has been widely used to measure the clay crystalline basal spacing
 (i.e., interlayer water hydration states) of purified montmorillonites and bentonite as a function of
 relative humidity (RH) and aqueous solutions under free swelling and/or constrained conditions
 (Chaaya et al., 2023; Holmboe et al., 2012; Norrish, 1954; Paineau et al., 2011; Saiyouri et al.,
 2004; Takahashi et al., 2015; Wang et al., 2021). However, most XRD data reported in the literature
 were obtained under relatively low humidity (%RH) conditions that may not represent the EBS
 (Holmboe et al., 2012). In an actual repository environment, the bentonite clay in the EBS will be fully
 saturated with an aqueous phase containing different cations (e.g., Na+, K+, Ca2+, Mg2+) and go
 through a long-term hydration process. Neutron diffraction measurements were also performed to
 investigate crystalline basal spacing as a function of RH (Devineau et al., 2006). These XRD studies
 reported that with increasing water content, one water (1w), two water (2w), three water (3w) and
 sometimes four water (4w) layer hydrates were formed corresponding to d001 spacings of 12.2 –
       12.7 Å for 1w, 15.2 – 15.7 Å for 2w and 18.4 – 19 Å for 3w layer hydrate (Holmboe et al., 2012).
 The 4w layer hydrate has been reported by Norrish (1954) with d001 = 22.5 Å and Saiyouri et al.
 (2004) with a d001 = 21.6 Å. An osmotic spacing = 40 Å was calculated using a one-dimensional
 Fourier transform method based on low angle XRD intensity obtained by Norrish (1954) in 0.3 M
 NaCl saturated montmorillonite capillary. Meleshyn and Bunnenburg (2005) applied a Monte Carlo
 simulation approach and reported existing of a gap between d001 = 19 Å (3w) and d001 = 40 Å
 (osmotic) for Na-montmorillonite. However, these data are obtained using the low angle XRD
 measurements, which have difficulty determining the peaks of 4w layer hydrate and the osmotic
 swelling due to strong background intensity (noise signals) at diffraction angle 2θ ≤ 3 degrees.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  5

 Small-angle X-ray scattering (SAXS) measurements target a scattering vector q-range of 0.009 – 0.7
 Å-1, corresponding to actual d001 spacing distances between 700 - 9 Å to cover multi-scale nature of
 montmorillonite swelling spacing (0w – 4w and the osmotic swelling). The SAXS technique has
 become a very powerful tool to characterize the microstructural and swelling properties of
 montmorillonite and has been applied in aqueous dispersions (Paineau et al., 2011; Segad et al.,
 2012a, b). Its application for in-situ measurements of microstructural changes of compacted
 montmorillonite as a function of water chemistry has rarely been explored.
 The swelling pressure is primarily controlled by microstructural change, which is affected by water
 ionic strength (IS), accessible cation type, water saturation condition, and clay density. A high
 swelling pressure builds up when the clay swelling is constricted by a constant volume. The voids
 between the individual particles or the interparticle porosity could be expected to decrease (Holmboe
 et al., 2012) or even closed with continuous swelling pressure building up, particularly under low IS
 and long hydration time conditions due to the formation of intense osmotic swelling. In this work, we
 applied an µ-oedometer system and the SAXS technique at the Advanced Light Source (ALS) for
 measurements of chemically- and time-resolved swelling pressure and microstructural changes of
 compacted montmorillonite to assess the influence of (i) NaCl and KCl concentrations, (ii) hydration
 time and (iii) clay density. The objective is to determine how the IS, cation type (Na+ vs. K+), hydration
 time, and clay density affect the swelling pressure, microstructural change (crystalline and osmotic),
 and swell-shrink reversibility.

 2.2 Material and Methods
 2.2.1 Clay Sample and Characterization
 The montmorillonite sample used in this work was the Kunipia-F obtained from the Tsukinuno Mine
 in Yamagata prefecture, Japan (Kunimine Industry Co. Ltd.). Kunipia-F is a purified montmorillonite
 sample, obtained via hydraulic alteration without using any chemical substances. It has been well
 characterized and widely used in many applications (Morodome & Kawamura, 2011; Takahashi et
 al., 2015). The chemical formula for this purified montmorillonite is:
        • Na0.42Ca0.068K0.008(Al1.56Mg0.31)Fe(III)0.09Fe(II)0.01(OH)2(Si3.91Al0.09)O10.
 A unit crystal of Kunipia-F montmorillonite is a very thin (flat) plate, about 1 nm thick and 100 to
 1,000 nm wide. The layer charge of this high-charge montmorillonite was estimated to be 0.564 e-
 per formula unit. The cation exchange capacity (CEC) of Kunipia-F was determined using the BaCl2
 compulsive exchange method (Gillman & Sumpter, 1986) and provided in Table 2-1. The relative
 contribution of each cation to the CEC was obtained by measurement of the Ba2+ exchangeable
 cations using ICP-MS. The CEC = 113.4 meg/100g from this work is in good agreement with the
 literature reported CEC = 116 meq/100g (Morodome & Kawamura, 2011) for Kunipia-F and
 CEC = 109 meq/100g for Kunipia-G (Chaaya et al., 2023; Sato, 2008). The major exchangeable
 cations are Na+ (85%) and Ca2+ (11%), with the remaining minor cations of K+ (2.6%), Mg2+ (1.1%),
 and Sr2+ (0.2%). Before use, it was dried at 110 °C until a constant weight was achieved.

 CEC Exchangeable cation distribution (%)

                         meq/100g       Na+       K+     Mg2+      Ca2+     Sr2+
           Kunipia-F       113.4*       85.0     2.6      1.1     11.0      0.2                This work

           Kunipia-G         109         83       0        8       12        -            (Chaaya et al., 2023)

November 8, 2024                                                                                                    6

 2.2.2 Chemical Solutions
 Chemical solutions with variable NaCl and KCl concentrations were prepared to measure their
 influence on clay swelling pressure and microstructural change. These IS solutions include (1) 5.0 M,
       1.0, 0.5 M, 0.25 M, 0.1 M, 0.01 M and 0.001 M NaCl, and (2) 1.0 M, 0.1 M, 0.01 M and 0.001 M
 KCl. All solutions were prepared with Milli-Q water (18.2 MΩ·cm) and degassed before use. All
 chemicals used are ACS analytical grade or above.

 2.3 Clay Swelling Pressure Measurement Experiments
 As described in the previous EBS progress report (Zheng et al., 2023), an μ-oedometer system
 (Figure 2-1) allows us to measure chemically and time-resolved swelling pressure. It is equipped with
 an X-ray transparent custom-made PEEK (Polyetheretherketone) cell (TECAPEEK CF30, Ensinger
 Plastics) that holds clay samples in place and can be used to measure changes of microstructure
 and swelling pressure as a function of pore water chemistry in compacted montmorillonite. A syringe
 pump (Figure 2-1a) introduces chemical solutions into a small black PEEK clay cell through the
 bottom inlet to the top outlet. The cell is fixed on the -oedometer cell holder (labeled with a red
 circle in Figure 2-1b). The cell was packed with a dry clay sample and bounded on either side by
 permeable membrane filters and metal frits. The clay was confined by a displaceable piston.
 Miniscule displacements on the piston generated a force measurement (labeled with a small red
 rectangle in Figure 2-1b), which was displayed on the displacement sensor meter (left in Figure 2-1c)
 and the force sensor meter (right side in Figure 2-1c). Simultaneously, the force and displacement
 measurements were recorded digitally into a data-logging Excel macro (Figure 2-1e), which enabled
 us to measure the time-resolved swelling pressure. The automatic fraction collector (Figure 2-1d)
 was used to collect the effluent for chemical analysis, particularly to monitor the time-resolved cation
 exchange reaction of K+/Na+.

 Figure 2-1. Photograph of clay swelling experimental setup: (a) Harvard syringe pump to introduce
 chemical solutions, (b) -oedometer including black PEEK cell to hold clay sample (red
 small circle) and force sensor on cell (red small rectangle) to monitor cell force
 changes, (c) displacement sensor display (left) and force sensor display (right),
 (d) automatic fraction collector to collect effluent for chemical analysis, and
 (e) computer with software to digitally record time-resolved force and displacement
 changes.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  7

 To avoid the potential variability of clay packing, the same packed clay cell was used to flow through
 all chemical solutions by switching syringes and solutions. The syringe pump (Harvard Apparatus,
 Pump 33 or Chemyx Inc, Fusion 200) and the gas-tight precision glass syringes with PTFE
 (Polytetrafluoroethylene) tipped plunger and dispenser (Hamilton 1000 Series Syringes: 25 mL,
 10 mL, and 5 mL, Hamilton Company Inc.) were able to deliver the accurate flow rate (100, 50, 25,
 10 or 5 µL/hr in this work) and solution volume. In addition, using the gas-tight syringes also
 prevents the exposure of the degassed solutions from the atmosphere, which could potentially affect
 the clay pore space and cation exchange. Cells were packed (5 mm inner diameter, 9 mm height)
 with designed dry bulk clay densities of d = ~1.3 and 1.60 g/cm3). Before injecting solutions, the dry
 clay-packed cells were purged with research-grade CO2 to remove trapped air in the pore space. To
 measure the swelling force under a constant temperature (25°C), the µ-oedometer and clay cell
 were placed in a temperature-controlled chamber. Effluent samples were collected to analyze the
 concentrations of Na+, K+, Ca2+, Mg2+, Sr2+ and Ba2+ to determine cation exchange reactions and
 interpret the clay swelling/collapse mechanisms.

 2.4 X-Ray Scattering Measurement Experiments
 SAXS measurements were conducted at the X-ray scattering beamtime 7.3.3. at the ALS to
 investigate the microstructural changes of clay as a function of Na+ and K+ concentrations and clay
 density. SAXS data was collected on a charge coupled device detector with sample-detector
 distances of 1983.36 mm. The radiation wavelength was 1.2398 Å, and X-ray Energy was 10 keV. A
 scattering vector q-range of 0.009 – 0.7 Å-1 was targeted, corresponding to actual space distances
 between 700 - 9 nm to cover multi-scale nature of montmorillonite swelling. The SAXS data was
 analyzed using the Igor Pro 9 program.
 The SAXS samples were prepared in four steps: (1) carefully pack dry clay sample into the PEEK cells
 (inner diameter 3.00 mm, height 8.52 mm) for a consistent density (~1.3 or 1.6 g/cm-3), (2) purged
 thoroughly with research grade CO2 to remove trapped air, (3) introduced chemical solutions (100,
 50, 25, or 10 µL/hr) into the clay cells to reach an “equilibrium”, then sealed tightly (steps 1 - 3 were
 conducted in our geochemistry lab space), and (4) transported to ALS Beamline 7.3.3 for SAXS
 measurements.

 2.5 Results and Discussion
 2.5.1 Influences of Na+/K+ Ratios on Clay Swell-Shrink
 Figure 2-2 shows the time- and chemically-resolved swelling pressure curves as a function of Na+/K+
       ratios under a constant IS = 1.0 M, i.e., Na+/K+ = 1:0 (1.0 M NaCl), 3:1 (0.75 M NaCl + 0.25 M KCl),
       1:1 (0.5 M NaCl + 0.5 M KCl), 1:3 (0.25 M NaCl + 0.75 M KCl), 0:1 (1.0 M KCl) and finally 1.0 M
 NaCl. The swelling pressure decreases with decreasing Na+/K+ ratio, indicating that the presence of
 K+ leads to reduced clay swelling compared to Na+. This result is consistent with the observation by
 Wakim et al. (2009) and Abdullah et al. (1999). It appears that a steady state of swelling pressure
 (equilibrium) was not achieved within 3 - 6 days of hydration time, suggesting that the K+/Na+
 interlayer exchange reaction is a slow process.
 The final step was to evaluate the reversibility of the shrinkage (collapse) caused by K+, by re-
 injecting 1.0 M NaCl at day 20.7 (Figure 2-2). The pressure immediately increased and then
 continuously increased up to 1.90 MPa on day 40 until it stabilized on day 50. The pressure at the
 final step, 1.90 MPa is lower than the pressure at the initial step of 2.12 MPa at day 4.7 under the
 same chemical condition of 1.0 M NaCl. This suggests that the swelling and shrinkage of the clay
 material in the cell are not entirely reversible. We also observed that the pore space was completely
 clogged at ~ day 40 because there was no more effluent from the clay cell outlet, and the injection
 pressure exceeded the maximum limit of the syringes i.e., the injecting fluid leaked from gas-tight

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   8

 Hamilton syringes (max pressure 0.7 MPa for 25-mL syringe, 1.0 MPa for 10- and 5-mL syringes).
 Table 2-2 summarizes the final swelling pressure, flow duration time, and injection pressure.

 2.5.2 Influence of Ionic Strength on Clay Swelling Pressure
 In our EBS FY2023 progress report, we reported the time- and chemically resolved swelling pressure
 curves (Figure 2-3) with a clay density of 1.3 g/cm3 as a function of aqueous IS (i.e., 5.0 M, 1.0 M,
 0.1 M, 0.001 M NaCl, and 1.0 M KCl). It shows that swelling pressure increases with the decrease of
 IS (5 M – 0.001 M NaCl) and changes with cation type (Na+ vs. K+). The observation agrees with
 those of Castellanos et al. (2008) and Wakim et al. (2009). There is a significant increase of swelling
 pressure at 0.1 M and 0.001 M NaCl vs. 5 M and 1 M NaCl, suggesting crystalline and osmotic
 swelling formation. However, those measurements were conducted with relatively short flow duration
 (hydration) (3 – 4 days at each chemical concentration)

 Figure 2-3. Clay swelling pressure as a function of IS (5M, 1.0 M, 0.1 M, 0.001 M NaCl, and 1.0 M
 KCl) at a clay density of 1.3 g/cm3.

 Rao et al. (2013) suggested that the clay osmotic swelling occurs at NaCl concentration ≤ 0.25 M.
 New swelling measurements were conducted with additional injection solutions to evaluate the
 potential concentration for the osmotic swelling occurring (Figure 2-4). The data shows that: (a) when
 5.0 M NaCl was injected, the swelling pressure increased rapidly within the first 5 hours, reached a
 maximum (1.36 MPa), then decreased slightly during day 1, and finally reached a steady state of
 pressure 1.27 MPa at day 2 – 3; (b) after switching to 1.0 M NaCl, only a minor pressure increase
 was observed with a pressure = 1.51 MPa at day 5 – 6; (c) when the injection solution was switched
 to 0.5 M NaCl, the pressure rose rapidly in the first few hours and then stabilized to 1.78 MPa at day
 8 – 9; (d) when switched to 0.25 M NaCl, the pressure increased continuously to 2.59 MPa and
 stabilized at day 13 – 14; (e) the pressure increased sharply once switched to 0.1 M NaCl, then
 decreased slightly and reached a steady state of 3.21 MPa at days 17 – 19; (f) again, the pressure
 rose sharply up to 4.42 MPa when switched over to 0.01 M NaCl, then decreased slightly and
 reached a pressure of 4.31 MPa between days 22 – 23; and (g) finally, when switched to 0.001 M
 NaCl, it achieved a final pressure of 4.50 MPa.

 The final swelling pressures with each chemical condition are summarized in Table 2-3. The final
 pressure as a function of NaCl concentration can be best fitted by an exponential model, as shown in

 occurrence of osmotic swelling at NaCl ≤ 0.25 M, as predicted by Rao et al. (2013).

 Figure 2-5. The final swelling pressure vs. NaCl concentrations in Table 2-3 can be best fitted by an
 exponential model.

 As noted in the Figure 2-4 experiments, the pump and syringe injection became stuck and unable to
 deliver fluid through the clay cell even at a very low flow rate of 5 mL/hr. Thus, no more effluent was
 collected after day 20, as shown in Figure 2-4. This indicates that the interparticle porosity became
 relatively small or was closed completely due to strong osmotic swelling (Holmboe et al., 2012) after
 a long hydration time (~20 days, particularly at low NaCl concentrations (0.01 M and 0.001 M NaCl).
 The results suggest that salt concentration and hydration time are important factors controlling the
 montmorillonite swelling.

 2.5.3 Influence of Hydration Time on Clay Swelling Pressure
 To evaluate the influence of hydration time, new swelling measurements were conducted with longer
 flow-through time, as shown in Figure 2-6. This figure shows that when 1.0 M NaCl was injected, the
 swelling pressure increased rapidly within the first day and reached a steady state of 1.38 MPa at
 approximately day 2 – 3, which is consistent with Figure 2-4 (1.33 MPa) under the same 1.0 M NaCl
 condition. After switching to 0.1 M NaCl, the pressure rose rapidly between days 3 – 6 and then
 continuously increased between days 6 – 12, reaching a steady state of 4.01 MPa at days 12 – 14
 with several quasi-steady states of swelling pressure approximately at day 6, 7, 9, and 11. The
 observed swelling pressure of 4.01 MPa is much higher than those observed in Figure 2-4
 (3.21 MPa) and 2.51 MPa in Figure 2-3 under the same 0.1 M NaCl concentration. The only
 difference is the hydration time, where the hydration time with 0.1 M NaCl is 12 days in Figure 2-6,
 5 days in Figure 2-4, and 3 days in Figure 2-3. The data shows the swelling pressure increases with
 increasing hydration time at the same concentration of 0.1 M NaCl (Table 2-4). The final swelling
 pressure measured with 3 - 5 days hydration in Figure 2-3 and Figure 2-4 is a quasi-steady state of
 swelling pressure. This observation indicates that the hydration time is important in controlling clay
 swelling processes.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 12

 Figure 2-6. Clay swelling pressure as a function of NaCl concentrations and hydration time at clay
 density of 1.3 g/cm3.

 In Figure 2-6, a 0.01 M NaCl solution was injected following saturation with a 0.1 M NaCl solution.
 The pressure increased to 4.23 MPa within ~ 5 hours, and the clay cell became clogged after day
 15. The final swelling pressure and flow duration time under each chemical condition are given in
 Table 2-5. The results further demonstrate that montmorillonite swelling with a long hydration time
 at low IS conditions will significantly reduce the permeability (Holmboe et al., 2012) and even result
 in interparticle porosity closure and thus limit ion diffusion and transport.

 2.5.4 Influence of Clay Density of Clay Swelling Pressure
 Figure 2-7 shows the time-resolved swelling pressure curve at a 1.6 g/cm3 clay density measured by
 introducing 0.001 M NaCl solution. The pressure increased rapidly within the first 10 hours, then
 decreased slightly until reaching a quasi-steady state of approximately 5.0 MPa within 2 days. Then,
 the swelling pressure rose again to get a steady state of 6.74 MPa over 3 days, although this is
 attributed to the effect of clogging. These four stages, or stepwise curves, are typical characteristics
 of clay swelling pressure at relatively high clay density, which is similar to those described in the
 literature (Chaaya et al., 2023; Imbert & Villar, 2006; Komine & Ogata, 2003; Massat et al., 2016;
       Wang et al., 2021).

                                        0.001 M NaCl
 clogged

 Figure 2-7. Clay swelling pressure as a function of hydration time at clay density of 1.6 g/cm3.

 The interparticle pore space appears to have closed completely after 3.2 days. It was observed that
 either the pump was stuck, or the injection syringe was leaking due to the injection pressure building
 and exceeding the limit of the pump or syringe even though the flow rate was reduced from an initial
 50 µL/hr to 5 µL/hr. The injection pressure increased to 3 MPa (above the red dashed line in Figure
 2-7) and injecting any fluid through the clay cell was still impossible. The final swelling pressure
 stabilized at 6.74 MPa without injection pressure. It may not reflect the actual “equilibrium” swelling
 pressure because no effluent was collected from the cell outlet. However, this pressure is
 comparable to 6.5 MPa measured by Chaaya et al. (2023) with a Kunipia-G clay d = 1.5 g/cm3 at
 0.0001 NaCl. Takahashi et al. (2015) used the same Kunipia-F with a d = 1.64 g/cm3 and obtained
 a final swelling pressure of 5.53 MPa by saturation with deionized water.
 Table 2-6 compares the final swelling pressure at clay density of 1.6 g/cm3 and 1.3 g/cm3. As
 expected, higher clay density results in higher swelling pressure at the same 0.001 M NaCl solution.

 2.5.5 Influence of Ionic Strength (NaCl) on Clay Microstructure
 Figure 2-8 shows the SAXS profile as a function of IS, including the montmorillonite cell samples
 saturated for ~ 7 days with the solutions of 0.001 M, 0.01 M, 0.1 M, 1.0 M, and 5.0 M NaCl. The
 diffraction peaks with maximum intensity values at q = ~ 0.40 Å-1 (d001= 15.7 Å) and q = ~ 0.34 Å-1
 (d001= 18.5Å) are typical of 2w and 3w layer crystalline hydrates, respectively. The broad peaks with
 maximum intermediate intensity values at q = ~ 0.156 Å-1 (d001= 40.3 Å) at 0.001 M NaCl, q = ~
       0.176 Å-1 (d001= 35.7 Å) at 0.01 M NaCl, and q = ~ 0.178 Å-1 (d001= 35.3 Å) at 0.1 M NaCl are typical
 characteristic of osmotic swelling spacing. The small peaks at q = ~ 0.63 Å-1 (d001= 10.0 Å), q = ~
       0.51 Å-1 (d001= 12.3 Å) and q = ~ 0.29 Å-1 (d001= 21.7 Å) reflect the presence of minor fractions of
 0w, 1w and 4w layer crystalline hydrates, respectively. A summary of the interlayer hydration state
 and osmotic swelling spacing is provided in Table 2-7.

 0w, 10.0Å

 1w, 12.3Å

                                                                      2w, 15.5 – 15.9Å

                                                                      3w, 18.0 – 18.8Å

 4w, 21.9Å

 Osmotic swelling
                                                                      35.3 – 40.3Å
 Platelets aggregated
 stacks, 210 Å

 Figure 2-8. SAXS profile as a function of NaCl concentration at clay density of 1.3 g/cm3.
 As expected, interlayer crystalline and osmotic swelling d001 increase with decreasing IS, which
 agrees with our chemically resolved swelling pressure measurement where the swelling pressure
 increases with decreasing IS in Figure 2-3 and Figure 2-4 (5.0 M to 0.001 M NaCl). The clay
 interlayer basal spacing increases from 15.5 Å to 18.8 Å with decreasing IS in response to the
 interlayer hydration state changing from 2w hydrate dominated at 5.0 M NaCl, to a transient mixture
 of 2w and 3w hydrates at 1.0 M NaCl, and 3w hydrate dominated at 0.1 M, 0.01 M, and 0.001 M
 NaCl.
 The osmotic swelling peaks with an intermediate spacing value of d001 = 40.3 Å at 0.001 M NaCl,
 35.7 Å at 0.01 M NaCl and 35.3 Å at 0.1 M NaCl (Table 2-7) are exciting and unique observations for
 compacted montmorillonite using the SAXS technique. According to literature data reported to date,
 there is a gap between d001 = 19 Å (3w) and d001 = 40 Å (osmotic) for Na-montmorillonite (Meleshyn
 & Bunnenberg, 2005; Norrish, 1954; Wang et al., 2021). The gap has been called “forbidden” layer
 spacing (Meleshyn & Bunnenberg, 2005; Ravina & Low, 1977). It suggests that no clear evidence is

November 8, 2024                                                                                               15

 available to define montmorillonite swelling for d001 between 19 Å (3w) and 40 Å (osmotic) because
 the peaks on most XRD profiles corresponding to this range were rarely clearly observed (Anderson
 et al., 2010; Wang et al., 2021). Meleshyn & Bunnenberg (2005) studied the mechanism of the
 forbidden layer spacing of a Na-montmorillonite with the Monte Carlo approach. They concluded that
 special chainlike structures consisting of Na cations, water molecules, and oxygen of substituted
 tetrahedrons of neighboring mineral layers was formed in the interlayer space of Na-montmorillonite
 at a layer spacing of 19 Å, where XRD investigations show termination of crystalline swelling. This
 “forbidden” layer spacing between 19 Å and 40 Å is inconsistent with our observations because we
 clearly show the presence of 4w layer spacing = 21.7 Å (Figure 2-9) and the osmotic swelling spacing
       between 35.3 Å and 40.3 Å with 0.1 – 0.001 M NaCl (Figure 2-8), and 45.5 Å with 0.001 M KCl
       (Figure 2-9.
 We also observed that the peak positions of the same 2w and 3w hydrates shifted to a slightly higher
 basal spacing as NaCl concentration decreased (Figure 2-8 and Table 2-7). The 2w d001 spacing
 increases from 15.5 Å at 5.0 M NaCl to 15.9 Å at 1.0 M NaCl. The 3w d001 spacing increases from
 18.0 Å at 1.0 M NaCl to 18.8 Å at 0.001 M NaCl. Takahashi et al. (2015) used the same
 montmorillonite Kunipia-F sample and obtained a basal d001 spacing = 16.3 Å for 2w hydrate and
 19.5 Å for 3 hydrate using deionized water (see Table 2-7). These results suggest that the 2w and 3w
 d001 spacing and the osmotic swelling spacing also depend on water chemistry. This variation is
 consistent with literature-reported data in different investigations (Ferrage et al., 2005; Holmboe et
       al., 2012).
 We also observed a minor peak with an intermediate d001= 210 Å (q = ~ 0.03 Å-1), which could be
 attributed primarily to the aggregated stacksof clay platelets in compacted montmorillonite. The
 single montmorillonite molecule is of a lamellar shape (a platelet but is not necessarily flat) with a
 size of approximately100 nm width and 1 nm thickness, and several platelets are gathered in a
 group, forming a stacked layer as individual particles (Takahashi et al., 2015). The stacks resemble
 the tactoid formation in aqueous dispersions (Segad et al., 2012a, b).

 2.5.6 Influence of KCl on Clay Microstructure
 The SAXS profile as a function of KCl concentration (0.001 M, 0.01 M, 0.1 M, and 1.0 M KCl) is
 presented in Figure 2-9 with a relatively longer hydration time of approximately 14 days. It shows
 different interlayer hydration states and osmotic swelling from the NaCl solutions in Figure 2-8. The
 peak of 2w hydrate dominates at all three concentrations of 1.0 M, 0.1M, and 0.01 M KCl with d001 =
 15.2 - 15.6 Å (Figure 2-9), while the mixed 2w and 3w hydrates dominate at 1.0 M NaCl, 3w hydrate
 and the osmotic swelling dominates at 0.1 and 0.01 M NaCl (Figure 2-8). The peaks of the 0w, 1w,
 3w, and 4w hydrate and the osmotic swelling in Figure 2-9 are small or negligible at 1.0 M, 0.1M,
 and 0.01 M KCl solutions. These results agree with the swelling pressure measurement in Figure
 2-2, where swelling pressure promoted by 1.0 M NaCl was decreased when the K+/Na+ ratio was
 increased in response to the microstructure changes.
 A very interesting strong, broad osmotic swelling peak was observed with an intermediate spacing
 d001= 45.5 Å and a broad mixed peak of 4w, 3w, and 2w hydrates with d001= 21.7 Å, 18.1 Å and 15.9
 Å, respectively at 0.001 M NaCl (Figure 2-9). These values suggest that the swelling is stronger at
 0.001 M KCl than the swelling at the same concentration of 0.001 M NaCl (Figure 2-8). We expected
 the swelling to be weaker when K+ replaces Na+ (Figure 2-2). This observation suggests that the
 hydration time plays a significant role in the microstructural change, since the stronger swelling was
 observed after a period of ~ 14 days with 0.001 M KCl, while ~ 7 days with 0.001 M NaCl. The
 coexistence of different hydration states and osmotic swelling indicates that the d001 increases with
 increasing hydration and is a stepwise process. The average increase in basal spacing upon water
 uptake has recently been reported to be more of a continuous process due to swelling heterogeneity
       (Holmboe et al., 2012; Wang et al., 2021).

November 8, 2024                                                                                                16

 0w, 10.0Å

 1w, 12.3Å

                                                                      2w, 15.2 – 15.9Å

                                                                      3w, 18.0 – 18.1Å

                                                                      (35.7) – 45.5Å

 2.5.7 Influence of Clay Density and Cation Type of Clay Microstructure
 The SAXS spectra with clay density of 1.6 g/cm3 and 1.3 g/cm3 at constant salt concentrations of
 0.01 M NaCl and 0.01 M KCl are presented in Figure 2-10 for comparison. The swelling d001 spacing
 values are given in Table 2-7. Figure 2-10 shows that (1) at 0.01 M NaCl, 3w hydrate and the
 osmotic swelling dominates, but the swelling peaks are much more potent at clay density of
 1.3 g/cm3 than those at a density of 1.6 g/cm3, and (2) at 0.01 M KCl, 2w hydrate dominates at
 density of 1.3 g/cm3 with small co-existing peaks of 0w and 1w hydrates, while all co-existing peaks
 of 0w, 1w, and 2w hydrates are small at clay density of 1.6 g/cm3. These results indicate that the
 higher clay density leads to a weaker microstructural change. This observation could be explained by
 the fact that the interparticle porosity was smaller for higher clay density samples, limiting the
 formation of the stronger interlayer hydrates and osmotic swelling.
 For the same clay density of 1.3 g/cm3 or 1.6 g/cm3, 0.01 M Na+ saturated clay shows significant
 peaks of 3w hydrate and osmotic swelling, while 0.01 M K+ saturated clay only has weak peaks of
 2w, 1w and 0w hydrates. These results also support the observations in Figure 2-2 where the
 swelling pressure decreased with decreasing Na+/K+ ratios because K+ tends to form 2w or less
 hydrates, and Na+ tends to form both 3w hydrate and osmotic swelling.

November 8, 2024                                                                                              17

 Figure 2-10. Influence of clay density and cation type on SAXS profile.

 Table 2-7. Summary of interlayer crystalline basal and osmotic swelling spacing (d001) of
 montmorillonite (Kunipia-F) as a function of ionic strength, cation types, and clay
 density in this work by using SAXS (d001= 2π/q) and literature data.

November 8, 2024                                                                                          18

 2.6 Summary and Future Work
 Bentonite has been proposed as a buffer material in the EBS for geological repositories of high-level
 radioactive waste. Montmorillonite is the primary mineral of bentonite. Its ability to swell upon being
 hydrated by groundwater from the host rock is essential for bentonite’s safety function in the
 repositories. Swelling is a dynamic process affected by geochemical and hydraulic changes that can
 lead to microstructural changes. In FY24, a series of laboratory experiments were conducted to
 determine the influence of water chemistry and hydration time on clay swelling and microstructural
 changes. We concluded that aqueous chemistry, including IS, cation type (Na+ vs. K+), and hydration
 time, as well as the clay density, can strongly impact montmorillonite swell-shrink behavior via
 changing the interlayer hydration states and osmotic swelling spacing.
 In summary, a μ-oedometer system was equipped with X-ray transparent cells to hold compacted
 montmorillonite. This system allowed us to determine the chemically- and time-resolved swelling
 pressure and microstructural changes using the SAXS technique. Our SAXS measurement profiles
 show the presence of mixed interlayer hydration states (0w – 4w) and osmotic swelling. Generally,
 the interlayer crystalline basal and osmotic swelling spacing (d001) increases with decreasing water IS
 and varies with cation type (Na+ vs. K+), hydration time, and clay density. Specifically, the hydration
 state is dominated by 3w in NaCl solutions vs. 2w which dominates in KCl solutions. This indicates
 that K+ leads to lower swelling pressures. Interestingly, the osmotic swelling peak was observed with
 an intermediate spacing d001 = 40.3 Å at 0.001 M NaCl, which decreases to 35.7 Å at 0.01 M NaCl
 and 34.3 Å at 0.1 M NaCl. We also observed a mixture of 4w, 3w, and 2w layer hydrates and a strong
 osmotic peak (45.5 Å) at 0.001 M KCl, which leads to a d001 spacing larger than that observed at
 0.001 M NaCl, suggesting the hydration time is an important factor given the difference in hydration
 times (14 days for 0.001 M KCl clay cell vs. 7 days for the 0.001 M NaCl). Finally, the hydrate and
 osmotic swelling peaks are stronger at lower clay density. This is the first work to experimentally
 determine the clay osmotic swelling spacing for compacted montmorillonite using unique SAXS
 measurements.
 These SAXS results agree with our chemically- and time-resolved swelling pressure measurements,
 where the swelling pressure continuously increases with decreasing NaCl concentration and
 decreases with increasing K+/Na+ ratio. In addition, the chemical effect on clay microstructure and
 swelling, is dynamic and highly depends on chemical composition, hydration time, and clay density.
 Longer hydration times and larger clay densities appear to reduce or close the interparticle pore
 space significantly and greatly affects ion diffusion and transport through the compacted
 montmorillonite, particularly at low chemical IS conditions. These observations provide an improved
 understanding of the dynamics of clay swelling under variable chemical, hydration time, and clay
 density conditions. The results are highly relevant to understanding and modeling hydro-mechanical-
 chemical coupling processes in EBS for geological repositories of high-level radioactive waste.
 In the remaining months of FY24 and FY25, experiments will continue focusing on:
 1. Quantification of microstructural changes of compacted montmorillonite as a function of
 hydration time, using a series of SAXS experiments, and
 2. Evaluation of swelling pressure as a function of montmorillonite content, using bentonite
 samples containing variable content of pure montmorillonite.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 19

 This page intentionally left blank.

November 8, 2024                                                           20

 3 MODELING OF RADIONUCLIDE RETENTION BY MINERAL SURFACES
 Assessing the safety of the geological/subsurface spent nuclear waste disposal requires the
 knowledge of how radionuclide migration and retention in the subsurface may occur over the
 repository's lifetime. We need to be able to predict the fate of the radionuclides released from the
 multibarrier storage site in case of a catastrophic leakage. The radionuclides’ mobility in the
 subsurface depends on the oxidation state, aqueous speciation, complexation, and affinity towards
 the mineral surfaces exposed to the leaking waste. Therefore, knowledge of these radionuclides'
 aqueous speciation and chemistry under relevant conditions, interactions with the surrounding
 mineral matrix (e.g., bentonite), and mobility within this matrix is critically vital for evaluating
 repository performance. Here, we focused on uranium because it is the most abundant radionuclide
 in non-reprocessed spent nuclear fuel and highly mobile if present in the oxidized form.
 Unfortunately, our understanding of aqueous and interfacial uranium speciation is still limited
 because thermodynamic data is lacking for the many uranium complexes, especially in the less
 stable oxidation states (e.g., U(III), U(V)). In this report, it is shown how thermodynamical and
 molecular modeling, as well as the usage of the most recent Artificial Intelligence/Machine Learning
 (AI/ML) algorithms, can improve understanding of uranium interactions with mineral surfaces.
 Specifically, we show: (i) how AI/ML-based surrogates of the thermodynamic bulk/interfacial
 speciation model can provide an ultrafast predictive tool for the uranium sorption to oxides,
 overcoming the convergence problems encountered by existing numerical solvers (Li et al., 2024);
 (ii) how to develop more complete speciation representation of uranium solution in the presence of
 carbonate ligands at varying pH and redox conditions; (iii) how to model uranium sorption to
 carbonate minerals and how porosity of carbonate sediments and sedimentary rocks affect uranium
 retention (Zarzycki, 2024); and finally (iv) how molecular modeling can assist us with parametrizing
 macroscopic speciation models based on the thermodynamics of aqueous speciation and Surface
 Complexation Models (SCM) of electric double a layer formed at the mineral/electrolyte interface
       (Zarzycki, 2023).

 3.1 Molecular and Thermodynamic Modeling of Radionuclides Sorption to
 Mineral Surfaces
 To assess the safety of the spent nuclear waste disposal sites in case of leakage, one must quantify
 the strength and extent of radionuclides retention by mineral/soil matrix (Li et al., 2024). The
 macroscopic models of radionuclide retention by sorption to mineral surfaces are usually based on
 the thermodynamic ion sorption model that describes the ion sorption to the mineral surface as a
 complexation-type reaction and accounts for the effect of the electric field generated by the charged
 mineral surfaces ions surface concentration. These thermodynamics models, the SCM, have been
 successfully used to analyze and interpret various experimental data.
 However, the parametrization of these models remains challenging, as there is not enough data to
 comprehensively describe radionuclide speciation in the bulk solution and at the mineral interfaces
 in thermodynamic conditions relevant to the spent nuclear fuel/nuclear waste repository. In addition,
 the convoluted nature of the SCM’s equations does not guarantee the convergence of numerical
 solvers or the finding of a unique algebraic solution. In the previous milestone report, we described
 the construction of the SCM model for uranium sorption to oxides and the development of the AI
 surrogate based on the multioutput regressor consisting of an ensemble of random forests. Here, we
 revisit the task for the development of an AI-surrogate, but instead of ensemble learning, a Deep
 Neural Network (DNN) regression model was developed that maps the computational input to output
 without any knowledge about the physics and chemistry involved, still the intrinsic relationships
 between the model parameters, properties and environmental conditions – as governed by the
 underlying physicochemistry – are unraveled by the dense DNN architecture.

November 8, 2024                                                                                               21

 3.1.1 Generic Surface Complexation Model of Uranium Sorption to Oxides
 Oxide mineral surfaces exposed to an aqueous solution become terminated by the amphoteric
 hydroxyl groups, which can donate or accept a proton (Dzombak & Morel, 1990; Brown et al., 1999).
 Several protonation schemes can describe this charging process (Yates et al., 1974; Davis et al.,
 1978; Westall & Hohl, 1980; Bolt & Van Riemsdijk, 1979; Hiemstra et al., 1989; Hiemstra & Van
 Riemsdijk, 1991; 1996; Hiemstra et al., 1996). Here, the 2-pK model was used, which assumes two
 protonation reactions and is one of the most generic and most frequently used models for
 interpreting experimental data for a wide range of mineral surfaces (Sverjensky, 2005).
 The SCM is a mathematical construct that combines the equilibrium interfacial chemistry with the
 geometric model of spatial charge distribution at the interface. Here, we present the development of
 AI surrogates for one mathematical representation of the spatial charge distribution: the Triple Layer
 Model (TLM) combined with the 2-pK surface protonation/complexation model (Westall & Hohl,
 1980). Extending or applying to other Electric Double Layer (EDL) models is straightforward.
 To model the mineral surfaces, we used a generic oxide surface covered by reactive amphoteric
 surface sites (SOH). These sites can act as an acid or a base by either releasing a proton or binding
 an additional proton. The electrolyte ions and uranium ions accumulate near the surface as outer-
 sphere complexes in the layer adjacent to the surface, which we describe as surface complexation.
 The electric field generated by a charged mineral surface affects ion concentrations near the
 surface. For example, positive surface potential depletes cation concentration and increases anions'
 surface concentration. Generally, the electric field effect can be considered as attraction/repulsion
 energic weight (Boltzmann factor) on the bulk ions.

 Figure 3-1. AI-surrogates for the SCM of uranium sorption to oxides: (a) multioutput regressor
 consisting of 12 estimators (random forests), each trained to predict a single target
 value (one from 12 outputted values) based on an input vector containing model
 parameters/system description (12 input values), and (b) the DNN-surrogate consists
 of 5 hidden layers (each with 512 neurons), as well as input and output layers (both
 containing 12 neurons). The input consists of a description of the oxide surface
 (surface area, site density), the SCM parameter values, such as affinity constants
 (logKi) capacitances, and a description of solution composition (12 values). The output
 consists of charge densities, electrostatic potential in each layer, and concentrations of
 surface complexes (12 values). See Li et al. (2024) for more details.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  22

 Figure 3-2a shows examples of how DNN-surrogates can mimic the numerical solver-producing
 titration and sorption curves. Figure 3-2b shows that the DNN-surrogate can make predictions even
 if the numerical solver fails (i.e., it cannot converge to a unique solution). The latter proves that DNN
 can extrapolate beyond the training dataset and reasonably predict the physicochemical properties.

 Figure 3-2. Comparison of charge densities and potentials predicted by the numerical solver and
 estimated by the DNN-surrogate – illustrated for the case when (a) the numerical
 solver converges for all pH values and (b) when the numerical solver fails to converge.
 Note that even if the numerical solver cannot find a unique solution, the DNN-surrogate
 can still provide the pH-dependent titration data. Figures taken from Li et al. (2024).

 Figure 3-3 shows the parity plot between the DNN prediction (subscript, pred) and the ground truth
 (subscript, GT) for charge densities, electrostatic potentials, and surface complex concentrations.
 The ability of DNN is quantified to reproduce ground truth by reporting R2 score and the mean square
 error (MSE). R² is referred to as the coefficient of determination, and it is a statistical measure of
 how well the regression model fits the data, in other words, the goodness of fit – here, the goodness
 of the representation and value inference. The MSE measures the quality of an estimator – the
 average of the squares of errors. The larger the R2 value and the smaller the MSE value, the better
 the performance of the regressor.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   23

 The DNN-surrogate achieves R2 scores higher than 0.9999, and the maximum absolute error of
 normalized data between ground truth and DNN is less than 0.01; MSE depends on the target. Still,
 the DNN-surrogate is about 63% more accurate than the random forest multioutput regressor (Li et
       al., 2024).

 Figure 3-3. Performance of DNN-surrogate for uranium sorption on oxides. Parity plot for DNN-
 surrogate model prediction vs. numerically calculated ground truth. The score
 represents the goodness of fit of surrogate predictions to corresponding ground truth
 values. MSE represents the mean squared error between prediction and target. The
                     absolute error (|GT - Pred|) between DNN-surrogate prediction and the corresponding
 ground truth value is shown as the color bar. Figure taken from Li et al. (2024).

November 8, 2024                                                                                             24

 Figure 3-4. Analysis of input feature attribution in predicting target values for DNN-surrogate.
 Figure taken from Li et al. (2024).

 To better understand how the DNN-surrogate learned to map an input to an output, a feature
 analysis was performed by measuring how each input feature impacts each output value. The
 integrated gradient algorithm, developed by Sundararajan et al. (2017), was implemented to
 calculate the feature attribution by assigning an importance score to each input feature and
 approximating the integral gradients of the model output concerning the inputs. Figure 3-4 shows the
 input feature importance analysis results concerning a given target property.
 The top five crucial input features for charge densities include the uranium initial concentration in
 the solution, anions binding constant, pH, protonation equilibrium constant, and the inner-layer
 capacitance. This is consistent with the SCM construction that defines charge densities based on the
 accumulation of protons and ions at the surface and in its vicinity.
 The surface area and surface site density give the extent of accessible binding sites for surface
 sorption. The feature importance analysis captures that surface area is critical for predicting the

November 8, 2024                                                                                               25

 extent of the surface complex concentrations and uranium uptake. Our recent report (Li et al., 2024)
 provides a more detailed discussion about the model construction, performance, and comparison
 between random forest and DNN.
 In conclusion, we showed that AI-based surrogates can be built to mimic the numerical solvers,
 provide rapid properties/sorption/retention estimation from the system description, and thus should
 be part of our toolkit to develop models of uranium mobility and retention in the subsurface.

 3.1.2 Complexity of Aqueous Uranium Speciation: Redox, Hydrolysis, and
 Complexation
 The aqueous and interfacial chemistry of uranium is very complex. It includes redox, acid-base,
 complexation, ligand exchange, polymerization, and precipitation/dissolution reactions (Figure 3-5).
 The uranium chemistry included in our initial work on AI surrogates (sections above) was simplified. It
 included only a few critical reactions in an aqueous solution and simple monodentate binding of U4+
 or UO22+ to the surface sites exposed by oxide. The uranium chemistry is reexamined to develop a
 more comprehensive and complete chemical speciation model that includes most of the available
 equilibrium constants (e.g., from NEA, 2020; Katz et al., 1986; and Morss et al., 2010) and our new
 estimation for the reactions of uranium in less common oxidation states, U(III) and U(V).
 In this report, the uranium aqueous speciation is modeled using equilibrium constants selected by
 the Nuclear Energy Agency (NEA, 2020). In addition, we also used recent equilibrium constants
 obtained for a single ionic strength, which cannot be easily extrapolated to the zero ionic strength
 limit and thus not yet recommended by NEA. The reactions, corresponding equilibrium constants
 (logK), and free energies (G) for uranium chemistry considered in this report are listed in Table 3-1
 through Table 3-3. The aqueous chemistry of uranium is dominated by reduction-oxidation,
 hydrolysis, and complexation (Figure 3-5).

 Figure 3-5. Uranium aqueous chemistry is dominated by reduction-oxidation, hydrolysis, and
 complexation. Currently, only a fraction of the uranium chemistry is understood on the
 thermodynamic level (equilibrium constants, stoichiometries), in part due to the
 challenges of determining stability constants for weak or transient complexes and
 simultaneously occurring reactions in the complex solution.

 The reduction potential values for the uranium oxidation-reduction reactions are taken from the NEA
       database (NEA, 2020) and Katz et al. (1986).

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 26

 At present, most of our knowledge about uranium speciation in aqueous solutions focuses on U(IV)
 and U(VI), the most stable oxidation states. However, U(III) and U(V) can also be formed in spent fuel
 repository conditions, especially at elevated temperatures and pressures. The uranium redox
 reactions and corresponding free energies and equilibrium constants are listed in Table 3-1 and
 schematically illustrated in Figure 3-6.

 Figure 3-6. Uranium reduction from U(VI) to U(III) and corresponding standard half-reaction
 reduction potentials. The values for the multielectron reduction reactions are
 calculated from one-electron reduction reactions or taken from the NEA database.
 These reduction potential values for one, two, and three electron reactions are
 thermodynamically consistent.

 Table 3-1. The reduction reactions for the uranium ions are considered in this study. The standard
 reduction potentials one one-electron reduction half-reactions are taken from the
 literature (Morss et al., 2010; NEA, 2020); the values for two and three-electron
 reductions are calculated (aredox potential from NEA, 2020, bredox potential from
                     Morss et al., 2010).

 Uranium ions in an aqueous solution undergo hydrolysis reactions leading to the formation of
 hydroxyl complexes (uranium ions with varying numbers of OH- ligands in their first coordination
 shell). The equilibrium constants for hydrolysis are available only for the most stable oxidation states
 U(IV) and U(VI) (NEA, 2020). Here, we estimated analogous hydrolysis reactions for U(III) and U(V),
 assuming the linear free energy relationships and close thermodynamic redox and ligand-exchange
 cycles. A more detailed discussion can be found elsewhere (Zarzycki et al., 2024). The hydrolysis
 constants are listed in Table 3-2 and Table 3-3. The polymeric hydrolysis reactions are also
 considered for U(VI) and listed in Table 3-4.
 The uranium ions can form complexes with environmentally abundant ligands, and typically, Lewis
 bases donate electrons to positively charged uranium ions. The equilibrium constants are known for
 several of these complexes, including those with carbonate and chloride (Table 3-5). We still need to
 learn more about the complexation of U(III), U(IV), and U(V) with environmental ligands to account for
 these reactions in the speciation model, and here we propose to use molecular modeling to provide
 estimates and then develop a more complete speciation description (see Future work section).

 temperature for U(III), U(IV), U(V), and U(VI) reacting with water. The U(IV) and U(VI)
 values were taken from the NEA (2020) report. The hydrolysis free energies and logK
 values for U(III) and U(V) are estimated in this study. No experimental data exists for
 the U(III) and U(V) aqueous speciation.

 temperature for U(III), U(IV), U(V), and U(VI) reacting with hydroxyl ions. The values for
 U(IV) and U(VI) were taken from the NEA (2020) report. The hydrolysis free energies
 and logK values for U(III) and U(V) are estimated in this study. No experimental data
 exists for the U(III) and U(V) aqueous speciation.
