 3.1.3 Uranium Speciation at the Calcium Carbonate/Electrolyte Interface
 In the diagenesis of sediments and sedimentary rocks, several physical and chemical processes
 cause porosity loss/compaction of the formation with burial (Croize et al., 2013). Bulk porosity is
 defined as the volume of the void spaces to the total volume – and it is one of the most critical
 parameters in reactive transport models of the radionuclide migration in the subsurface. Moreover,
 the chemical and physical properties of sediments, and their permeability, change with an increase
 in the overburden pressure (or burial depth). Here, we focus on the carbonate sediments because
 they are considered the host rock formation for spent nuclear fuel storage; they dominate gas and oil
 reserves and have compaction behavior completely different from shales and sandstones (Croize et
       al., 2013).

November 8, 2024                                                                                               29

 Figure 3-7. The compaction of sediments and sedimentary rocks increases with depth. The
 porosity of the formation decreases. Figure taken from Zarzycki et al. (2024).

 We used the SCM like our previous studies of the calcite/electrolyte interface (Prus et al., 2019;
 2021; Zarzycki, 2023) in combination with the charge-regulated overlapping double-layer models
 (Lyklema & Duval, 2005) to understand the role of the mineral surfaces' proximity in uranium
 retention by carbonates (Figure 3-7F). More details of the modeling uranium sorption to mineral
 surfaces based on the SCM of the interface and bulk uranium speciation can be found in the recent
 review by Sun and Li (2021).
 Figure 3-8 and Figure 3-9 show how the disjoining pressure caused by the overlapping double layers
 changes with surface separation (columns on the left-hand side) and how this translates to the
 additional uptake of the uranium at varying redox conditions (columns on the right-hand side).

November 8, 2024                                                                                            30

 Figure 3-8. An increase in the electrical double layer forces/pressure between charged calcite
 particles as a function of surface separation (column on the left-hand side) affects the
 uranium compaction at varying redox and pH conditions (column on the right-hand
 side). The negative pe, Eh indicates the reducing conditions. The pH was adjusted to
 satisfy the charge neutrality conditions (charge balance). We consider that all possible
 uranium aqueous species can adsorb to calcite surface. However, hydrolysis products
 dominate uranium speciation in each case.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                31

 Figure 3-9. An increase in the electrical double layer forces/pressure between charged calcite
 particles as a function of surface separation (left column) and effects on the uranium
 compaction at varying redox and pH conditions (positive pe and Eh indicate the
 oxidizing conditions). The pH was adjusted to satisfy the charge neutrality conditions
 (charge balance). We assume that all possible uranium aqueous species can adsorb to
 the calcite surface. However, hydrolysis products and U(VI) species dominate uranium
 speciation.

November 8, 2024                                                                                              32

 In principle, the EDL overlap increases the uranium retention by mineral surfaces. The uranium
 uptake changes at distances larger than those that affect EDL pressure, suggesting that charge
 regulation is sensitive to even small increases in the excess osmotic pressure in the midplane
 between mineral surfaces. However, Pe and pH simultaneously affect the speciation in bulk
 (e.g., precipitation of uranium solid phases) and at the interface. Consequently, the uranium uptake
 by mineral surfaces cannot be easily predicted based on the magnitude of the disjoining pressure.
 Our calculations suggest that uranium aqueous chemistry is dominated by hydrolysis products.

 3.1.4 Molecular Modeling for Parametrizing Speciation Models

 3.1.4.1 Equilibrium Constants (logK)
 The available experimental data for uranium aqueous complexes does not always allow the
 determination of the complexation equilibrium constants (logK value) at zero ionic strength; for
 example, we need at least two logK values at varying ionic strength and the same temperature to
 extrapolate to zero ionic strength limit (NEA, 2020). Moreover, the linear interpolation model may not
 accurately represent the ions and their complexes in the diluted system, or the current ion-ion
 interactions models cannot accurately account for the varying activity effect at a given ionic strength.
 The thermodynamic characterization of the formation of weak uranyl complexes (e.g., UO2Cln(2-n))
 remains a challenge, even though these complexes are very important for accurately describing
 uranium speciation and sorption behavior (NEA, 2020).
 Luckily, the molecular modeling methods allow us to estimate the free energies of the chemical
 reactions, complexation, and ion-pair formation in the dilute solutions and at the interfaces
 (Beveridge & DiCapua, 1989; Roux, 1995; Zarzycki et al., 2015). The equilibrium constants (logK)
 are defined by the free energy change of reaction, G (i.e., G = -RT logK, R is the gas constant, T is
 the temperature); thus, molecular modeling can assist and complement the parametrization of the
 macroscopic models that assume chemical equilibrium.
 The acid-base chemistry of the interfacial groups can also be computationally determined using free-
 energy molecular dynamics simulations. The most robust computational methodology to determine
 the acidity constant of mineral surface in contact with aqueous solution from ab initio molecular
 dynamics simulations was developed by Sprik and coworkers (Cheng & Sprik, 2010). Their approach
 is based on estimating the free energy of proton transfer (PT) from an acid to the solvent and was
 initially tested for acidity and redox of isolated molecules (Blumberger & Sprik, 2006; Sulpizi & Sprik,
       2008; Cheng et al., 2009; Sulpizi & Sprik, 2010).
 We propose to use molecular dynamics simulations to determine the free energy and logK values for
 the reactions that have been parametrized based on experimental data (e.g. NEA, 2020) and
 reactions for which the experimental determination is not yet feasible. By comparing the
 experimental and computed values of logK, we will find the appropriate mapping and the numerical
 corrections to the computed values that account for the simplification in the interaction models
 implemented in molecular modeling. Next, by knowing how to map the calculated to experimental
 values, we will estimate the equilibrium constants for reactions that, although necessary for the
 uranium speciation/retention, are too challenging to be determined experimentally.
 We strive to develop the most complete thermodynamic description of the chemical speciation of
 uranium at varying conditions (redox, temperature, pH, composition) that can be implemented in
 existing speciation and reactive transport codes. This study aims to develop a predictive model of
 uranium chemistry needed for the safety assessments of the spent nuclear fuel/nuclear waste
 geological disposal sites.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  33

 3.1.4.2 Dielectric Constants, Capacitances
 The aqueous and interfacial speciation models usually assume the bulk value of the water dielectric
 constant. However, the dielectric constant decreases with increasing ionic strength or approaching
 the mineral/electrolyte interface. Computational protocols were developed for calculating the
 dielectric constant and dielectric relaxation spectrum of the complex solutions and solvents at the
 interface (Zarzycki & Gilbert, 2020; Zarzycki, 2023). By combining the spatially averaged dielectric
 constant with the average distance of ions (layer of ions accumulation), we showed that the
 capacitances of the SCMs can be computationally predicted (Zarzycki, 2023). This is relevant
 because predicting capacitances is one of the most challenging problems because unrealistic and
 non-physical values are often proposed as the best-fit parameters.

 3.1.5 Future Work
 We aim to develop a thermodynamic model of uranium speciation, assisted by molecular modeling
 and AI/ML-based regression, to better predict uranium retention in the subsurface in case of
 catastrophic leakage from the multibarrier storage of spent nuclear fuel. This report illustrates our
 progress towards this goal. However, a more comprehensive uranium aqueous and interfacial
 chemistry model must be developed.
 A computational pipeline was developed to generate synthetic datasets, train AI/ML models, and use
 these trained models as the SCM surrogate for uranium sorption to oxides at varying environmental
 conditions. Next, we started developing more complex models of uranium bulk and interfacial
 chemistry, including hydrolysis, complexation, acid-base, and redox reactions. Many essential
 reactions are not yet parametrized or are difficult to determine experimentally.
 We propose to use molecular simulations to determine free energies and logK values for some of
 these reactions, as well as the acid-base properties of the mineral surfaces. In addition, we propose
 using molecular modeling to predict solvent/interfacial properties (dielectric constants,
 capacitances).

November 8, 2024                                                                                               34

 4 EXPERIMENTAL INVESTIGATIONS ON BENTONITE AND OTHER CLAY
 BASED MATERIALS
 4.1 Introduction
 For nuclear energy to be viable, a long-term nuclear waste disposal repository capable of isolating
 high-level waste (HLW) and low-level radioactive waste (LLW) over the time scales necessary for the
 decay of long-lived radioactive isotopes (>106 years) must be created. Most disposal options
 currently under investigation use clay media (i.e., bentonite or shale) as engineered barriers or as the
 host rock for geologic storage (Altmann, 2008; Altmann et al., 2012; Delay et al., 2007; Guyonnet et
 al., 2009; SKB, 2011; Tournassat et al., 2015). Clays are suitable barriers for HLW due to their low
 hydraulic conductivity, which restricts contaminant mobility to slow diffusion-based transport, and
 their high adsorption capacity for radionuclides slows transport even further. Montmorillonite
 (M+0.33(Al1.67Mg0.33)Si4O10(OH)2) is the dominant clay mineral found in bentonite. It has a 2:1 layer-
 type phyllosilicate structure, with a large specific surface area (~750 m2/g), cation exchange
 capacity (~1 molc/kg), and strongly-sorbing surface complexation sites on clay edge surfaces.
 In compacted clay, solute transport is controlled by diffusion, and adsorption of solutes to the clay
 can significantly retard transport. While uranium is the primary constituent of spent nuclear fuel,
 79Se is a major driver of the safety case for radioactive waste disposal due to its long half-life

 (3.3 x 105 yr) and presence as relatively mobile anionic species under a range of chemical conditions
 (e.g., HSe-, SeO32-, SeO42-). Se redox chemistry is complex, with oxidation states ranging from -II to +VI
 over environmentally relevant conditions. While Se(-II) and Se(0) are relatively immobile due to the
 formation of low solubility precipitates, Se(IV) and Se(VI) exist as the oxyanions selenite (SeO32-) and
 selenate (SeO42-) and are highly mobile in water due to their high solubility. Se adsorption to clay
 minerals is quite low compared to other essential radionuclides such as U. Kd values for selenite
 adsorption to smectite are in the range of 1-50 L/kg (Missana et al., 2009; Montavon et al., 2009)
 compared to values up to 104 for U(VI) (Tournassat et al., 2018). Selenite and selenate adsorption to
 the clay minerals kaolinite and Ca-montmorillonite were studied by Bar-Yosef and Meek (1987) over
 the pH range of 4-8. Both selenite and selenate adsorption decreased with increasing pH, with very
 low or negligible adsorption above pH 8, and selenate adsorption to kaolinite was lower than selenite
 adsorption (Bar-Yosef & Meek, 1987). Similar trends of lower selenate vs selenite adsorption and
 decreasing adsorption with increasing pH are observed on iron oxides and oxyhydroxides (Balistrieri
 & Chao, 1987; 1990). Montavon et al. (2009) observed higher Se(IV) adsorption to synthetic
 montmorillonite in the presence of Ca, and suggested that ternary surface complexes with Ca may
 explain this enhanced sorption effect.
 Selenite diffusion through bentonite has been investigated in several studies (Garcı́a-Gutiérrez et al.,
       2001; Idemitsu et al., 2016; Wang et al., 2016; Wu et al., 2017). Idemitsu et al. (2016) measured
 apparent diffusion coefficient (Da) values of 2.5 x 10-11 to 1.9 x 10-13 m2/s over a range of dry bulk
 densities (0.8-1.6 kg/L), ionic strengths (0.01-1.0 M NaCl), and temperatures (10-55°C) for purified
 bentonite consisting of 99% montmorillonite. Measured Da values for bulk bentonite (i.e., with lower
 smectite content) under similar conditions are 1-2 orders of magnitude higher (Sato et al., 1994; Wu
 et al., 2014). Under anaerobic conditions, Se(IV) can become reduced to Se(0) or Se(-II) (Charlet et
 al., 2007; 2012; Ma et al., 2019). Due to the lower adsorption of selenate compared to selenite,
 selenate diffusion may be even higher than observed for selenite, although we could find no studies
 on selenate diffusion through bentonite in the literature.
 In FY22 and FY23, we presented the results from 3H (tritiated water - HTO) and Se(VI) through-
 diffusion experiments using a well-characterized, purified montmorillonite source clay (SWy-2) (Zheng
 et al., 2022; 2023). Experiments were conducted with the application of three different electrolyte
 compositions (under a single ionic strength of 0.1 M) representing pure Na, pure Ca, and a Na-Ca
 mixture to probe the effects of electrolyte composition on clay microstructure, Se(VI) aqueous

November 8, 2024                                                                                                    35

 speciation, and ultimately diffusion. This report presents modeling results from the HTO and Se(VI)
 diffusion experiments and summarizes the experimental results (Fox et al., 2024a,b). In addition, we
 present results from a new set of diffusion experiments conducted with Se(IV) under the same
 conditions as the experiment with Se(VI), which were conducted over 7.5 months from FY23-FY24.
 Se(IV) batch adsorption experiments on montmorillonite over a range of relevant chemical conditions
 were conducted to aid with the interpretation and modeling of the diffusion results, and these data
 are also presented.

 4.2 Selenate Diffusion Experiment: Model Results and Summary
 Details of the HTO and Se(VI) diffusion experiment were presented in previous reports (Zheng et al.,
 2022; 2023). A manuscript describing this work was submitted and is in press (Fox et al., 2024b)
 and the raw data have been published (Fox et al., 2024a). Here, we present the modeling results of
 the diffusion experiment, which were completed in FY24.

       4.2.1     Methods
 Through-diffusion experiments were conducted at pH 6.5 under three different electrolyte
 compositions consisting of 0.1 M NaCl, 0.085 M NaCl + 0.005 M CaCl2, and 0.033 M CaCl2,
 representing pure Na Na-Ca mixture, and pure Ca, respectively. Experiments were conducted with
 purified montmorillonite source clay (SWy-2), which had been pre-equilibrated with the three
 electrolytes. Tracer diffusion was measured over time as described previously (Zheng et al., 2023).
 The diffusion experiments were modeled using the reactive transport model CrunchClay (Steefel &
 Tournassat, 2021; Tournassat & Steefel, 2021; Tournassat et al., 2020). The consideration of an O-
 ring in the filters necessitated carrying out the calculations with a 2D-axisymmetric geometry
 (Tournassat et al., 2023). CrunchClay was used in combination with the CrunchEase interface
 (Tournassat et al., 2023) to estimate clay diffusive properties within the framework of a classical ε –
 De – Kd modeling approach, where ε is the diffusion porosity (dimensionless), De is the effective
 diffusion coefficient (m2 s-1) and Kd (L kg-1) is the distribution coefficient that accounts for reversible
 and linear adsorption processes. We assumed that HTO was present only in solution and that Se was
 present in solution (with c = aqueous concentration) and on surfaces (with Csurf in mol kgsolid-1) so that
 Ctot (in mol mmedium-3) can be expressed as:
 𝐶𝑡𝑜𝑡 = 𝑐𝜀 + 𝐶𝑠𝑢𝑟𝑓 𝜌𝑑 Eqn. (4-1)

 where d is the bulk density. We assumed that the Se surface concentration Csurf was linearly related
 to the aqueous concentration through 𝐶𝑠𝑢𝑟𝑓 = 𝑐𝐾𝐷 (with Kd in m3water kgsolid-1) and that the adsorption
 process was instantaneous and reversible. Based on this assumption,
 𝐶𝑡𝑜𝑡 = 𝑐𝜀 + 𝑐𝐾𝐷 𝜌𝑑 . Eqn. (4-2)

 It was further assumed that the media were homogeneous. Hence 𝐷𝑒 , 𝜀, 𝜌𝑑 and 𝐾𝐷 are independent
 of 𝑥, and Fick’s second law of diffusion reduces to:

       4.2.2         Results
 Table 4-1 summarizes the measured diffusion cell properties along with modeled  and 
 parameters for HTO diffusion. While there were some minor differences in cell packing (i.e., d),
 these differences alone were insufficient to explain the differences in HTO diffusion parameters (De
 and ). A reasonable explanation of the observed difference lies in the difference in microstructure of
 Na- and Ca-exchanged montmorillonite samples: the presence of exchangeable Ca promotes the
 formation of thicker layer stacks compared to Na, which in turn fosters the presence of larger
 interparticle pores, hence increasing the diffusivity of the material (Melkior et al., 2009; Tournassat
       et al., 2011; 2016).

 The normalized flux for Se(VI) reached a steady state after approximately 300 hrs, with the Ca-
 montmorillonite showing the highest normalized flux (Figure 4-1). The steady-state flux of Se was
 much lower than that of HTO in all three cells. The observed variations in Se concentration/flux may
 result from differences in effective porosity for Se and from changes in Se adsorption in the
 presence of increasing Ca concentrations.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                    37

 Figure 4-1. Left: Se normalized concentrations in the low-concentration reservoir as a function of

 Error bars are representative of the analytical error on the Se measurements. To ease
 a direct comparison, the y-axis scale is the same for all conditions.

 Through-diffusion data makes it possible to extract a single set of α, De values for each tracer in one
 experiment (Crank, 1975; Van Loon et al., 2003). However, it does not provide enough constraints to
 extract a single set of ε, Kd, and τ values and, thus, additional data and/or hypotheses are necessary
 to derive these three quantities independently. In the first attempt to model Se(VI) diffusion, the Kd
 values for Se(VI) were set to zero. In this condition, the fitted porosity and tortuosity values take their
 maximum and minimum value, respectively (εmax = α and τmin; Table 4-1). The corresponding fitted
 parameters enabled almost perfect reproduction of the diffusion data (Figure 4-1).
 In Na-montmorillonite, clay layers are negatively charged, and part or all the porosity is influenced by
 the presence of an EDL that forms at the surface and in which the solution is not electroneutral
 (Tournassat & Steefel, 2019). Negatively charged species, including SeO42-, are excluded from the
 EDL-influenced portion of the porosity because of their interaction with the negative electrostatic
 potential field created by montmorillonite surfaces. This anion exclusion is also responsible for a
 higher geometrical factor, G (or 1/), for anions compared to neutral tracers such as HTO, because

November 8, 2024                                                                                                     38

 pore throats, which are responsible for the pore network connectivity, may partially or totally exclude
 anions, thus ascribing longer diffusion pathways to anions compared to neutral species.
 Consequently, τSe  τHTO and it is possible to define a minimum accessible porosity value for Se
 corresponding to De,Se = τHTO × εSe,min × D0,Se. This minimum porosity corresponds to a maximum Kd
 value according to αSe = εSe,min + ρd Kd,max (Table 4-2). The very low Kd,max values (<<1) in Table 4-2 are
 nearly impossible to measure in batch experiments.
 The calculated minimum value of SeO42- and accessible porosity (εSe,min = 0.021) was like the values
 reported for Cl- in similar compacted Na-montmorillonite materials (Tournassat & Appelo, 2011). Cl-
 does not adsorb on clay mineral surfaces, and thus, the difference between total porosity values and
 Cl- accessible porosity values reported in the literature are representative of the excluded volume for
 monovalent anions. However, one would expect an increased exclusion for SeO42- compared to Cl-
 because of their difference in charge. This surprisingly high Se(VI) accessible porosity in the Na-
 montmorillonite sample may be partly explained by the presence of aqueous and exchanged Mg2+,
 which originates from a limited dissolution of the clay layers (Orucoglu et al., 2018). The formation of
 neutral MgSeO4 ion pairs, which are not excluded from the EDL, would explain the lower value of
 Se(VI) exclusion than expected. The lowering of Se exclusion from the EDL could also be related to
 an increased ion pairing in the EDL compared to bulk water conditions (Appelo et al., 2010), which in
 turn would increase the apparent accessible porosity of Se.

 Table 4-2. Summary of constraints on Se diffusion parameters in Na-, Na-Ca-, and Ca-
 montmorillonite. Kd,max values are given in L kg-1.
 εSe,min considering Kd,max considering

                        Na        0.021     0.057               -                       -
                       Na-Ca      0.032     0.084            0.036                   0.081
                         Ca       0.086      0.29             0.18                    0.22

 In the Ca-montmorillonite sample, the minimum accessible porosity was 0.086. This higher
 accessible porosity for Ca-montmorillonite compared to the Na-montmorillonite sample can be
 explained by a combination of the following effects: the presence of wider interparticular pores,
 which increases the average anion accessible porosity (Tournassat et al., 2016) and a decrease of
 the magnitude of the negative electrostatic potential field in the presence of divalent cations (Ca2+).
 However, the presence of CaSeO4 aqueous complexes, which are neutral and thus not repelled from
 clay mineral surfaces, will also affect the minimum accessible porosity, which is not accounted for in
 the above estimate (εSe,min of 0.086 for Ca). Aqueous speciation calculation carried out with the
 Thermochimie database (Giffaut et al., 2014) indicates that 32% of the total Se concentration is
 present as CaSeO4 in a 0.033 M CaCl2 background solution. Equivalently, at least 32% of the total
 porosity is accessible to Se in this neutral form (i.e. a revised minimum accessible porosity of
 0.32 × 0.56 = 0.18), giving in turn a revised Kd,max value of 0.22 L kg-1 (Table 4-2). Assuming that the
 D0 value for CaSeO4 is like that of CaSO4 (D0 = 4.71 10-10 m2 s-1), a τSe,max value of 0.115 can be
 deduced.
 In the Na-Ca cell, with 0.005 M CaCl2 + 0.085 M NaCl background solution, 6.6% of the total
 aqueous Se(VI) is in the form of CaSeO4, leading to a minimum accessible porosity of εSe,min =
 0.066 × 0.55 = 0.036. The corresponding τSe,max value is 0.123, like the value found for the Ca-
 montmorillonite sample.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                     39

 The above analysis highlights the importance of a good knowledge of aqueous speciation in
 understanding and predicting the diffusion properties of Se and provides realistic constraints for the
 ranges of Se, KdSe, and Se.

 4.2.3 Selenate Diffusion Summary
 An increase in Ca/Na ratio in solution and on clay surfaces is responsible for increased HTO and
 selenate diffusivity in montmorillonite samples. The rise of HTO diffusivity can be explained by a
 change of microstructure leading to an increase in the τHTO value. These changes will affect the
 transport of all radionuclides through clay barriers, including Se. In addition, the aqueous speciation
 of Se(VI) has a significant effect on Se transport. Se(VI) diffusion is limited by anion exclusion due to
 the formation of the negatively charged species SeO42-. However, the presence of Ca2+, forming
 CaSeO4 neutral ion pairs, which can access a larger portion of the total porosity, increases selenate
 diffusivity. While additional insights about the exact distribution of the interlayer and interparticle
 porosities and the influence of the diffuse layer on selenate ion pair formation would be necessary to
 build a comprehensive mechanistic model for selenate diffusion in montmorillonite samples, we
 provide constraints on model parameters that can be used to predict a range of Se(VI) transport in
 EBSs. Our analysis highlights that Se(VI) diffusion parameters (e.g., effective diffusion coefficients)
 obtained with simplified laboratory systems cannot be transferred to the simulation of a repository if
 in-situ geochemical conditions, especially ionic strength and major element concentrations (e.g., Ca),
 are different from the laboratory conditions. This critical message remains true unless Se(VI)
 aqueous speciation and the presence of an EDL are explicitly considered in the model. The results
 also emphasize the need to understand the overall chemical evolution of repository conditions as
 changes in aqueous speciation due to many factors (e.g., mineral dissolution/precipitation or
 groundwater penetration) can change the mobility of essential species.

 4.3 Selenite (Se(IV)) Experiments: Materials and Methods
 4.3.1 Clay Samples
 Batch Se sorption and diffusion experiments were conducted with a well-characterized
 montmorillonite source clay (SWy-2) obtained from the Clay Minerals Society. The montmorillonite
 was purified to remove minor impurities (quartz, feldspars, and calcite). The purification procedure
 was adapted from (Tinnacher et al., 2016). The procedure included the following steps: (1) dialysis
 against sodium acetate at pH 5 for carbonate mineral removal, (2) dialysis against NaCl to remove
 acetate and complete Na-saturation, (3) dialysis against water to remove excess salts, and
 (4) centrifugation to remove particles greater than 2 m as described previously (Zheng et al.,
 2022).
 To ensure that aqueous porewater chemical concentrations were constant during the diffusion
 experiments, purified montmorillonite was pre-equilibrated with the appropriate background
 electrolyte before packing in to diffusion cells, 2 g of purified clay was suspended in 100 mL of
 electrolyte solution, and the clay suspension was transferred to pre-rinsed dialysis tubing
 (SpectrPor7, 8 kDa). Clay samples were dialyzed against 1 L of background electrolyte for 1.5 weeks,
 changing dialysis solution at least three times and manually adjusting pH daily. Once the pH was
 stable, the background electrolyte solution was replaced with MilliQ water, and dialysis was
 continued for five days, changing the MilliQ water daily. After dialysis, the clay suspension was
 transferred to 40 mL polycarbonate centrifuge tubes and centrifuged at 39,000 x g for 20 minutes.
 The supernatant was removed, and the clay was dried at 60°C, ground in a ball mill with tungsten
 carbide balls, and stored at room temperature before packing the cells. When packing, a subsample
 of each clay was dried at 150°C to determine the moisture content.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   40

 4.3.2 Se(IV) Sorption
 Batch Se(IV) sorption experiments were conducted with purified, pre-equilibrated montmorillonite at
 10 g/L. The clay was suspended in background electrolyte (Na: 0.1 M NaCl, Ca: 0.033 M CaCl2, or
 Na-Ca: 0.085 M NaCl + 0.005 M CaCl2), the pH was adjusted to the target pH (6.0-7.5) and allowed
 to equilibrate for five days. The clay suspension was then spiked with sodium selenite to achieve the
 target final concentration (0.01-1.0 mM), and the pH was adjusted again. After 48 hours, the final pH
 was measured, and the clay suspension was centrifuged, filtered through a 0.45 m polyvinylidene
 difluoride (PVDF) filter, and analyzed for total Se by ICP-MS (Agilent 8900 ICP-MS Triple Quad).

 4.3.3 Se(IV) Diffusion Experiments
 Diffusion experiments were conducted in the same manner as reported previously for Se(VI);
 however, Se(IV) was used instead (Zheng et al., 2022). Through-diffusion experiments are conducted
 by circulating a relatively high volume of solution containing the tracer of interest at one end of the
 cell (“high concentration reservoir”) and a low volume of tracer-free solution at the other end of the
 cell (“low concentration reservoir”), replacing the low concentration reservoir at regular time intervals
 with fresh solution. This sets up a quasi-steady state diffusion gradient, and diffusion of the tracer is
 measured by monitoring concentrations in the low-concentration reservoirs over time. Diffusion
 experiments were conducted with purified, pre-equilibrated montmorillonite at a dry bulk density (d)
 of approximately 1.3 kg/L using custom-built PEEK diffusion cells and PEEK filters with a PCTFE ring
 as described previously (Zheng et al., 2022). The clay plug had a length of 5 mm and a diameter of
 9.5 mm.
 Experiments were conducted under a single ionic strength (0.1 M) and three different electrolyte
 compositions: 0.1 M NaCl, 0.033 M CaCl2, and 0.085 M NaCl + 0.005 M CaCl2, representing pure
 Na, pure Ca, and a Na-Ca mixture, respectively. All experiments were performed at room temperature
 at a target pH of 6.5 in equilibrium with atmospheric conditions (~20% O2 and 400 ppm CO2). The
 pre-equilibrated dry clay samples were carefully weighed into PEEK diffusion cells and compacted
 using a custom PEEK packing rod. Three phases of the diffusion experiment were conducted:
 (1) saturation, (2) tritiated water (3H – HTO) diffusion, and (3) Se(IV) diffusion.
 The clay was saturated by circulating 200 mL of background electrolyte at both ends of the cell at
 approximately 1 mL/min using a peristaltic pump for 40-42 days. After the saturation period, the 3H
 through-diffusion phase was started by replacing the background electrolyte solutions with a high 3H
 reservoir containing background electrolyte spiked with 30 nCi/mL 3H (100 mL) at one end and a low
 3H reservoir containing only background electrolyte (15 mL) at the other end. The high-concentration

 reservoir was sampled at the beginning and the end of the 3H diffusion experiment and did not
 change significantly. The low 3H reservoir was changed at regular time intervals using an in-house
 built autosampler, and the 3H concentration was measured in the low reservoir samples by liquid
 scintillation counting (Perkin-Elmer Liquid Scintillation Analyzer Tri-Carb 2900TR) by mixing 4 mL of
 sample with 18 mL of Ultima Gold XR liquid scintillation cocktail. The 3H concentration in the low
 reservoir never exceeded 0.5% of the concentration in the high reservoir.
 After the 3H diffusion pulse, the high-concentration reservoir was replaced with a Se(IV)-spiked
 solution containing 1.0 mM sodium selenite in the background electrolyte with a total volume of
 200 mL, marking the start of the Se(IV) diffusion experiment. The low-concentration reservoirs
 containing only background electrolytes (15 mL) were changed at regular time intervals, and Se
 concentrations were measured by ICP-MS (Agilent 8900 ICP-MS Triple Quad). Selected samples were
 analyzed for Se(IV) and Se(VI) using HPLC-ICP-MS (Ge et al., 1996). Chromatographic separation was
 achieved with an Agilent 1260 Infinity II HPLC pump equipped with an autosampler. The analytical
 column was a Hamilton PRP-X100 anion exchange column (10 m, 250 mm long, 4.1 mm inner
 diameter). PEEK tubing was used to connect the outlet end of the analytical column directly to the
 nebulizer to allow for in-line quantification of Se. The mobile phase consisted of 5 mM ammonium

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   41

 citrate buffered at pH 5.2 with 2% methanol. The mobile phase was delivered isocratically at a
 1 mL/min flow rate. The sample injection volume was 100 L. An Agilent 8900 ICP-MS Triple Quad
 was used to detect 78Se. Hydrogen was used in the collision/reaction cell to remove interferences
 from the argon dimer 40Ar38Ar, which interferes with detecting 78Se.
 At the end of the Se(IV) diffusion pulse, the clay was extruded from the diffusion cell and sliced into
 0.3-1.3 mm thick slices. The thickness of the clay slices was measured using a digital caliper with a
 precision of 0.1 mm. Each clay slice was split into separate subsamples (approximately 30 mg each)
 for extraction with either phosphate buffer (adsorbed + dissolved Se) or HCl (total Se). Adsorbed +
 dissolved Se was determined by adding 2 mL of 0.1 M KH2PO4 buffer at pH 8, mixing for two hours,
 and then centrifuging, filtering, and analyzing the extract. The phosphate buffer was purged with N2
 gas to remove any dissolved O2 before extraction to minimize the Se oxidation during the extraction.
 Phosphate extracts were analyzed for Se(IV) and Se(VI) using HPLC-ICP-MS, as described above.
 Total Se was measured by adding 2 mL of 0.5 M HCl, mixing for 24 hours, and then centrifuging,
 filtering, and analyzing the extract for total Se by ICP-MS. Residual Se, likely representing Se(0) or
 Se(-II), is determined by subtracting adsorbed + dissolved Se (phosphate-extracted) from total Se
 (HCl extracted). Fe(II) concentrations were also measured in the HCl extracts using ferrozine.

 4.4 Se(IV) Experiments: Results and Discussion
 4.4.1 Se(IV) Sorption
 Se(IV) adsorption to purified montmorillonite was investigated over various chemical conditions
 relevant to the Se(IV) diffusion experiments. Distribution coefficients (Kd; adsorbed Se/dissolved Se)
 ranged from 0 to 60.2 L/kg for the conditions investigated (Figure 4-2). Se(IV) adsorption varied as a
 function of pH (Figure 4-2), Se concentration (Figure 4-3), and Ca concentration (Figure 4-2 and
 Figure 4-4). The lowest adsorption was observed at 1 mM Se(IV), with Kd values decreasing linearly
 with the log of the dissolved Se concentration (Figure 4-3). This has important implications for
 interpreting diffusion data and predicting Se(IV) transport because many diffusion models assume
 that Kd is constant as a function of concentration. Se(IV) adsorption (Kd) increases with Ca
 concentration (Figure 4-4). However, the role of pH is more complex (Figure 4-2), with different
 trends for different total Se(IV) concentrations and electrolyte compositions. At 1 mM Se(IV),
 adsorption generally increases from pH 6.0 to pH 7.5 (for Na and N-Ca samples) or stays flat (Ca). At
 lower Se(IV) concentrations (0.1 and 0.01 mM), adsorption stays flat or decreases from pH 6.0 to pH
 7.5.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 42

 0.1 mM, and 0.01 mM) and three solution compositions, including Na: 0.1 M NaCl, Na-
                     Ca: 0.085 M NaCl + 0.005 M CaCl2, Ca: 0.033 M CaCl2.

 Where Clow is the concentration in the low-concentration reservoir, Chigh is the concentration in the

 pulse, the autosampler missed several samples for the Ca cell during the first 48 hours. This is a
 critical period when the tracer is breaking through. Therefore, after 300 hours, the tritium was
 flushed from the cells by replacing the high-concentration reservoir with a fresh background solution,

 and the tritium pulse was repeated. The normalized mass flux for both tritium pulses agreed well
 (Zheng et al., 2023), and the second pulse is shown in Figure 4-5. Tritium diffusion reached steady-
 state after approximately 70 hours, with higher flux in the Ca cell compared to the Na and Na-Ca
 cells. This observation is consistent with previous experiments (Zheng et al., 2022). Slight
 differences in the measured 3H normalized flux may result from minor differences in cell packing,

 approximately 2.84 kg/L (Bourg et al., 2006; Tournassat & Appelo, 2011). There were some minor
 differences in the calculated bulk density and porosity for the three cells due to differences in the
 moisture content of the clays at the time of packing. A summary of the diffusion cell parameters,

 Se(IV) diffusion was continued for 224 days, and the normalized diffusive flux is shown in Figure 4-6.
 As with HTO, Se diffusive flux follows the order Ca>Na-Ca>Na. The Ca cell appears to reach steady-

November 8, 2024                                                                                                        45

 state after approximately 3,500 hrs (~146 days), while the Na-Ca and Na cells don’t reach steady-
 state until about 4,200 (175 days) and 4,900 hrs (~204 days), respectively. This contrasts with
 Se(VI) diffusion tested previously, in which Se(VI) reached a steady state after approximately 300
 hours (~ 12.5 days). Given the higher sorption of Se(IV) to clay minerals compared to Se(VI), the
 slower breakthrough of Se(IV) is expected. While modeling is required to determine the diffusive
 parameters for Se(IV) (Kd, Se [Se accessible porosity], and  [tortuosity parameter]), the apparent
 slower breakthrough for the Ca cell compared to the Na cell is consistent with higher sorption of
 Se(IV) in the presence of Ca.
 A small amount of Se(VI) was detected in the low-concentration reservoirs, typically constituting 1-5%
 of the total dissolved Se and up to 12%. A very small amount of Se(VI) was detected in the high-
 concentration reservoir (0.1% of total Se) at the end of the experiment. Given the faster diffusion of
 Se(VI) compared to Se(IV), the Se(VI) detected in the low-concentration reservoirs may represent the
 transport of trace Se(VI) from the high reservoir, although the possibility of oxidation of Se(IV) to
 Se(VI) within the clay plug cannot be ruled out.

 Figure 4-6. Normalized Se flux in three diffusion cells (Na: 0.1 M NaCl, Na-Ca: 0.085 M NaCl +
 0.005 M CaCl2, Ca: 0.033 M CaCl2). Top: full scale; Bottom: zoomed in y-axis to better
 see Na and Na-Ca data.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                46

 Extractable Se concentrations in the clay profile at the end of the experiment are shown in Figure
 4-7. Only Se(IV) (no Se(VI)) was detected in the phosphate extract (adsorbed + dissolved Se). Total
 Se concentrations decrease along the clay profile. The Ca cell had the highest Se concentrations,
 followed by the Na-Ca and Na cells. This trend is consistent with higher Se(IV) adsorption on Ca-
 montmorillonite compared to Na-montmorillonite, as observed in batch experiments. Similar trends
 are observed in the phosphate extract (adsorbed + dissolved Se), although the differences between
 the cells near the high-concentration reservoir are much smaller. A small amount of residual Se was
 detected, with the largest amount in the Ca cell. This residual Se may represent Se(0) or Se(-II)
 associated with the solid phase. However, we cannot fully rule out incomplete extraction of adsorbed
 Se(IV) in the phosphate extraction. Se(IV) reduction to Se(0) or Se(-II) has been observed under
 anaerobic conditions (Charlet et al., 2007; 2012; Ma et al., 2019). Both reservoirs were maintained
 under aerobic conditions during this experiment, minimizing the development of anaerobic
 conditions. HCl-extractable Fe(II) was measured in the clay profile (Figure 4-7D) as an indication of
 the redox status of the clay. No changes in Fe(II) concentration were noted.

 Figure 4-7. Clay profiles for the three diffusion cells (Na: 0.1 M NaCl, Na-Ca: 0.085 M NaCl + 0.005
 M CaCl2, Ca: 0.033 M CaCl2) at the end of the Se(IV) diffusion experiment. The high Se
 reservoir was located at 0 mm, and the low Se reservoir was located at 5 mm. (A) Total
 extractable Se (HCl-extractable), (B) adsorbed + dissolved Se (phosphate-extractable),
 and (C) residual Se [Total - (Adsorbed + Dissolved)]. Se concentrations are expressed
 as a concentration in the total porewater volume divided by the concentration in the
 high-concentration reservoir, and (D) HCl-extractable Fe(II) in the clay at the end of the
 experiment. Fe(II) concentrations are expressed per gram of clay.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  47

 4.5 Summary and Future Work
 While uranium is the primary constituent of spent nuclear fuel, 79Se is a major driver of the safety
 case for radioactive waste disposal due to its long half-life (3.3 x 105 yr) and presence as relatively
 mobile anionic species under a range of chemical conditions (e.g., HSe-, SeO32-, SeO42-). In this work,
 we investigated the adsorption and diffusion of two key Se species, Se(IV) (selenite) and Se(VI)
 (selenate), through compacted clay over a range of chemical conditions relevant to spent fuel and
 waste repository environments. These data and model results can be used to predict Se transport in
 EBS and better understand some critical variables affecting Se transport that must be considered in
 performance assessment (PA) models.
 Diffusion experiments revealed that an increase in the Ca/Na ratio in solution and on clay surfaces
 leads to a rise in HTO, Se(VI), and Se(IV) diffusivity in montmorillonite samples. The increase of HTO
 diffusivity can be explained by a change in clay microstructure wherein the presence of
 exchangeable Ca promotes the formation of thicker layer stacks compared to Na, which in turn
 promotes the presence of larger interparticle pores. These microstructural changes will affect the
 transport of all radionuclides through clay barriers, including Se. In addition, the aqueous speciation
 of Se(VI) has a significant effect on Se transport. Se(VI) diffusion is limited by anion exclusion due to
 the formation of the negatively charged species SeO42-. However, in the presence of Ca2+, forming
 CaSeO4 neutral ion pairs, which can access a significant portion of the total porosity, increases
 selenate diffusivity. We provide constraints on model parameters that can be used to predict a range
 of Se(VI) transport in the EBS. The results also emphasize the need to understand the overall
 chemical evolution of repository conditions as changes in aqueous speciation due to many factors
 (e.g., mineral dissolution/precipitation or groundwater penetration) can change the mobility of
 essential species.
 Se(IV) adsorption to montmorillonite is much higher than Se(VI) adsorption. This leads to much
 longer breakthrough times for Se(IV) diffusion (>146 days to reach steady-state) compared to Se(VI)
 (~10 days to reach steady-state). Despite greater Se(IV) adsorption in the presence of Ca, Se(IV)
 breakthrough in diffusion experiments is faster, and Se(IV) diffusive flux is greater in Ca-
 montmorillonite. The adsorption of Se(IV) to montmorillonite is non-linear concerning aqueous
 concentration, and thus, a constant Kd modeling approach will not accurately describe the diffusive
 transport of Se(IV). This has important implications for Se transport through EBS.
 In the next year, we will create a surface complexation model for Se(IV) adsorption to montmorillonite
 and model Se(IV) diffusion experiments. Future experimental work will build on the key results from
 this work, namely that chemical speciation and redox state of crucial risk drivers (e.g., Se) are
 essential for the safety case and that the specific molecular scale environment of the clay barriers
 (e.g., cation type and concentration) is necessary for their overall performance. Our recent work has
 focused on purified clays. However, deployment scale clay barrier materials will offer more ability to
 chemically react with key constituents due to increased levels of trace contaminants, e.g., Fe(II), or
 modify clay behavior due to other solids phases (e.g., CaCO3). In addition, it is well established that
 long-term heating can change the properties of the barrier materials. To address these critical
 challenges, we will begin working with heat-treated bentonite samples (e.g., from HOTBENT heated
 column experiments) for adsorption and redox reactivity with radionuclides such as Se and U. These
 materials are expected to have more complex chemical and physical behavior than purified samples.
 We will approach that complexity first through batch experiments designed to interrogate critical
 variables identified in previous work. The batch experiments and subsequent interpretation and
 modeling will then be used to assess the need for and design of future diffusion experiments. These
 results will provide an additional important step for predicting clay barrier performance with natural
 materials under more realistic and varied operational conditions.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   48

 5 COUPLED MICROBIAL ABIOTIC PROCESSES IN EBS AND HOST ROCK
 MATERIALS
 5.1 Introduction
 This work was designed to investigate whether residual microbial activity exists in clay that was
 emplaced in a heated and dry environment for extended periods of time. This is important because
 microorganisms that survive in a nuclear waste repository can become active over time as gas is
 introduced or produced in the system, water is introduced through cracks in the containment or
 other environmental changes. For FY23, effort was used to complete the data analysis and prepare a
 manuscript for submission. No additional experimental work was performed.

 5.2 Methods
 Methods used were previously reported in FY22 (Zheng et al., 2022) and are briefly summarized here.
 Samples were collected in 2015 from several heating zones at the Full-Scale Engineered Barrier Experiment
 (FEBEX) site (high heat, mid-heat, and no heat). The final samples had an average volume of 18 cm3, were
 wrapped in plastic, vacuum sealed in aluminized Mylar bags, and stored at room temperature. To prepare
 the sample of each clay type, the Mylar bags containing the subsamples were opened and a piece was broken
 away from the large block. A subsample was acquired and added to 60 mL serum vials containing 10 mL
 of sterile Minimal Salts Media (MSM) (Sigma M9), which contains 15 g/L KH2PO4, 64 g/L Na2HPO4
 ⋅7H2O, 2.5 g/L NaCl, 5.0 g/L NH4Cl. 15 psi of gas mix was added to each serum bottle. This was done by
 starting with a full serum bottle of air, adding 7 mL of CO2, and pressuring to 15 psi with an N2/5% H2 mix.
 All gas was filtered through a 0.2 µm filter. This method provided a consistent gas mix for all bottles, with
 an average final composition of 5% CO2, 2.8% H2, 9% O2, with a balance of N2.
 Sample designations were: ‘T-ambient’ (non-heated, from BD-59-10); T-35, (35°C, from BD-48-4); T-50
       (50°C, from BD- 48-5); T-95 (95°C, from BD-48-6) (Villar et al., 2018). For Experiments 1 and 2, only
 T-ambient and T-95 were tested. All four samples were included in Experiments 3 and 4 (Table 5-1). Sterile
 control samples (autoclaved clay) and blanks (no clay, only media) were run for assessment. Four to six
 samples were prepared of each clay type, an additional two to three samples of autoclaved controls for each
 clay and three blank controls for the experiment.

 Samples were placed horizontally on a rocking table and incubated at 35°C. To monitor activity, gas
 composition of the serum bottle headspace was analyzed periodically. Gas analysis was performed
 on a Shimadzu GC-8AIT with Argon (Ar) carrier gas, which allowed for simultaneous quantitative
 determination of H2, O2, N2, and CO2. After the experiment, bottles were either maintained at 35°C
 without shaking or destructively sampled to analyze pH, solids content, chemical analysis, and
 microbial community.
 Four experiments have been completed, as described in Table 5-1. Experiment 1 focused on
 developing experimental protocols. Experiment 2 consisted of the high heat samples (T-95) and the
 control, ambient heated sample (T-ambient).
 At the conclusion of the experiment, three samples from the T-ambient incubation bottles, two with
 high, medium high, and one with low H2 consumption rates, and one sample from the High T-95
 bottles (with no H2 consumption) were sent for Deoxyribonucleic Acid (DNA) microbial analysis.

 5.3 Data Interpretation
 The FY22 report shows gas composition data for H2, CO2, and O2 from all experiments. In summary,
 the non-heated condition showed the largest shifts in gas concentration over time. The high-heat
 experiment showed little or no activity; there were no significant changes compared to the controls.
 In the mid-temperature samples, some minor activity was observed, but less than in the unheated
 treatment.
 In the 59-10 samples (T ambient), the overall trends of oxygen and hydrogen consumption, along
 with CO2 production, serve as evidence of an active microbial community. Figure 5-1 through Figure
 5-3, which plot all the gas consumption data over the experiments for the T-ambient condition,
 illustrate these trends. Note that the sharp increases in values in these plots are due to periodic re-
 gassing of the system. However, the specific nature of the metabolic processes driving this gas
 consumption and production remains to be seen, highlighting the importance of considering the
 potential metabolic diversity of barrier materials. To better understand the metabolic potentials,
 extraction of DNA was attempted but this proved to be challenging due to clay minerology and low
 biomass. Sufficient DNA was obtained from the no-heat samples (T-ambient) to show that at the
 phylum level, experiments were dominated by Actinobacteria, with some replicates from Experiment
 4 also exhibiting a notable abundance of Firmicutes, both of which have been found in isolates from
 nuclear waste repositories (Moll et al., 2015; Povedano-Priego et al., 2021; Mitzscherling et al.,
 2023). However, at the genus level, the samples were largely composed of rare taxa and specific
 species cannot be identified. Among the known taxa, the Actinobacteria genera Saccharomonospora
 and Saccharopolyspora were present. The relative abundance of rare taxa at the genus level was
 significantly higher in the non-heated samples compared to the heated samples (p<0.5). Conversely,
 the relative abundance of Saccharopolyspora at the genus level was significantly higher in the
 heated samples than in the non-heated samples (p < 0.005).

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 50

 Figure 5-1. Oxygen consumption from all non-sterile replicates from T-ambient (59-10)
 experiments show a consistent downward trend. Note that the sharp increases in
 oxygen content were due to the re-gassing of samples in the mid-experiment.

 Figure 5-2. CO2 concentrations (% composition) from all non-sterile replicates from all T-ambient
 (59-10) experiments showed a slightly upward trend for most samples. Note that the
 sharp increases in CO2 content were due to the re-gassing of samples in the mid-
 experiment.

November 8, 2024                                                                                             51

 Figure 5-3. H2 consumption from all non-sterile replicates from all T-ambient (59-10) experiments
 showed a slightly upward trend for the samples. Note that the sharp increases in H2
 content were due to the re-gassing of samples in the mid-experiment.

 5.4 Summary and Future Work
 The experimental results revealed that compacted clay materials can sustain microbial communities
 with the potential for growth. However, it is intriguing that material from the hot zone did not exhibit
 this growth potential within the experimental time frames. It remains unclear whether this lack of
 potential is due to the heating process, the drying process, or a combination of both. Additionally, the
 specific communities that developed were found to be sensitive to the gas composition and solids
 content. This tendency is supported by the influence of high solid-to-solution ratios observed in our
 second experiment and the lack of significant microbial activity in the samples closest to the heaters.
 Moreover, the differential impact on groundwater media samples compared to minimal salt media
 and observing non-H2 metabolisms in several samples underscores the importance of understanding
 the composition of fluids within the formation to predict the dominant metabolic processes.
 The absence of growth in the control (sterile) samples indicates that the observed enrichment of
 communities originates from bacteria already present in the clay before incubation. Although the
 duration of these experiments is relatively long by laboratory standards, they are considerably short
 compared to the time scales involved in repository dynamics. Over longer time scales, microbial
 activity within the barrier material is expected to be significant. Given the limited moisture and
 presence of other chemical species, it is reasonable to assume that this microbial activity would
 primarily occur along fractures and at the boundaries of the formation.
 To the best of our knowledge, this is the only experimental study demonstrating the activity of
 microbial communities from FEBEX materials under minimal media conditions, as opposed to growth
 media, highlighting the presence of H2 metabolism. Further research should explore the range of
 metabolisms possible within the barrier material's existing community and investigate how specific
 chemical and physical conditions regulate the microbial growth rate.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  52

 6 HEATING AND HYDRATION COLUMN TEST ON BENTONITE
 6.1 Introduction
 This section describes HotBENT-Lab experiments designed to obtain well-characterized datasets to
 understand the heating and hydration of bentonite clays and provide modeling data to predict
 performance in the HotBENT field-scale test results. Furthermore, the aim is to develop standard
 systems for future studies of bentonite under high-temperature conditions.
 As described in our FY22 report (Zheng et al., 2022), we dismantled the benchtop-scale column
 experiments, which had temperatures up to 200°C in the center of a packed bentonite cylinder. Two
 test columns were used—a control column undergoing only hydration and an experiment column
 experiencing heating and hydration. During the experiment, we took frequent X-ray Computed
 Tomography (XCT) images to provide insight into the spatiotemporal evolution of:
 (1) hydration/dehydration, (2) clay swelling/shrinkage, (3) displacement/deformation, and
 (4) mineral precipitation. A comprehensive post-dismantling characterization of bentonite samples
 was carried out after the column tests were run for 1.5 years.
 In FY23, we conducted comprehensive testing and analysis of the collected bentonite samples from
 HotBENT #2 experiments. The new set of column experiments (HotBENT #3) has been planned but
 delayed due to the non-operational Computed Tomography (CT) scanner. Effort was instead directed
 to analyzing experimental data, including the Electrical Resistivity Tomography (ERT) data, coupons
 emplaced in the sample, and the bentonite clay recovered from the columns.
 Table 6-1 and Table 6-2 summarize the differences between HotBENT Lab #1 and Lab #2.

 6.2 Analysis of HotBENT Lab #2 Dismantled Samples
 6.2.1 XCT Imaging
 XCT images were collected to provide a 3D visualization of the density distribution in the column,
 which can be used to visualize density changes due to water saturation, clay swelling, or structural
 deformation during the experiments. A GE Lightspeed 16 medical CT scanner was used to scan the
 two columns periodically throughout the experiments. Based on the column dimensions, the spatial
 resolution of the CT images is 400 × 400 × 625 μm. Because of the large weight of the experimental

November 8, 2024                                                                                              54

 columns (~55 lbs), a cart/lift was used to move the columns onto and off the CT scanner table.
 Alignment of the scans was achieved via markings on the CT table and the outside of the column, as
 well as critical features in the CT images (high and low-density anomalies) inside the column during
 post-processing. The post-processing and analysis of the obtained CT scan images were conducted
 using self-developed codes in ImageJ software (Rasband, 1997–2022).
 The correlation between bentonite bulk density and CT number (i.e., the calibration curve and
 equation), was obtained by scanning separate columns compacted with bentonite of known bulk
 densities (see Figure 6-1). Note that CT numbers at 0 and 1 g/cm3 were obtained by scanning air
 and water, respectively. This close correlation between material bulk density and CT number has
 been suggested by Mull (1984). Seven bentonite samples were collected in the heated column after
 dismantling using tube samplers at an internal diameter of 0.7 cm further to verify the calibration
 curve specifically for the experiment. The bulk wet mass was measured right after sampling. High-
 resolution XCT scanning was then applied to obtain the sample volume so that the bulk wet density
 could be calculated precisely and compared with CT density (density obtained from the CT images
 using the calibration equation). The CT density for each sample deviated <5% from the measured
 bulk density (𝜌𝑤𝑒𝑡 ), indicating the effectiveness and precision of CT images in predicting bentonite
 bulk density. Assuming 𝜌𝐶𝑇= 𝜌𝑤𝑒𝑡 , the dry density (𝜌𝑑𝑟𝑦 ), degree of saturation (𝑠𝑟 ) of the collected
 bentonite samples after the experiments can then be calculated as:
 𝑚𝑑𝑟𝑦

 𝑠𝑟 =
 𝜙 𝑚𝑤𝑒𝑡 ⁄𝜌𝐶𝑇

 where mdry refers to the stable sample mass measured after oven drying, and ρdry is the mineral or

 Karnland et al. (2006) measured.

 number from XCT images. The equation derived from the data-fitting was then used to
 compute bentonite bulk wet density from XCT scans during the experiment.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 55

 6.2.2 Continuous Temperature Monitoring
 Temperature monitoring was conducted using thermocouples (type T) installed in the column. Twelve
 (12) thermocouples were installed in each column between the heater shaft and the column. The
 distances of these thermocouples from the heater shaft were 0 (on the heater), 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 2.5, 3,
 and 3.5 inches (the latter was on the inside of the column wall). Two thermocouples were placed
 next to the two ends of the heater, while another two thermocouples were placed axially parallel and
 radially 1.5 inches away in the bentonite. These feedthrough-coupled thermocouples were acquired
 from Conax Technologies with an accuracy of 0.1°C. The temperature readings from the
 thermocouples were recorded at 20-second intervals with a Keithley data logger (Tektronix,
 Beaverton, Oregon, USA) throughout the experiment. In addition to the thermocouples inside the
 columns, additional temperature measurements were taken on the outside wall of the column.

 6.2.3 Time Lapse ERT Monitoring
 ERT data were acquired using a DAS-1 ERT instrument (Multi-Phase Technologies, LLC, USA) and
 electrodes to send electrical current or measure electrical potential. Measurements were conducted
 using a pre-defined monitoring schedule, where a total of ~2,600 data points were collected for a
 single resistivity survey from each column, which took ~ 60 minutes. The data collection frequency
 was coordinated with CT scan frequency, with denser acquisition periods during the initial stage of
 the experiment and reduced for the long-term experiment as the rate of resistivity change decreased.
 Data was collected autonomously via Multi-phase Technologies, LLC (MPT) software. Analysis of the
 post-experiment collected datasets included data quality assessment, inversion, and visualization.
 The open software BERT (Boundless ERT, Günther & Rucker, 2013) and Paraview (Kitware, NY) were
 used for data inversion and visualization.

 6.2.4 Metal Coupons
 Figure 6-2 shows the metal coupons placed in the heated and non-heated column, including
 Stainless steel 316 (SS 316), Stainless steel 304 (SS 304), low carbon steel, and two coated
 coppers from the HotBENT field site. All metal coupons were imaged to have a detailed surface
 geometry before the experiments, so a comparison of potential corrosion is available after the
 experiments. After the experiments, we induced dents to some coupon surfaces as comparison
 marks. All coupons were recovered during the dismantling of the HotBENT Lab #2 experiments, and
 any changes are being analyzed.

 Figure 6-2. The metal coupons are used in the heated and non-heated columns. The color bars in
 the Figure bound the surface roughness, varying from 0 to 2,276 μm in the top right
 Figure due to the induced dent and 0-287 μm in the bottom right Figure without dent.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                56

 6.2.5 Emplaced Tracers
 Silicon Nitride ceramic ball bearings, diameter 3 mm (BC Precision, TN, USA) were used as tracers
 and were placed at regular intervals throughout the columns. The purpose of these tracers was to
 track the movement of the clay caused by hydration and strain. As the bentonite clay swelling
 occurred, forces developed which pushed these ball bearings either inward or outward and their
 movements were used to estimate swelling and strain within the clay.

 6.3 Results
 6.3.1 XCT
 One of the main advantages of XCT is that data is collected in real time during the experiment, so the
 bulk analysis of that data was completed for the FY22 report. In summary, Figure 6-3 shows the 3D
 view of the initial CT density distribution in both columns, including: (1) the heater and heater shaft
 in the center, (2) bentonite compacted between the sand layer and the central shaft, (3) the sand
 layer surrounding the clay, (4) the ERT arrays emplaced at the sand-clay boundary, and
 (5) thermocouple sensor, tracer particles and metal coupons emplaced in bentonite. The density
 variations and bedding layers in bentonite were induced by uneven compaction and were expected
 to result in the preferential brine flow in the following hydration process. Overall, the average CT
 density of bentonite for the heated column is 1.54 g/cm3. The dry density and porosity for the heated
 column were calculated as 1.46 g/cm3 and 0.44. For the non-heated column, the average CT
 densities for the two segments were 1.68 and 1.58 g/cm3, the corresponding dry densities and
       porosities thus were 1.59 and 1.50 g/cm3, 0.39 and 0.43.

 Figure 6-3. Cross-sectional CT images of the two compacted columns used for heating and
 hydration experiments. The blue curve presents the dry density profile calculated from
 the bulk CT density and the water content.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 57

 6.3.1.1 3D Hydration and Temperature Profile
 Overall, the radial symmetry of the hydration distribution was observed from the sand layer to the
 central shaft. The primary factor contributing to the spatiotemporal density distribution is the
 increase in bentonite density due to hydration from the sand boundary. Both columns (Figure 6-4)
 show an increase in bentonite density ahead of the density peak (the hydration front) at an early
 stage. This suggests significant compression of the interior bentonite, which initially had a lower
 density following hydration.
 Additional analysis was completed on the heating data, which is presented in Figure 6-5. Plots are
 shown with both standard and log scales to highlight the steady-state temperature profiles and the
 initial step-wise heating period.
 Density distribution and changes were observed in the columns for ~490 days. Additional details of
 the experiment can be found in the report by Zheng et al. (2022). In HotBENT Lab #1, we presented
 a low-density zone around the heater shaft after heating due to water evaporation. In HotBENT Lab
 #2, we no longer observed any water evaporation or dry-out zone from the CT images. This may be
 attributed to the increased water pressure in the sand layer from 120 to 290 psi.

 Figure 6-4. Comparison of the changes of radially averaged density vs. radial distance from the
 heater shaft in the two sets of heated columns in HotBENT Lab #1 and #2. It should be

 Pressure in the column was maintained with an inlet ISCO pump (Teledyne ISCO, Lincoln, Nebraska,

 recorded to get a water balance in the system (Figure 6-6 and Figure 6-7). Water uptake was initially
 very rapid, followed by a slower rate. The outlet was clogged early in the experiment, so outlet flows
 were accounted for in the plots below but were not significant over the length of the experiment.
 Overall net uptake was more substantial in the heated column, and dismantling results (see Figure
 6-16 in section 6.2.2) show although the maximum saturation was similar in both columns, the
 heated column had higher water content on average.

 6.3.2 Electrical Resistivity Tomography (ERT) Results
 ERT data were collected periodically throughout the experiment and inverted to obtain the three-
 dimensional distribution of the resistivity of the clay columns. Figure 6-8 (non-heated) and Figure 6-9
 (heated) show a few selected ERT scans of the columns from the start to the end of the experiment.
 Three cross-sectional planes – longitudinal, frontal, and transverse – were plotted, with the inlet
 injection port to the lower left, the outlet port to the upper right, and the sand-clay hydration
 boundary at the circumferential periphery of each image. The resistivity of the non-heated column
 decreased progressively, spatially inward from the hydration front at the outer radius and temporally
 in a short period, with no significant changes in the mean resistivity of the entire column observed
 beyond day 40. For the heated column, the spatial contrast in resistivity became less evident due to
 the coupled factors of higher temperature at the radial center and increasing hydration from the
 radial periphery, both leading to a decrease in resistivity.

 Figure 6-8. Non-heated column: time lapse of selected ERT (the color bar is log scaled).

 Figure 6-9. Heated column: time-lapse of selected ERT.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 61

 To quantitively monitor the hydrological processes of the clay using ERT, it is essential to establish a
 relationship between the resistivity and water content of the BCV (Bentonite Černý Vrch) bentonite.
 We selected the widely adopted Waxman-Smits model (Eqn. (6-3); Eqn. (6-4); Eqn. (6-5)) (Waxman &
 Smits, 1968; Dacy & Martin, 2008), which includes a surface conductivity term that contributes
 significantly to the overall conductivity when the medium is rich in surface charges:

 The constants are the CEC [meq g-1] cation exchange capacity of the BCV bentonite (cec = 0.637)

 In the second HotBent experiment, the number and placement of the electrodes and thermocouples
 were significantly increased and improved, providing higher coverage of the modeled temperature
 and resistivity data in the columns. Resistivity (ERT), density (CT), and temperature (thermocouples)
 data were spatially registered, and two calibration datasets were selected: saturated and
 unsaturated. The saturated dataset includes clay volume close to the sand-clay boundary (radial
 distance to the center of the heater ≥ 0.055 m) and later in the experiment (time elapse > 70 days),
 and the unsaturated dataset was a subset of the clay volume close to the heater (r ≤ 0.025 m) and
 in the beginning of the experiment (< 2 days). Figure 6-10 shows the distribution of density and
 resistivity of the calibration datasets from the non-heated column. The saturated clay had less
 variable density and resistivity than the unsaturated clay.

 unsaturated clay, spatial θs and φ were calculated from the pre-experiment dry CT scan, assuming no

 non-heated columns to calibrate one model, the parameter estimation shows good fitting results and
 produces water content estimates. Since the type of clay, the packing density, and the fluid
 composition were all targeted to match conditions at the field, the application of the model at the
 field ERT datasets can be evaluated.

 6.3.3 Postmortem Sampling

 In FY22, both HotBENT Lab #2 columns were depressurized and dismantled. The hydrated column

 (Figure 6-14 and Figure 6-15). Sample tubes were emplaced in the clay and CT scans were done to

 tubes was collected as well and were defined in three radial groups – (1) 0 to 2 cm, (2) 2-5 cm, and
 (3) 5-7.5 cm ring distances from the center of the column. The center most layer was kept intact and
 preserved for future analyses. Characterization of the bentonite samples after dismantling, including
 the water content, dry density, degree of saturation are shown in Figure 6-16.

 6.3.3.2 Metals Analysis
 Metals on clays removed from the column were analyzed by total digestion followed by ICP-MS
 (Enthalpy Analytical, Berkeley, California). Samples were selected from different locations in the

 coupons, or the Grimsel water. Silicon was requested, but the analysis could not be completed by the
 contract laboratory, so it is still pending analysis. The results are presented below in Figure 6-18,
 along with the relative positions of the emplaced coupons.
 Due to clogging, only a slight flow occurred after columns were saturated, perhaps limiting the
 movement of cations through the sample. Some increases in Cr, Cu, Mg, and Ni were observed,
 possibly due to corroding coupons. Analysis was conducted on wet samples, and it would be
 appropriate to repeat it with dried samples that have been rinsed with deionized water to remove
 pore water.

       6.3.3.3    Coupons
 Emplaced coupons were removed from the columns during dismantling. Coupons were received from

 Stainless steel (304 SS) and 316 Stainless steel (316 SS). Each coupon was machined to a 1 cm3
 cube, placed close to the heater shaft, along the length of the column, and packed in place with the
 bentonite.
 Primarily the results to date are imaging data, and a more thorough analysis is needed to assess any
 chemical changes to the coupon surfaces. In addition to the following images, coupons were
 weighed before and after emplacement and overall changes were within experimental error, except
 for the LCS which lost 1.7% of its weight in the non-heated column and 3.26% in the heated column.
 Imaging techniques used were standard digital photography, surface imaging, and electron
 microscopy. In Figure 6-19 through Figure 6-22, the before and after digital photographic images of
 one or two faces of the coupons are shown. Note that an indentation was made on most of one side
 of each coupon so the surfaces could be re-identified after emplacement. Although color levels and
 exposures were potentially different in the before and after images, substantial physical changes can
 be seen in most coupons, including some erosion and deposition on the surfaces.
 The surface roughness of the coupons after removal (Figure 6-23 through Figure 6-26) can be
 compared to Figure 6-2, a typical surface roughness map of the coupons before emplacement in the
 columns. While these results are not yet quantitative, they demonstrate changes to the metal
 surfaces after 13 and 14 months of emplacement.

 6.3.3.4 XRD on MX-80 Clay Removed from the Columns
 XRD analysis of clays is not trivial, and while current analysis results demonstrate that changes have
 occurred to the bentonite during hydration and heating; further study is needed to quantify hydration
 layers. MX-80 bentonite has been shown to have 4 hydration layers, corresponding to 4 layers of
 interstitial water (Perdrial & Warr, 2011). Illitization of clay can also be detected in dried MX-80.
 Since XRD is a tool that can be used to detect the presence of these layers, the primary goal of this
 analysis was to see if the changes can be observed in the hydration layers between the heated and
 non-heated columns, as well as radial distance from the heater.
 To obtain XRD data, hydrated samples removed from the HotBENT#2 columns were pressed into an
 Aluminum coupon and covered with Kapton tape to reduce water loss during analysis. Prior to
 analysis clays had been stored in sealed containers at 4°C to maintain moisture levels. It should be
 noted that additional method development on preparing an XRD sample is warranted, as the height
 and smoothness of the sample surface and application and seal of the Kapton tape could be
 improved by using a modified sample holder. For the heated column, four samples of clays from the
 dismantling representing layers, in order from inlet to the column middle, D, F, L7, L6 (Figure 6-15)
 were analyzed. For the non-heated column, layers H, F, and L11 were analyzed, with H being close to
 the inlet and L11 near the column center. For each layer, radial samples were analyzed, with R1
 samples closest to the outer sand layer, R2 is midway, and R3 is closest to the heater.
 Figure 6-27 compares the dry bentonite (Nagra-Dry) with heated and hydrated bentonite,
 demonstrating a hydration peak formed at early 2θ representing hydration. Figure 6-28 shows
 samples from L6 in the hydrated and hydrated column which is located near the column center. In
 the region from 3θ-6θ, peaks in the L6 R1 scan (nearest to the outside of the column) around 3.5,
 4θ, 4.5θ and 5θ representing the four hydration layers. The samples L6 R2 (mid radial distance from
 the center) and L6 R3 (nearest to the heater) show predominant peaks for only single hydration and
 the innermost sample, L6 R3 also shows an illitization peak, suggesting that samples near the
 heater are dryer than those near the center. This corroborates observations of increased swelling
 near the sand/clay interface that was seen with the XCT data and the emplaced tracers. In general,
 this trend holds for the heated column for layers L11, F, and D which are removed from locations
 toward the inlet. The R3 samples, closer to the heater shaft, have predominantly the 5θ peak, and

November 8, 2024                                                                                                73

 the R1 and R2 samples are shifted more than to 4θ and 4.5θ peaks (Figure 6-29 through Figure
 6-31). For the non-heated samples (Figure 6-32 through Figure 6-34) this shift toward less hydrated
 clay in the center of the column persisted but was not as pronounced and in some central R3 layers
 multiple hydration layers were detected.

 Figure 6-27. Comparison of a heated and hydrated clay sample as compared to the dry bentonite
 used for filling the column (NAGRA dry). Note the large peak formed at 2θ ~5 that is
 formed when hydrated.

November 8, 2024                                                                                             74

 ring (R1), middle ring (R2), and center ring (R3). The rings closest to the heater show a
