 6.4 Summary and Future Work
 HotBENT Lab #2 test using granulated Wyoming bentonite has been completed, and analysis of the
 clays is ongoing. Additional analysis of the coupon surface changes and interpretation of the XRD
 analysis is needed. The metals analysis will be re-done for washed/dried clay to remove pore water.
 Plans for collecting CEC will also aid interpretation.
 Meanwhile, HotBent #3, using Czech bentonite, is in the planning stage. This bentonite was used to
 pack a section of the NAGRA field test heaters. Due to problems with our XCT scanner, this
 experiment has been delayed. If the CT scanner is not replaced within a reasonable time frame, the
 experiment will be completed without this monitoring tool but will include ERT monitoring and
 additional sensors (such as humidity sensors) to monitor water content in the clay. Some column
 inlet and outlet design modifications are needed to prevent clogging experienced in HotBENT#2.
 The continuation of this work is necessary because the bulk of the nuclear waste design work in the
 U.S. is done with modeling, and this experiment provides an opportunity to test and measure the
 parameters needed for the modeling efforts. This year, we learned new analysis techniques for
 investigating changes in the bentonite during heating and hydration, but additional work is required
 to understand the changes. Next year, we will continue the analysis of the bentonites from both
 HotBENT #1 and #2 and will run a new column with the Czech bentonite provided by NAGRA.

November 8, 2024                                                                                              77

 7 MODELING THE CONCRETE / BENTONITE INTERFACE: TASK 12 IN SKB
 EBS TASK FORCE
 7.1 Introduction
 An EBS in a mined geological repository for high-level radioactive waste undergoes coupled thermal,
 hydrological, mechanical, and chemical (THMC) changes. Among these changes, the long-term
 geochemical changes are critical because they affect the migration of radionuclides.
 The geochemical changes of bentonite are triggered by both the thermal perturbation and interaction
 between two different media, for example, canister and bentonite, bentonite and concrete liner,
 bentonite and host rock (if there is no concrete liner), or concrete liner and host rock. The alteration
 of the mineral phase around the interface area of different media may significantly change the rock
 and bentonite porosity and permeability, impacting the long-term migration of radionuclides through
 bentonite buffer. Concrete has been proposed to be used as backfill material, and cementitious
 materials are used as tunnel support (particularly for argillite host rock), mechanical plugs, backfill
 materials, buffer materials, and waste form (Abe & Lida, 2022). The geochemical alteration at the
 interfacial area between bentonite and concrete must be better understood (e.g., Fernández et al.,
       2009; Marty et al., 2015).
 In Task 12 of the SKB EBS task force (Birkholzer et al., 2023), modeling teams collaboratively
 simulate long-term reactions at cement-bentonite interfacial regions to assess mineralogical,
 chemical, and textural changes. Task 12 was established in FY21 and has been conducted by four
 modeling teams: SKB, SURAO, Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory (LBNL), and Sandia National
 Laboratory (SNL). Currently, Task 12 focuses on modeling a benchmark problem and will later
 incorporate experimental data. In this section, we document FY24 LBNL’s effort to simulate the
 benchmark problem in Task 12 of the SKB EBS task force.

 7.2 Description of Task 12
 Task 12 is divided into three subtasks to simulate different initial saturation states of bentonite and
 types of concrete. Subtask A is conducted to study the interaction between MX-80 and Ordinary
 Portland Cement (OPC) (Figure 7-1). Since developing reactive transport is a complex process to
 make the models by different groups comparable, subtask A is a relatively simple case. The
 benchmark specifies the following conditions:
 1. A fully saturated isothermal system (25ºC) consisting of aged OPC concrete and MX-80
 bentonite,
 2. A constant atmospheric pressure of 0.1 MPa,
 3. No-flux boundary conditions, assuming the transport is diffusive, and
 4. The bentonite-to-concrete volume ratio is assumed to be one. In a 1D model, equally long
 domains (of 1 m each) are considered.
 The initial mineralogical and pore water conditions will be discussed later. The simulation time
 interval is 100,000 years.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  78

 Figure 7-1. A schematic of the benchmark model for Subtask A.

 Subtask B focuses on MX-80 and low-pH concrete (LPC) interaction. Because the high pH of OPC
 might lead to undesirable alteration of bentonite, the usage of LPC has been proposed. The
 specification of Subtask B will be the same as that of Subtask A.
 Subtask C is designed to simulate a scenario that concrete is a liner in the emplacement tunnel
 (Figure 7-2), especially for argillite rock that is less indurated, in which case the groundwater from
 the host rock will infiltrate through the concrete liner and then into the bentonite buffer. The
 benchmark case Subtask C specifies the following conditions:
 1. An isothermal system (25ºC) consisting of aged OPC concrete and MX-80 bentonite,
 2. Initially, bentonite is unsaturated, but OPC is fully saturated,
 3. Atmospheric pressure is 0.1 MPa,
 4. Water inflow from the concrete side will be prescribed with constant pressure, however, the
 pressure value hasn’t been decided yet.
 The bentonite-to-concrete volume ratio is assumed to be one. In a 1D model, equally long domains
 (of 1 m each) are considered.

 Figure 7-2. The schematic design of Subtask C (Zheng et al., 2023).

 Other specifications regarding the hydraulic parameters and initial mineralogical and porewater will
 be presented in the following when model development is discussed.
 Mineralogical alteration of bentonite and concrete around the bentonite/concrete interface could be
 induced by (1) the initial disequilibrium between the porewater and mineral phases, and (2) the
 perturbation due to the interaction between bentonite and concrete. Because the latter is what the
 current benchmark in Task 12 is aiming for, it is critical to minimize initial disequilibrium between
 the porewater and minerals phases. The initial mineral and exchanger composition of MX-80 is given
 in the Task 12 description provided by SKB (Rasmusson, 2022). Therefore, our first effort in FY23
 was to check if the initial porewater in bentonite and concrete is close to equilibrium, although a
 complete equilibrium between all the chemical species and minerals is not possible. SKB employed
 a different code (PHREEQC) and a different database, which means the calculation scheme is
 different, and the thermodynamic data for some mineral phases and aqueous complexes might
 differ. As a result, the porewater composition provided by SKB differs from that calculated by LBNL
 (Zheng et al., 2023). One of the reasons is how cation exchangers are involved in the initial
 equilibrium. To be consistent with other teams, the LBNL model is based on the SKB calculated,
 even though the initial composition of water for OPC and LPC is questionable, particularly the high

November 8, 2024                                                                                                79

 concentrations of K and Na. Subtasks A and B were therefore expanded to account for different ways
 of calculating initial water to evaluate the uncertainties caused by the initial water:
 • Subtask A1: It is the original Subtask A, in which the initial porewater of OPC is equilibrated with
 pure water and exchangers,
 • Subtask A2: Similar to Subtask A1, the initial porewater of OPC is equilibrated with groundwater,
 and exchangers are considered.
 • Subtask A3: Similar to Subtask A1, but the initial porewater of OPC is equilibrated with
 groundwater, and exchangers are not considered.
 Similarly, Subtask B is divided into three subtasks:
 • Subtask B1: It is the original Subtask B, in which the initial porewater of LPC is equilibrated with
 pure water, and exchangers are considered.
 • Subtask B2: Similar to Subtask B1, but the initial porewater of LPC is equilibrated with
 groundwater, and exchangers are considered.
 • Subtask B3: Similar to Subtask B1, but the initial porewater of LPC is equilibrated with
 groundwater, and exchangers are not considered.
 LBNL scientists have finished all Subtasks A and B, and modeling results are given in the following
 sections.

 7.3 Modeling for Subtask A
 7.3.1 Initial Conditions
 The initial mineral and exchanger compositions of MX-80 are given in the Task 12 description
 provided by SKB (Rasmusson, 2022). Table 7-1 includes the initial mineral and exchanger
 composition of MX-80, according to Idiart et al. (2020). The initial porewater composition of MX-80 is
 calculated by equilibrating the minerals and exchanger with groundwater at the Äspö hard rock
 laboratory (Svemar et al., 2016). The initial porewater composition is listed in Table 7-2. Note that
 the mineralogical composition of MX-80 has been reported elsewhere (e.g., Bradbury & Baeyens,
 2002), and it is different from what is used in Task 12.
 Table 7-1. Initial mineral and exchanger composition of MX-80 (Idiart et al., 2020)

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 80

 Table 7-2. Initial pore water composition of MX-80 (Idiart et al., 2020).

 The composition of OPC porewater is calculated based on the mineral content of the hydrate
 assemblage (Table 7-3). As mentioned above and in Zheng et al. (2023), three cases with different
 initial pore water were created for Subtask A, see Table 7-4.

 Table 7-3. Suggested mineralogical phase assemblage and exchanger composition of the OPC

November 8, 2024                                                                                           81

 7.3.2 Thermodynamic and Kinetic Data
 Benchmarking a reactive transport model can be very challenging because many aspects of
 developing the model can cause differences in model results. The first aspect is the thermodynamic
 data. Because Task 12 modeling groups are using different codes and the format of the database
 required for the code is different, it is difficult for all groups to use the same database. Eventually,
 LBNL has chosen to use a database that is largely based on data0.dat.YMPv4.0, a database that
 was qualified by the U.S. DOE for the Yucca Mountain project. However, the thermodynamic data for
 minerals in OPC are taken from a database called “ThermoChimie_v11a”
 (https://www.thermochimie-tdb.com/), which is a thermodynamic database initially created and
 developed by Andra (French National Radioactive Waste Management Agency). The SKB and SURAO
 groups also use this database.
 Another aspect that can lead to different modeling results is the kinetic rate for mineral
 dissolution/precipitation. In this report, we are using TOUGHREACT V3.32 to conduct all simulations.
 In the code, mineral dissolution/precipitation can be specified as equilibrium-controlled or kinetically
 controlled. In the simulations presented in the next sections, kinetically controlled mineral
 dissolution/precipitation is used for all the minerals. The kinetic law for mineral
 dissolution/precipitation is given by Xu et al. (2011):
                                                     K 𝜂                                            Eqn. (7-1)
                                        𝑟 = 𝑘𝐴 |1 − ( )θ |
 Q

 where r is the kinetic rate, k is the rate constant (mol/m2/s), which is temperature dependent, A is
 the reactive surface area per kg water, and K is the equilibrium constant for the mineral–water
 reaction written for the destruction of one mole of mineral, and Q is the reaction quotient. Here, for
 simplicity, the exponents θ and η are assumed equal to 1.
 The kinetic rate constants can usually be summed for three mechanisms: neutral, acid, and base
       (Lasaga et al., 1994):
                              − Eanu  1      1              − EaH  1 1   nH
                 k = k25                  −         + k25 exp         −      H
 nu H
 exp   
                              R  T 198.15                   R  T 198.15                     Eqn. (7-2)
                                   − EaOH  1   1   nOH
                      + k25                 −           H

 exp 
                                   R  T 198.15  

 where superscripts nu, H, and OH indicate neutral, acid, and base mechanisms, respectively, E is the
 activation energy, k25 is the rate constant at 25°C, R is the gas constant, T is the absolute
 temperature, a is the activity of the species, and n is a power term (constant). It should be noted that
 reaction rates depend on the reactive surface area A (defined in Eqn. (7-1)), which is a function of
 the product of the specific surface area and the volume fraction of each mineral. Therefore, the
 calibrated values of specific surface area and volume fraction of calcite should be viewed as
 arbitrary and non-unique (co-linearly varying) values, the product being relevant but not each value
 separately.
 The kinetic rates and surface areas for the minerals are the most uncertain aspects of reactive
 transport modeling because the kinetic rate can vary up to 5 orders of magnitude depending on the
 scale of measurements or calibration. Also, the reactive surface area (i.e., the area of a mineral
 phase in the assemblage exposed to the pore water), is not accurately calculated. To focus on the
 effect of chemicals on the reaction paths of minerals in the OPC and MX-80, a kinetic constant of
 7e-9 mol/m2/s, a surface area of 0.3 m2/g, and an activation energy of 105 KJ/mol were used for all
 the minerals (Table 7-5). These values are arbitrary and somewhere between the kinetic rate for
 soluble minerals like calcite and gypsum and slow-reacting minerals like quartz.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  82

 Table 7-5. The kinetic rate for the primary and secondary minerals in OPC.

 The illitization is always a primary concern when bentonite undergoes mineral alteration. In this
 report, we used the illitization rate (the rate of illite precipitation and montmorillonite dissolution)
 calibrated in previous works (Zheng et al., 2015). We also applied the rate for illite and
 montmorillonite for other Al-Si minerals. For the rest of the minerals, the kinetic rate is taken from
 the previous model for bentonite (Zheng et al., 2017).

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  83

 Table 7-6. The kinetic rate for the primary and secondary minerals in MX-80

 7.3.3 Model Setup
 For Task 12 simulations, we designed a 1-D reactive transport model to simulate the interaction
 between bentonite and concrete. The model extends 100 cm on each side with MX-80 sitting on the
 left side, and no-flux boundary conditions are imposed such that there is only diffusive flux across
 the interface. The model runs for 100,000 years. The porosity is 0.11 for OPC and 0.423 for MX-80.
 The following equation was used to calculate the change of porosity due to mineral
 precipitation/dissolution:

 Eqn. (7-3)

 where nm is the number of minerals, frm is the volume fraction of mineral m in the rock
 (Vmineral/Vmedium, including porosity), and fru is the volume fraction of nonreactive rock. The
 effective diffusion coefficient is calculated as ∅1/3 𝑆 10/3 × 2 × 10−10 m2 /s where ∅ is porosity and S
 is water saturation, and the pore water diffusion coefficient is to be 2×10-10 m2/s.

 ettringite ((CaO)6Al3(SO4)3∙(H2O)9.68). The porewater provided by SKB is close to equilibrium with all
 the primary minerals⎯slightly oversaturated concerning hydrotalcite and monocarboaluminate and

 quartz (SiO2), and felspar (KAlSi3O8). The initial porewater is practically at equilibrium with gypsum,
 calcite, and quartz, remarkably oversaturated for illite and montmorillonite (Table 7-7.)

November 8, 2024                                                                                                86

 The driving force for the interaction between OPC and MX-80 is diffusion initiated by the
 concentration gradients across the interface as there is no pressure gradient on both sides in
 Subtask A1. Because Cl is only affected by aqueous complexation, it is primarily viewed as a
 conservative element. Its concentration profile (Figure 7-3) is a marker of the diffusion process for
 both OPC and MX-80. This figure shows that the OPC undergoes an increase in Cl concentration and
 decreases in MX-80 after about 1,000 years; the concentrations on both sides are similar. All other
 species are affected by diffusion and chemical reactions, and their profiles different from that of Cl,
 indicating that chemical reactions dominate the temporal-spatial evolution. For example, the C
 concentration profile is shown in Figure 7-3, K, show in Figure 7-4, and Al and Si are in Figure 7-5.
 The K profile is similar to that of Cl, meaning that diffusion significantly shapes its temporal-spatial
 evolution. Some species have a profile controlled by chemical reactions in the early time but
 dominated by diffusion later, such as S, suggesting that chemical reactions occurred early. In MX-80,
 pH increases from the area near the interface and maintains a gradient from the interface to the
 area further away. Similarly, on the OPC side, pH decreases in the interface area initially but
 decreases with time across the entire domain.

 Figure 7-3. The concentration profiles of Cl, pH, C, and S at different times from the model for
 Subtask A1. The interface is located at 0 m.

 Subtask A1. The interface is located at 0 m.

 Figure 7-5. The concentration profiles of Al and Si at different times from the model for
 Subtask A1. The interface is located at 0 m.

 In OPC, as the pH decreases (Figure 7-3), portlandite, C3AH6, monocarboaluminate, and CSH1.6
 dissolve, but the dissolution of portlandite spans larger area whereas the dissolution of C3AH6,
 monocarboaluminate, and CSH1.6 are constrained around 5 cm away from the interface and right
 next the area with dissolution of these minerals, precipitation occurs and extends around 10 cm.
 Because of the dissolution of gypsum in MX-80 and a high initial concentration of S, the S
 concentration in OPC significantly increases (Figure 7-3), causing the precipitation of ettringite
 (Figure 7-6). High Mg concentration in OPC at the vicinity of the interface causes significant
 precipitation of hydrotalcite (Figure 7-6) despite of the decrease in pH.
 At the OPC, the dissolution of primary minerals leads to the precipitation of a few secondary minerals
 (Figure 7-7) including CSH1.2 ((CaO)1.2 (SiO2) ∙ (H2O) 0.06), phillipsite (Na(SiO2)3AlO2.H2O), stratlingite
 (Ca2SiO2Al(OH)7H2O), and brucite (Mg(OH)2).

November 8, 2024                                                                                                89

 Figure 7-8 shows the change in the primary mineral phase in MX-80. Gypsum quickly dissolves
 starting from the interface and is depleted across the entire domain sometime after 1,000 years. In
 the early times, calcite precipitated because of the influx of Ca from the OPC near the interface and
 dissolved farther away. By 100,000 years, spatially, there are two distinct zones: an area of about
 10 cm next to the interface is characterized by slight dissolution/precipitation, and an area far from
 the interface is featured with a large amount of calcite dissolution. The rise of pH in MX-80 leads to
 the dissolution of both montmorillonite and illite; the influx of Al and K from OPC to MX-80 drives the
 precipitation of K-feldspar, but the deprivation of Si causes the dissolution of quartz. This is quite
 different from the model results of bentonite/argillite interaction (Zheng et al., 2015), which showed
 the dissolution of montmorillonite, K-feldspar, and precipitation of illite and quartz, a typical
 illitization process. The difference between current model results and those in Zheng et al. (2015)
 highlights that illitization highly depends on the chemical conditions.

 A1.

 difference between the porosity at a given time and the initial porosity, which is 0.43 for MX-80 and

 porosity at the vicinity of the interface increases as high as 0.65 after 100,000 years due to the
 dissolution of primary minerals (Figure 7-8). On the OPC side, model results showed a monotonic
 decrease in porosity up to 10,000 years. The porosity drops to almost zero at 10,000 years.

 but drops to almost zero at an area about 4 cm away from the interface.

 groundwater has high Na and Cl concentrations, the porewater for OPC in Subtask A3 has higher Na
 and Cl concentrations than those in Subtask A1. Because exchangers are not involved in modeling,
 K concentration in Subtask A3 is lower than in A1. Note also that the pH in Subtask A3 is slightly

 7.3.4.4 Sensitivity to Diffusion Coefficient
 In the base models, the effective diffusion coefficient is calculated as ∅1/3 𝑆 10/3 × 2 × 10−10 m2 /s
 (where ∅ is porosity and S is water saturation) with the pore water diffusion coefficient of 2×10-10
 m2/s. The effective diffusion coefficient in MX-80 ranges from 1.5×10-10 to 1.7×10-10 m2/s, and the
 effective diffusion coefficient of OPC is 0.17×10-10 to 0.96×10-10 m2/s. Since diffusion drives the
 interaction between OPC and MX-80, the diffusion coefficient is presumably one of the most
 important parameters, we therefore conducted a sensitivity analysis, for Subtask A1, A2, and A3
 using a porewater diffusion coefficient of 2 × 10−11 m2 /s which leads to an effective diffusion
 coefficient one order of magnitude lower than the base model presented in previous sections. These
 runs were named as cases A1-D, A2-D, and A3-D, respectively. However, the difference between A1
 and A1-D, A2 and A2-D, and A3 and A3-D are quite similar. Therefore, the discussion in the section
 focuses on comparing A2 and A2-D.
 With lower diffusivity in A2-D, the interaction between OPC and MX-80 is more constrained to the
 area around the interface. Compared with results from A2, a model for A2-D shows that the area with
 high pH in MX-80 is narrower (Figure 7-23), and area with substantial minerals
 dissolution/precipitation is also narrower, for example, montmorillonite dissolution extends only
 30 cm in the bentonite in case A2-D (Figure 7-24) and the precipitation of heulandite (Figure 7-25) is
 limited to the 35 cm next to the interface. On the OPC side, dissolution/precipitation also occurs in
 narrower areas, and exemplified by the dissolution of portlandite (Figure 7-26) and precipitation of
 hydrotalcite (Figure 7-27).

November 8, 2024                                                                                                100

 Figure 7-23. The pH in Subtask A2 (left) and case A2-D (right).

 Figure 7-24. The volume fraction change of montmorillonite in Subtask A2 (left) and case
                    A2-D (right).

 Figure 7-25. The volume fraction change of heulandite in Subtask A2 (left) and case A2-D (right).

November 8, 2024                                                                                           101

 Figure 7-26. The volume fraction change of portlandite in Subtask A2 (left) and case A2-D (right).

 Figure 7-27. The volume fraction change of hydrotalcite in Subtask A2 (left) and case A2-D (right).

 Porosity change is the result of mineral precipitation and dissolution. As the mineral alteration
 happens in a narrower area in Case A2-D, the porosity change is also limited to a location close to
 the interface (Figure 7-28).

 Figure 7-28. The porosity in Subtask A2 (left) and case A2-D (right).

 7.4 Model for Subtask B
 The model setup for Subtask B is like that for Subtask A, except the area from 0 to 1 m is LPC. The
 initial pore water for low pH concrete is calculated with minerals in the hydrate assemblage listed in
 Table 7-10, and porewater compositions are given in Table 7-11. As for the case of OPC, the SKB
 and LBNL calculated concentrations of K and Na in the porewater for LPC differ significantly because
 of the different ways of using exchangers by Phreeqc and TOUGHREACT to calculate the initial
 speciation. Like Subtask A, Subtask B is divided into three subtasks to account for the different ways
 of calculating initial porewater composition for LPC:

November 8, 2024                                                                                                102

 • Subtask B1: It is the original Subtask B, in which the initial porewater of LPC is equilibrated with
 pure water, and exchangers are considered.
 • Subtask B2: Similar to subtask B1, the initial porewater of LPC is equilibrated with groundwater,
 and exchangers are considered.
 • Subtask B3: Similar to subtask B1, the initial porewater of LPC is equilibrated with groundwater,
 and exchangers are not considered.

 OPC concrete (Rasmusson, 2022).

 the initial porewater of MX-80.

 The ion concentration difference between LPC and MX-80 drives the diffusive flux across the
 interface. As shown in Table 7-11, LPC has higher concentrations in Al, K, and Si and lower
 concentrations for the rest of the ions. As a result, we see an increase in Cl, carbonate, and S and a
 decrease in pH (Figure 7-29).

 were modeled partly because of the transport of these ions to MX-80 and partly because of the
 precipitation minerals. Ca, Mg, and Na concentrations increased in LPC (Figure 7-30).

 7.5 Summary and Future Work
 The mineral phase alteration around the interface area may significantly change the porosity and
 permeability, profoundly impacting the long-term migration of radionuclides through a bentonite
 buffer. Concrete has been proposed as backfill material; cementitious materials are used as tunnel
 support (particularly for argillite host rock), mechanical plugs, backfill materials, buffer materials,
 and waste forms. The geochemical alteration at the interfacial area between bentonite and concrete
 must be better understood and modeled. In Task 12 of the SKB EBS task force (Birkholzer et al.,
 2023), modeling teams collaboratively simulate long-term reactions occurring at cement-bentonite
 interfacial regions concerning mineralogical, chemical, or textural changes. Task 12 was established
 in FY21 and has four modeling teams: SKB, SURAO, LBNL, and SNL. Task 12 was divided into three
 subtasks: Subtask A simulates the interaction between the OPC under fully saturated conditions;
 Subtask B simulates the interaction between LPC and MX-80 under different initial saturation
 conditions. Reactive transport modeling for the concrete/bentonite interaction is complex, and
 benchmarking is challenging; many aspects of the model development include database, kinetic
 reaction rate, selection of aqueous complexes, and choice of secondary minerals. While the task
 force tried to harmonize these aspects, each team was allowed to make their choice. Because
 different codes have different handling cation exchanges in the speciation calculation, the initial
 porewater compositions for OPC, MX-80, and LPC, calculated by LNBL, differ from those established
 by SKB (Zheng et al., 2023). Eventually, the group decided to move on with the initial porewater
 composition calculated by SKB, but simulations with different initial porewater were conducted to
 evaluate the effect of initial porewater on the interaction between cement and MX-80: Subtask A was
 divided into A1, A2 and A3 and each has different initial porewater for OPC and subtask B was
 divided into B1, B2 and B3 and they differ in the initial porewater for LPC.
 In FY24, we finished the simulations for Subtasks A1, A2, and A3, and Subtasks B1, B2, and B3.
 Some concluding remarks are as follows:
 In Subtask A, where interaction between MX-80 and OPC occurs, areas with significant mineral
 alteration are limited to 5-10 cm next to the interface. On the OPC side, porosity drops to almost zero
 in the area 3-5 cm away from the interface; on the MX-80, porosity increases to as high as 0.7, about
 62% higher than the initial porosity of 0.43.
 In Subtask A, mineralogical changes in OPC are characterized by the dissolution of portlandite,
 C3AH6, monocarboaluminate, and CSH1.6 and the precipitation of ettringite, hydrotalcite and the
 appearance of CSH1.2, phillipsite, stratlingite and brucite; Mineralogical changes in MX-80 include
 the dissolution of gypsum, calcite, montmorillonite, illite, and quartz and the precipitation of K-
 feldspar, clinochlore, muscovite, analcime, heulandite-Ca, heulandite-Na, kaolinite and saponite.
 Modeling results for Subtask A are not sensitive to the initial porewater composition. In the long run,
 Subtasks A1, A2, and A3 lead to similar results.
 For subtask A, model results are sensitive to diffusion coefficient, a lower diffusion coefficient leads
 to a narrower area with significant alteration of minerals phases.
 For interaction between MX-80 and LPC in Subtask B, areas with significant mineral alteration are
 limited to 5-10 cm next to the interface. OPC experienced a substantial drop of porosity (to almost
 zero) in the area 1 cm away from the interface but an increase in porosity in an area further away
 from the interface; MX-80 has the most significant porosity of 0.47 right next to the interface, only
 9% higher than the initial porosity of 0.43.
 In subtask B, mineralogical changes in OPC are characterized by the dissolution of dissolution of
 SiO2(am), CSH0.8, katoite, and precipitation of hydrotalcite and thaumasite. Ettringite precipitates
 until 10,000 years but then dissolves in the area about 10 cm away from the interface. Mineralogical
 changes in MX-80 include the dissolution of gypsum, calcite, montmorillonite, illite, and quartz and

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  113

 the precipitation of K-feldspar, clinochlore, muscovite, analcime, heulandite-Ca, heulandite-Na,
 Kaolinite and saponite.
 Model results for Subtask B are not sensitive to the initial porewater composition. Subtasks B1, B2,
 and B3 lead to similar results in the long run.
 In the remaining months of FY24 and FY25, we will continue the modeling work on Task 12.
 Specifically, we will
 • Organize a group meeting to compare the results of Task 12 participating teams, pinpoint the
 reason for the difference, and establish a ‘standard’ practice for reactive transport modeling of
 bentonite.
 • Finish Subtask C.
 • Conduct sensitivity analyses of critical model parameters.

November 8, 2024                                                                                              114

November 8, 2024                                     115

 8 UNDERSTANDING THE THMC EVOLUTION OF BENTONITE USING
 MODELING OF THE LARGE-SCALE FIELD EXPERIMENT: HOTBENT
 8.1 Introduction
 Bentonite buffer in a deep geological repository will be simultaneously heated from decaying
 radioactive waste and hydrated from the surrounding host rock, triggering complex, and coupled
 THMC processes. Numerical models are commonly applied for the performance assessment of the
 repository. These models should be built upon faithful representation of all relevant processes and
 formulated with parameters that are either calibrated at the scale close to the repository or
 measured at conditions relevant to the repository performance. However, the latter is proven to be
 very difficult because of: (1) the incapability of numerical models to capture all the relevant
 processes, (2) the inaccuracy of measuring those parameters by experimental techniques, and
 (3) the mismatch between what models need and what experiments can measure. Over time,
 researchers keep expanding the modeling capability, for instance, expanding thermo-hydro-chemical
 (THC) (Samper et al., 2008) to THMC (Zheng et al., 2010; Zheng et al., 2015) models, including
 electrostatic potential in reactive transport modeling (Tournassat & Steefel, 2021), and improving
 experimental capability such as studying ion exchange selectivity at the nanoscale level using the
 high-resolution transmission electron microscopy at cryogenic conditions (cryo-TEM) (Whittaker et al.,
 2019). Before we can synergize all the latest modeling capabilities with a thorough understanding of
 the key processes and parameters that can be reliably measured, model prediction based on the
 large-scale field test is probably still the best practice to predict the long-term evolution of a
 bentonite buffer. The FEBEX in-situ tests and the HotBENT field test and the corresponding THMC
 models are great examples of such practice. The FEBEX-DP project that LBNL/DOE joined from 2015
 to 2019 is an example. After the THMC model provided a coherent explanation of THMC data
 collected at the FEBEX in-situ test and the models was employed to explore key THMC processes
 (e.g. Zheng et al., 2020) and make long-term predictions (Zheng & Fernández, 2023).
 While bentonite behavior for a temperature of 100°C has been well-documented based on
 laboratory and field experiments, data is very limited for high temperature (>100°C) conditions
 (e.g., Zheng et al., 2015; Zheng et al., 2017). It is important to understand the behavior of bentonite
 at high temperatures for the disposal of high-level nuclear waste to expand the knowledge base
 regarding the perturbation of bentonite buffer and open the possibility of designing the repository
 with a higher thermal limit. Currently, a high-temperature experiment in a crystalline rock
 environment, called HotBENT, is being conducted under the leadership of NAGRA with several
 international partners (Kober et al., 2023). Such large-scale tests are extremely important for better
 understanding of the bentonite EBS system behavior under high temperature and conditions with
 strong thermal, hydraulic and density gradients. In this section, we give an update on the progress of
 field test and model results for the field test.

 8.2 HotBENT Project
 Bentonite as a buffer material for high-temperature heating and hydration has recently caught great
 attention. A series of modeling studies on the evolution of bentonite buffer under high temperature
 up to 200°C showed that bentonite buffer could sustain high heating temperature considering the
 concern of illitization and the resulting swelling stress change (e.g., Zheng et al., 2015; 2017). These
 studies suggested that the thermal limit for a repository of higher than 100°C (commonly used in
 most countries' design of repositories) might be too conservative. These modeling studies and the
 large-scale tests, for example, the HE-E heating test (Gaus et al., 2014), inspired the idea of
 conducting a large-scale 1:1 field test on bentonite buffer under high temperature (Vomvoris et al.,
 2015). This idea led to the development HotBENT project in 2016.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  116

 After a design phase from 2017 to 2018, the construction preparatory work at the gallery from 2018
 to early 2020 (Figure 8-1), and with roughly one year and a half of effort, the construction of the field
 test was finished in August 2021, and the heating started on September 9, 2021. A model platform
 was also established. LBNL actively participated in the project from the beginning, including
 conducting scoping calculations to support the design (Zheng et al., 2018). In this section, we give
 an update on the development of the HotBENT project. DOE’s participation in this collaborative effort
 is highly beneficial; substantial cost savings would be achieved in the design of a repository if
 HotBENT demonstrates that the maximum temperature of bentonite backfill can be raised without
 drastic performance implications.

 8.2.1 Field Test Updates
 The official HotBENT Project started in 2018 once all agreements with the partner institutions had
 been in place. In addition to DOE and NAGRA, the joint project has three full partner institutions
 (SURAO from the Czech Republic, NWS from the United Kingdom, and NUMO from Japan) and four
 associated institutions (NWMO from Canada, BGR and BGE from Germany, ENRESA from Spain, and
 Obayashi from Japan). After the 9th partner meeting on November 22, 2022, KORAD (Korea
 Radioactive Waste Agency) joined the HotBENT Consortia. The updates in the following sections are
 also up to the 12th partner meeting.
 Figure 8-1 shows the project timeline. At the 9th partner meeting (Kober & Schneeberger, 2022), a
 decision was made to extend Phase 1 to 2027, and H3 and H4 will be dismantled then. There has
 been no change in the project timeline since the 9th partner meeting.
 The field test was constructed in the FEBEX gallery (drift) that starts at the northern entrance of the
 Grimsel Test Site (Figure 8-2). The experiment is built modularly, whereby a module represents a
 heater rested on a bentonite block pedestal and encapsulated by a granular bentonite backfill
 (Figure 8-3). Modules differ in design temperature, bentonite type, experimental duration, and
 whether a liner is used. The two modules deepest in the drift are separated from the others by an
 insulation plug to enable the excavation of part of the experiment with minimal perturbations to the
 remaining modules. The THM evolution of the HotBENT experiment is monitored with ~1,200
 instruments, measuring parameters such as temperature, mechanical pressure, hydraulic pressure,
 water content, saturation, humidity, displacements, gas concentration, and gas pressure.

 Figure 8-1. Planned timeline for HotBENT experiment (Kober et al., 2024).

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   117

 Figure 8-2. Location of the FEBEX Drift at the Grimsel Test Site (Kober et al., 2023).

 Figure 8-3. Final HotBENT design with individual modules and key construction milestones (Kober
                     & Schneeberger, 2023).

 The heating started on September 9, 2021, shortly after the second retaining wall was constructed.
 The plan was to increase the temperature stepwise to reach the target temperature in 4.5 months.
 However, due to the poor accessibility of the test site in winter, the heating was conducted at a
 slower pace with close monitoring at the site. Eventually, the heating was ramped up with a scheme,
 as shown in Figure 8-4. All heaters have reached their respective target temperature in May 2022:
 H2, H3, and H4 reached the target temperature on May 13, 2022, and H1 on May 29, 2022.
 Sensors continued producing data while the heating ramped up to the targeted temperature. The
 site variables monitored included temperature, pore pressure, RH, water content, total pressure,
 thermal conductivity, heater displacement, plug displacement, and oxygen. A sensor layout around
 Heater 1 is shown in Figure 8-5, 447 temperature sensors were installed, and 436 (98%) were still
 functioning at the 9th Partner Meeting (Kober & Schneeberger, 2022). An example of temperature
 measurement is given in Figure 8-6, 48 thermal conductivity sensors were installed, and 42 (87.5%)
 were still functioning. Figure 8-7 shows Wyoming-type and BCV bentonite thermal conductivity
 measurements.

 hydrological behavior. Unfortunately, all the sensors were installed near the tunnel wall, and it is

 8.2.2 Modeling Platform
 NAGRA and partners have initiated the HotBENT Modeling Platform, and the first kickoff meeting was
 held in 2020, followed by the second meeting in January 2022. Using this modeling platform, groups
 associated with individual partner organizations work on predictive and interpretative test data
 modeling. The participants and structure of the modeling platform are given in Figure 8-10.

November 8, 2024                                                                                            122

 Figure 8-10. Structure of HotBENT modeling platform (Kober et al., 2022)

 The preliminary tasks are listed in Table 8-1. The multi-institutional group was structured (Figure
 8-10) to: (a) increase the support provided to the HotBENT experiment and its interpretative
 evaluation, (b) broaden questions being addressed to advance fundamental understanding, and
 (c) address modeling issues, specifically regarding the quantification of conceptual uncertainties. All
 partners encourage the modeling platform to pursue a broad spectrum of alternative conceptual
 models that address a common question or support a decision to be made jointly by the HotBENT
 participants. In addition, specific expertise and skills of the modeling groups can be used to examine
 aspects raised by the data collected as part of the HotBENT experiment or to make predictions.
 SFWD scientists from LBNL and SNL are participating in the modeling platform. LBNL’s modeling
 plan is to start with a 2D TH model and gradually increase the level of complexity until a 3D coupled
 THMC model is developed and tested using experimental data. A preliminary modeling effort will be
 presented in the next section of the report.
 Table 8-1. Description of tasks of the model platform in the HotBENT project.

 In addition to the field test and modeling platform, laboratory programs that mainly target the
 measurements of bentonite properties have also been established. One such effort is the high-
 temperature column tests (see Section 6 in this report), but other laboratory works are certainly
 being encouraged.

 8.3 Model for the Field Test
 The LBNL team is part of the HotBENT modeling platform, and the goal is to develop a coupled THMC
 model for the field test. We started with a 2D TH model for a cross-section (Zheng et al., 2023) and
 then developed the 3D TH model. Later, the 2D TH model was expanded to a THM model. We have
 now finished the 2D TH and THM and the 3D TH model; the 3D THM model is ongoing.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 123

 8.3.1 Model Set Up

       8.3.1.1    2D Model
 The 2D TH model simulating the coupled thermal and hydrological processes in the bentonite of the
 HotBENT test in FY23 was updated to include vapor diffusion and borehole pressurization. In
 addition, the simulation start date was changed to nine months before heating to accurately
 represent the hydration conditions from the surrounding rock before heating. The numerical model
 was developed using TOUGH2 EOS4 for non-isothermal two-phase flow with a vapor pressure
 lowering effect. A cross-section surrounding Heater 1 (target temperature 200°C) was modeled
 considering vertical symmetry about the axis of the gallery. Hydration and heat transfer processes in
 the EBS and the host rock are assumed to be predominantly radial. The model considered the
 backfill of granular bentonite, bentonite block pedestal, and concrete floor. The surrounding granitic
 host rock, including an excavation damage zone (EDZ), was also represented. The numerical grid
 used in the model is shown in Figure 8-11. The mesh extends 10 m in each direction from the
 gallery's center with a total length of 20 m in the vertical direction and 10 m in the horizontal
 direction. The thickness of the bentonite buffer is 0.545 m. The simulation is run for five years,
 including the current heating phase that started in September 2021.

 Figure 8-11. Numerical grid used in the 2D model (y-z cross section).

       8.3.1.2    3D Model
 For the 3D simulation, the 2D model was extended to include both heater 1, heater 2, and plug 1.
 The current analysis is focused on sector 1 of the field test consisting of heaters 1 and 2 and
 backfilled with Wyoming-type bentonite. In addition to the material zones in the 2D model, the 3D
 model considers zones for retaining wall, shotcrete, sand layer, and bentonite wall. The surrounding
 granitic host rock extending 5 m from the west end of the backfilled gallery was also considered in
 the model. The total length of the mesh in the x direction is 20.75 m. The numerical grid used in the
 3D model is shown in Figure 8-12. The grid consists of 149,112 elements and 438,042 connections
 between them. Given the larger mesh requirements in the 3D model, it was developed using
 TOUGH3, including new parallel linear solvers.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                           124

 8.3.1.3 Thermal Model
 Modeling included heat flow by both conduction and convection. The key parameter for heat
 distribution and evolution is thermal conductivity. Effective dry and wet thermal conductivities were
 specified, and the following relationship implemented in TOUGH2 (Pruess et al., 1999) was used to
 obtain the thermal conductivity (λ) as a function of liquid saturation (Sl):
 𝜆 = 𝜆𝑑𝑟𝑦 + 𝑆𝑙 (𝜆𝑤𝑒𝑡 − 𝜆𝑑𝑟𝑦 ) Eqn. (8-1)

 where λdry is the thermal conductivity under dry conditions, and λwet is the thermal conductivity at fully
 saturated conditions.

 Figure 8-12. Numerical grid used in the 3D model.

 8.3.1.4 Hydraulic Model

 engineered components. The following unsaturated hydraulic conductivity curves (Corey, 1954) were
 used to define the relative permeability of the material zones in the EBS:

 (van Genuchten, 1980), as shown below:

November 8, 2024                                                                                                       125

 8.3.1.5 Mechanical Model
 The swelling and shrinkage behavior of bentonite resulting from coupled heat and fluid flow was
 modeled using the state surface approach presented by Lloret and Alonso (1995), as shown below.
                      𝑒 = 𝐴 + 𝐵𝑙𝑛𝜎 ′ + 𝐶𝑙𝑛(𝜓 + 𝑝𝑎 ) + 𝐷𝑙𝑛𝜎 ′ 𝑙𝑛(𝜓 + 𝑝𝑎 )                           Eqn. (8-4)

 where e is the void ratio, σ’ is the mean effective stress [Pa], pa is the atmospheric pressure [Pa], ψ
 is the suction [Pa], and A, B, C, and D are empirical constants. In this study, the coefficients for
 granular bentonite mixture were A = 1.09, B = -0.055, C = -0.041, and D = 0.005, whereas for
       bentonite blocks A = 0.9, B = -0.055, C = -0.061, D = 0.005. Nguyen et al. (2005) adopted the state
 surface equation to propose an incrementally non-linear poroelastic approach where the coefficients
 of the governing equations are assumed to be functions of suction and the void ratio. The state-
 surface approach has been successfully used in previous studies to model the swelling
 characteristics of bentonite in FEBEX in-situ and mock-up tests (Nguyen et al., 2005; Zheng &
 Samper, 2008). The material properties for the numerical model were obtained from the NAGRA
 database and studies on FEBEX in-situ test modeling (Gens et al., 1998; Seiphoori, 2015; Zheng et
 al., 2020; 2022) and are summarized in Table 8-2. The properties were assumed to be independent
 of temperature.
 Table 8-2. Thermal and hydrodynamic material properties.

 8.3.1.6 Boundary and Initial Conditions
 A time-dependent Dirichlet temperature boundary condition is prescribed to the heater where the
 temperature was increased from 13°C to 200°C in sequential steps. The temperature at far field
 boundaries was maintained at 13°C. Hydrostatic pressure at the top and bottom boundaries was
 prescribed as 0.5 MPa and 0.69 MPa, respectively. No-flow boundary conditions were maintained at
 the two vertical faces. The half-space model also simulated two enhanced geosphere pressurization
 boreholes at a 1 m distance from the gallery wall. The borehole pressure was increased stepwise
 from atmospheric levels to 300 and 700 kPa using a time-dependent Dirichlet boundary condition.
 The increase in borehole pressure with time is shown in Figure 8-13. The initial pressure distribution
 in the host rock was obtained by simulating the gallery excavation with Dirichlet atmospheric

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 126

 pressure boundary conditions within the gallery. The simulation was run for 10 years to allow
 pressure near the gallery to reach hydraulic equilibrium conditions. The initial domain temperature
 was prescribed as 13°C, and the initial pressure of engineered components was given as
 0.101 MPa. The THM model assumed an isotropic initial stress of 28 MPa. The initial saturation of
 granulated bentonite and bentonite blocks was calculated based on dry density and water content
 measurements. The granite host rock was assumed to be fully saturated. The initial saturation of
 each engineered material component is given in Table 8-3.

 8.3.2 2D TH Model Results
 The hydrodynamic effects in the EBS resulting from simultaneous heating and hydration are
 simulated in the numerical model. Field test data for temperature, RH, and pore pressure for the EBS
 sections have been made available by NAGRA to its partners. The temperature sensors are placed at
 different radial distances and angles from the vertical axis. As only a half-space is simulated in the
 model, sensor data on either side of the vertical axis are compared with a representative element in
 the half-space. The time-series results for temperature during the 5-year simulated period at
 different radial distances from Heater 1 (target temperature 200°C) are shown in Figure 8-14. The
 modeled 2D cross-section represents section 4 of the EBS. The dotted lines represent field data,
 whereas the solid lines represent simulated results. The notation of each data series includes a two-
 digit numeric figure followed by a three-digit figure, which refers to the sensor number and its
 placement angle, respectively. For example, Exp03_024 will represent field data measured by
 sensor 3, which was placed at an angle of 24 degrees from the vertical axis. GBM refers to
 granulated bentonite material, whereas BB refers to bentonite blocks.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                127

 Figure 8-14a shows the temperature evolution at the heater surface. The simulation period starts
 nine months before the heating phase. The heating is conducted incrementally until the target
 temperature of 200°C is reached. Some variation in heater temperature is observed in the field
 measurements. The temperature reported by sensor 12 is slightly lower as the sensor was located
 slightly below the heater within the pedestal. A stepwise increase in temperature corresponding to
 the heating input at Heater 1 can be observed at different radial distances. At locations closer to the
 heater (up to a radial distance of ~0.85 m), the temperature is steady after approximately 600 days.
 However, the temperatures are still observed to increase at the outer boundary of the bentonite
 backfill. With the updated values for thermal conductivity based on field measurements, the
 simulated results follow the trends in field data. Although the simulated results closely predict the
 GBM behavior, the simulated results for bentonite blocks overpredict the temperatures compared to
 field data. It should be noted that the current model only represents a 2D cross section of Heater 1
 and does not account for the heat transfer and losses along the length of the tunnel. The
 temperature evolution in the EDZ and the granite is shown in Figure 8-15. The rock temperature at
 5 m radial distance increased to 30°C after heating for five years.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 128

 Figure 8-14. Time-series temperature results in the buffer: “Exp03_024” are data from Section 4
 and “Num03_024” are model results.

November 8, 2024                                                                                         129

 Figure 8-15. Time-series model results of temperature in the host rock in Section 4.

 The fully saturated surrounding rock is at a higher pressure than the bentonite barrier, leading to
 water flowing inwards to the barrier. In addition, the pressurization from the geosphere boreholes
 aids the hydration from the host rock to the bentonite buffer. Due to the hydration of the barrier, an
 increase in the degree of saturation of its components can be expected. This will be more dominant
 at the boundary between the barrier and the host rock. On the other hand, in the inner boundary
 near the heater, due to the increase in temperature, some degree of drying can be expected in the
 clay buffer. Simulated results for liquid saturation at different radial distances are shown in Figure
 8-16. The initial saturation of the pedestal composed of bentonite blocks is much higher than the
 granular bentonite fill. In this test setup, the hydration process from the host rock starts before the
 heating phase. The relatively dry bentonite saturation remains unchanged before heating, whereas
 the saturation of the pedestal is increased. As heating progresses, liquid saturation is reduced near
 the heater. The bentonite blocks gradually reach full saturation with time, whereas the granular
 bentonite shows a rapid increase in saturation after about 1,400 days. The initial saturation of
 granular bentonite is close to its residual saturation of 0.15, and the saturation occurs slowly
 compared to the bentonite blocks. As the radial distance from the heater increases, the movement
 of the saturation front can be observed where water flow occurs from the clay-rock interface towards
 the center. The flow of water toward the buffer has resulted in an initial desaturation of the EDZ as
 seen in Figure 8-16F.
 The NAGRA database's RH measurements were also compared with the simulated results, as shown
 in Figure 8-17. Unlike the temperature data, data collected for RH shows significant variability.
 Sometimes, the sensors in this sector have interrupted data records or discontinued operations.
 Closer to the heater, an initial increase in RH is observed, followed by a decrease when heating
 begins. As the temperature reaches equilibrium, the RH is observed to increase again. At distances
 closer to the rock interface, the RH in granular bentonite gradually increases with time, reaching a
 value close to 100%. Interrupted data records from the field test make it challenging to compare the
 predictions and the measured data accurately. It is expected that improved data from other sectors
 of the test site will assist with better calibration of the numerical model.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 130

 Figure 8-16. Time series model results of liquid saturation in Section 4.

November 8, 2024                                                                   131

 Figure 8-17. Time series results of RH, “Exp09_180” are data from Section 4 and “Num09_180”
 are model results.

 The results for pore water pressure with time are shown in Figure 8-18. The increase in pore water
 pressure follows a similar trend to temperature. At locations closer to the heater, pore water
 pressure increases with temperature, followed by a further increase as time progresses. The
 increase in pressure in bentonite blocks is higher in comparison to the granular bentonite after five
 years. However, the pressure in granular bentonite is still increasing at the end of the simulation
 period. The latter increase in pore water pressure can be explained by the saturation increase
 observed in granular bentonite, as shown in Figure 8-16. At radial distances closer to the rock
 interface, the pore water pressure in granular bentonite gradually increases with time. Within the
 EDZ, the pore water pressure increases after about 1,150 days. This corresponds with the time when
 the temperature in this region starts to increase above 50°C.

 Time series results for RH in different sectors are shown in Figure 8-24 through Figure 8-28. As

 Figure 8-29 through Figure 8-33 show the results for pore water pressure with time. As seen in the
 2D TH model, the increase in pore water pressure follows a similar trend to that of temperature. In
 S04 (center of heater 1) and S08 (center of heater 2), the pore water pressure in bentonite blocks
 shows similar behavior with time, where higher peak pressures are observed closer to the heater.
 The peak pore water pressure is higher in S04 as the temperature in heater 1 is higher than that of
 heater 2 (200°C vs 175°C). In comparison, the peak pressures observed in granular bentonite are
 significantly lower. This is due to the low initial saturation in the granular fill. As the radial distance
 increases, a slight increase in pore water pressure can be observed as the fill gets gradually
 saturated due to inward flow from the host rock. Like the observations made in bentonite blocks, the
 peak pressures are lower in S08 due to the low peak temperature of heater 2. Within the granular
 bentonite fill in S02, S06, and S10, the pore water pressure is comparatively lower owing to the low
 initial saturation but is observed to increase gradually with time. Furthermore, the pressures are also
 observed to increase as the radial distance from the center increases. Field measurements of pore
 water pressure also show some variability and discontinuities.
