 8.3.4 2D THM Model Results
 The 2D THM model was developed by extending the TH model to include mechanical effects. The
 model was created using the TOUGH-FLAC simulator, which links the TOUGH2 multiphase flow and
 heat transport simulator (Pruess et al., 2012) with the FLAC 3D geomechanical code (Itasca, 2017).
 The numerical grid used in the model represents a cross-section surrounding Heater 1 (target
 temperature 200°C), as shown in Figure 8-11. Results for the evolution of temperature at the heater
 surface and within the bentonite buffer at radial distances 0.53 m, 0.67 m, 0.8 m, 0.95 m, and
 1.14 m from the center are shown in Figure 8-34. The peak temperature at the heater surface is
 200°C as introduced by the time-dependent boundary condition. The simulated results are like
 those from the 2D TH model, with more stable equilibrium temperatures observed in the THM model.
 However, the simulated results for the bentonite blocks still overpredict the field measurements in
 the 2D THM model. The temperature evolution in the EDZ and the granite is shown in Figure 8-35.
 The rock temperature at 5 m radial distance increased to 25°C after heating for five years.

November 8, 2024                                                                                             142

 Figure 8-34. Time series of temperature in the buffer ⎯ Data from Section 4 and 2D THM model.

November 8, 2024                                                                                       143

 Figure 8-35. Time series model results of temperature in the host in Section 4 ⎯ 2D THM model.

 Simulated results for liquid saturation are shown in Figure 8-36. As hydration from the host rocks
 starts before heating, an initial increase in liquid saturation is observed, which is more significant in
 the bentonite blocks and granular bentonite closer to the rock interface. The initial saturation of
 granular bentonite is very close to its residual saturation of 0.15, and hydration occurs slowly at
 locations closer to the heater compared to bentonite blocks with a higher initial saturation. When the
 temperature reaches a peak equilibrium value, a reduction in saturation can be observed. Compared
 to the 2D TH model, the reduction in saturation in bentonite blocks is more pronounced in the THM
 model, as observed in Figure 8-36A-C. The bentonite blocks reach full saturation at the end of the
 simulation period. The liquid saturation in granular bentonite at the rock interface reaches a
 saturation level of about 0.94 at the end of the simulation period. This saturation decreases as you
 move towards the heater, where the granular bentonite close to the heater remains relatively dry.
 Figure 8-37 shows the evolution of RH within the bentonite buffer. At locations closer to the heater,
 an initial increase in RH is observed, which is more pronounced in the bentonite blocks. As heating
 progresses, the RH decreases in granular bentonite and bentonite blocks. Following a trend
 observed in liquid saturation, the decrease in RH in bentonite blocks is more prominent in the THM
 model. As the temperature reaches a steady state, the RH of bentonite is observed to increase.
 Having lower equilibrium temperatures than those closer to the heater surface, the RH of the
 granular bentonite starts to expand at a higher rate at distances closer to the rock interface. At the
 interface between the bentonite and the host rock, the RH of granular bentonite shows an overall
 increase and reaches a value close to 100% by the end of the simulation period.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   144

 Figure 8-36. Comparison of time series results for liquid saturation in Section 4 ⎯ 2D THM vs TH
 model.

November 8, 2024                                                                                          145

 Figure 8-37. Comparison of time series results for RH (Data from Section 4) ⎯ 2D THM vs 2D TH
 model.

 The evolution of pore water pressure within the buffer is shown in Figure 8-38. Closer to the heater,
 the pore water pressure in bentonite blocks increases with heating and reaches a value of about
 1.8 MPa. Beyond this point, this thermally induced excess pore water pressure is observed to
 dissipate, followed by another increase. This decrease follows the observed reduction in RH as the
 clay undergoes drying near the heater due to high temperature. The maximum pore water pressure
 in bentonite blocks is observed to decrease as the radial distance from the heater increases. This
 behavior corresponds with the lower equilibrium temperatures observed with increasing radial
 distance from the heater. The increase in pore water pressure in the granular bentonite is lower than
 in the bentonite blocks, which can be explained by the low initial saturation of the granular
 bentonite. In contrast to the bentonite blocks, the pore water pressure in the granular bentonite
 increases as the radial distance from the heater increases. Pore-water pressure within the granular
 bentonite increases gradually with time, showing the effects of both hydration and temperature. Both
 hydration and temperature contribute to the generation of excess pore water pressure within the
 partially saturation bentonite backfill. In the bentonite blocks with higher initial saturation, the
 excess pore water pressure generated is predominantly thermally induced, whereas it is more
 hydration induced in the granular bentonite with low initial saturation.

November 8, 2024                                                                                               146

 Figure 8-38. Comparison of time series results of pore water pressure (Data from Section 4) ⎯ 2D
 THM vs TH model.

 Figure 8-39 shows the evolution of porosity. Closer to the heater, an increase in porosity is observed
 initially in bentonite blocks, which then reduces as heating progresses. After about two years, the
 porosity of bentonite blocks starts to increase again. This trend closely follows the changes in

November 8, 2024                                                                                                147

 saturation that the bentonite blocks undergo, swelling as they saturate and shrinking as they dry.
 Bentonite undergoes contraction due to drying and high suction near the heater. The effect of drying
 is observed to be more significant for granular bentonite, where the porosity shows an initial
 decrease. This can be explained by the low initial saturation of granular bentonite, which is close to
 its residual saturation as opposed to the high initial saturation of bentonite blocks. Figure 8-36
 shows that hydration occurs slowly within the granular bentonite closer to the heater. However, as
 time progresses, granular bentonite can be observed to start swelling with the infiltration of water.
 Near the interface between bentonite and the host rock, initial swelling is observed in granular
 bentonite, which decreases once heating commences. As a result of the combined hydration and
 heating processes, the porosity distribution within the bentonite buffer at the end of the simulation
 period is non-uniform, with the porosity ranging between 0.43 near the heater to 0.45 near the rock
 interface for granular bentonite. This indicates that the density distribution within the buffer would
 also be non-uniform after being subjected to coupled effects of heating and hydration. This
 heterogeneity within the buffer can impact the fluid flow and radionuclide migration. It should be
 noted that plastic deformation of bentonite was not considered in the numerical model presented in
 this study.

 Figure 8-39. Time series result of porosity ⎯ 2D THM model.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                148

 8.4 Summary and Future Work
 Understanding the behavior of bentonite at high temperatures for the disposal of high-level nuclear
 waste is important for expanding the knowledge base regarding the perturbation of bentonite buffer
 and opening the possibility of designing a repository with a higher thermal limit. Currently, a high-
 temperature experiment in a crystalline rock environment called HotBENT is being conducted under
 the leadership of NAGRA with several international partners, including DOE. The HotBENT Project
 continued to progress in FY23 and FY24: all the heaters have reached their respective target
 temperatures, and the heating phase continues. Data is collected through the extensive sensor
 network, and data from different sectors are shared with the partners. LBNL actively participates in
 the HotBENT Modeling Platform, and the preliminary 2D TH model has been extended to a 3D TH
 model and a 2D THM model simulating the field test. The 3D TH model predictions match the trends
 observed in thermal and hydrodynamic behavior in the EBS. However, the current model
 underpredicts the temperature results in sectors surrounding the heaters with bentonite fill. The 2D
 THM model better captures the hydration behavior within the buffer, incorporating updated porosity
 as introduced by the mechanical model.
 In the remaining months of FY24 and FY25, LBNL will gradually extend the 3D TH model to a 3D THM
 model and include chemical effects. In addition, to address the discrepancies with temperature and
 saturation predictions, efforts will be made to investigate the impact of different thermal conductivity
 functions (e.g., Lu & Dong, 2015), Soil Water Retention Curves (SWRC)s, and mechanical constitutive
 models on the thermo-hydro-mechanical behavior of the EBS. The modeling efforts are expected to
 enhance our understanding and provide valuable information regarding the coupled THMC behavior
 of an EBS.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  149

November 8, 2024                                     150

 9 HYDROTHERMAL ALTERATION OF EBS INTERFACES
 9.1 Background
 9.1.1 EBS Concepts in Crystalline Rock
 The benefits of siting an underground spent fuel repository in a crystalline host rock include stability
 of excavated tunnels, reducing chemical conditions that are favorable to radionuclide immobilization,
 and low porosity/groundwater fluxes (Andersson et al., 2013). As with any potential host rock,
 however, specific local concerns must also be addressed. Crystalline rock may allow for the
 sustained presence of natural and manmade fractures (Figure 9-1), which can create pathways for
 radionuclide transport away from the repository near-field (Figueiredo et al., 2016; Missana and
 Geckeis, 2006). The long-term efficacy of EBS materials and design are then prerequisite for
 ensuring radionuclide retention for repository function (Mariner et al., 2011).

 Figure 9-1. Possible scenario for bentonite-colloid facilitated transport of radionuclides away from
 a corroded waste package. Waste package breach coincides with fracture formation in
 the bentonite buffer and the formation of bentonite colloids at the buffer-host rock
 interface. Radionuclides sorbed to colloids are transported away from the EBS via
 fractures in the host rock. (Figure from Missana & Geckeis, 2006).

 Several international approaches to protecting repository materials, specifically at 100 °C upper
 limit, would be achieved by placing design constraints on spacing and packing of spent fuel tailored
 to the properties of the local host rocks (SKB, 2011; Sellin & Leupin, 2013; Kukkonen et al., 2011;
 Oy, 2012), since alteration of EBS materials is expected to be either minimal or inhibited by slow
 reaction rates below 100 °C (Zhou et al., 2021a; Zhou et al., 2021b). Potential alteration in
 crystalline rock repository concepts up to 100 °C have hence been widely evaluated in laboratory
 and full-scale experiments. The Grimsel Test Site in Switzerland specifically provides a platform for
 multinational exploration of repository concepts in a generic crystalline host rock underground
 research laboratory (URL). Additionally, Sweden and Finland have each submitted license
 applications for repository operations in crystalline (granitic) rock including temperature limits for
 buffer materials of 100 °C (Sellin & Leupin, 2013; Hedin & Olsson, 2016) and have completed

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  151

 extensive experimental programs to characterize barrier material stability in the proposed repository
 locations.
 In previous experiments performed on heated bentonite blocks at temperatures between 60 and
 85 °C at the Äspö Hard Rock Laboratory in Sweden, cation exchange was observed in reacted
 smectites after eight years of reaction time, but no changes to the clay mineral structures were
 observed (Dohrmann & Kaufhold, 2012). In-situ heater tests also conducted at the Äspö Hard Rock
 Laboratory in Sweden characterized bentonite alteration in contact with heat sources in a crystalline
 rock environment. Initial tests heated different types of water-saturated bentonite blocks in contact
 with a heater maintained at 130 °C for 18 months. A final heater test conducted for 4.4 years
 experienced a maximum temperature (at the heater surface) of 250 °C. The results showed a loss of
 Si and increase in Mg with increasing temperature in the bentonite, as well as a potential slight
 increase in cation exchange capacity in the regions that experienced the highest temperatures
 (Olsson & Karnland, 2011). In Switzerland, the FEBEX heater test that was recently completed at the
 Grimsel Test Site was an 18-year long full-scale EBS demonstration comprising a heater that
 maintained a temperature of 100 °C at a dummy canister surface. The results of the FEBEX
 experiment showed smectite recrystallization to saponite and chlorite, an accumulation of Mg near
 the heater, and decrease in clay mineral cation exchange capacity and surface area. Clay alteration
 was restricted to areas directly adjacent to the heater surface (within ~10 cm) (A. M. Fernández et
       al., 2018).

 9.1.2 Cement components in EBS
 Cementitious materials are a critical structural component of repository designs and the cement
 composition for use in any given repository setting is an area of active investigation. Ordinary
 Portland cement (OPC) and other traditional cements with a high portlandite content, however, can
 significantly increase the pH of infiltrating fluids due to the equilibration of pore water with
 portlandite through the following generalized reaction:
                                  Ca(OH)2 (Por) → Ca2+ + 2OH-                                     Eqn. (9-1)

 A pH of 12.6 is a critical value for detrimental changes of smectite. At this value, migration of
 calcium from the cement to the clay via ion exchange may lead to changes in the microstructure of
 the clay (Pusch et al., 2003). Cement-bentonite interaction is observed to be a non-linear system
 that involves multiple coupled processes that occur simultaneously and affect each other (Savage et
 al., 2010). These processes include transport of hydroxide and cations into bentonite,
 montmorillonite ion exchange and dissolution, precipitation of secondary phases, dissolution of
 accessory minerals, and changes in porosity and swelling properties (Takase, 2004; Savage et al.,
 2010).
 Cement-bentonite interaction studies for repository research have been performed as part of the
 URL at the Grimsel Test Site (Martin et al., 2006). Results of 13 years of cement-bentonite
 interaction at the FEBEX demonstration are reported in Alonso et al. (2017) and Fernández et al.
 (2017). Spacing from the heater (maximum temperature 100 ºC) in the FEBEX demonstration
 resulted in an observed maximum temperature of 28 ºC in the concrete liner (Martínez et al., 2016).
 Both the shotcrete plug and bentonite experienced alteration due to groundwater interaction (Alonso
 et al., 2017). Portlandite dissolution occurred at the host rock-concrete interface. CSH phases in the
 concrete were altered due to the incorporation of Al, S, and Mg. At the bentonite-concrete interface,
 the main alteration mineral observed was ettringite (Ca6Al2(SO4)3(OH)·26 H2O), indicating that the
 breakdown of sulfur-rich phases in the bentonite, concrete, and/or sulfur sourced from the
 groundwater resulted in mineral precipitation (Alonso et al., 2017). In the bentonite, alteration was
 mostly limited to the immediate interface zone, mostly in the form of precipitated magnesium-rich
 phases and the change in exchangeable cations in the bentonite (Fernández et al., 2017). These

November 8, 2024                                                                                                152

 results highlight the potential for alteration at the cement-bentonite interface at ambient
 temperatures in a realistic repository scenario.
 The potential for significant and wide-ranging geochemical and mineralogical effects of bentonite-
 cement interaction has led repository programs to implement a pH limit for cement porewater
 (e.g., pH < 11). The pH limit is achievable using low-pH cements that replace traditional cement
 components (e.g., limestone aggregate) with siliceous materials (e.g., fly ash, non-pozzolanic silica
 flour, blast furnace slag, and/or silica fume) resulting in a reduced abundance of portlandite in the
 cured product and a lower Ca/Si ratio in the CSH minerals (Calvo et al., 2010; Savage & Benbow,
 2007; Lothenbach et al., 2011). The effect of hydrothermal conditions on cured low-pH cement is an
 area of active study. Recent experimental studies evaluating a concrete composition expected to
 have lower porewater pH and including talc as a superplasticizer to negate the need for organic
 components showed that hydrothermal conditions improved the sealing properties of the concrete,
 specifically by increasing strength (Mohammed et al., 2015; 2016) and decreasing hydraulic
 conductivity (Mohammed et al., 2016).
 Co-alteration studies of low-pH cement with bentonite have largely been conducted at or near
 ambient conditions. Pusch et al. (2003) performed an experiment involving low-pH cement (slag
 cement Merit 5000) with Friedland Ton (70% montmorillonite, 30% muscovite). From XRD data, they
 observed only slight dissolution of the clay minerals; paired with minor changes of potassium
 concentration in the experimental fluid, they concluded that illitization of the bentonite was
 insignificant. González-Santamaría et al. (2018) conducted a small-scale laboratory experiment
 using distilled water, low-pH cement (60 wt.% OPC + 40 wt.% silica fume), and FEBEX bentonite
 (25 °C). At the cement-bentonite and cement-water interfaces, the cement experienced
 decalcification. Magnesium enrichment in the bentonite at the cement-bentonite interface was also
 reported. A subsequent laboratory-scale study from González-Santamaría et al. (2020) examined the
 interface of low-pH cement and FEBEX bentonite in a system with Grimsel Test Site groundwater.
 They reported that alteration in the dilute crystalline groundwater solution resulted in the
 precipitation of Mg-hydroxides and silicates forming within 100 μm of the cement.
 One type of low-pH cement, ESDRED (Engineering Studies and Demonstrations of Repository
 Designs) shotcrete cement, was especially designed for repository applications. The cement was
 designed to be compatible with engineered barriers, such as bentonite, by substituting 40% of the
 cement with silica fume. As a result, fluid in equilibrium with the cement has a pH equal to or below
 11 in accordance with repository program limits. Jenni et al. (2014) and Mäder et al. (2017)
 examined the in-situ interaction between ESDRED cement and Opalinus Clay at the Monti Terri URL.
 A field experiment consisted of two boreholes in the Opalinus Clay filled with three types of cements
 alongside two monitoring boreholes that recorded pore water pressure in the formation. After two
 and five years, respectively, both boreholes featured a de-calcification zone at the ESDRED cement
 interface which was overprinted by a carbonate alteration zone (i.e., calcite precipitation). The
 interface contained a wide magnesium-enriched zone with claystone pore-water as the magnesium
 source. When magnesium migrated into the cement from the host rock, the magnesium reacted with
 alite to form Mg-hydrate. There was a limited extent of reaction within the Opalinus Clay itself.

 9.1.3 Steel Alteration in Geologic Repositories
 Repository designs in crystalline rock rely more specifically on the waste package as a primary
 mechanism to prevent radionuclide release rather than in ductile host rock lithologies, such as clay
 and salt, that provide a measure of self-sealing capacity. The stability of the metal canisters
 designed to contain waste packages is then of substantial importance in a crystalline repository.
 Under dry conditions, bentonite mineralogy will likely be stable (Wersin et al., 2007) and steel
 corrosion will be minimal. Infiltration of groundwater over time, however, is expected to result in in-
 situ reactions including canister corrosion, potential alteration of the bentonite, and reactions

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 153

 between corrosion products and the bentonite (Gaudin et al., 2013; Wilson et al., 2006A; Wilson et
 al., 2006B). This report focuses on a brief review of interactions of metal corrosion products with
 bentonite barrier materials that may impact barrier performance in a crystalline rock environment.

 Formation of an Fe-oxide Layer on Steel
 Design concepts for a crystalline rock repository in the U.S. are still being explored, but the current
 base case in crystalline rock includes stainless and carbon steel (Matteo et al., 2016). Steel will
 corrode at the bentonite-buffer interface in the presence of oxygen according to one of the following
 reactions (aerobic corrosion) (Kursten et al., 2004):
                                    4FeO + 3O2 → 2Fe2O3                                             Eqn. (9-2)

                                 2Fe + 2H2O +O2 → 2Fe(OH)2                                          Eqn. (9-3)

 The resulting oxide film has been shown to act as a protective coating against further corrosion in an
 effect called passivation. The formation of even, porous Fe(OH)2 layers, expected to form in cement-
 influenced alkali solutions in geologic repositories, may still decrease corrosion rates (Kursten et al.,
 2004). In contrast, at lower pH values and/or because of reactions with aggressive aqueous species,
 such as chlorides, the oxide passive films can break down. Previous laboratory studies conducted at
 temperatures of 25 to 100 °C have shown the formations of the Fe-oxide layer at the steel-bentonite
 interface, with a range of different Fe-oxide/hydroxides observed to precipitate on the steel surface
 dependent on experimental conditions. (Alvarez et al., 2008; Bourdelle et al., 2014; Pignatelli et al.,
 2014; Smailos et al., 2003; Kursten et al., 1997). The main corrosion products observed are
 magnetite (Fe3O4) and goethite (Fe(III)O(OH)) in experiments below 100 °C, with hematite (Fe2O3)
 occurring in the experiments at 100 °C.

 9.1.4 Mineral Reactions at Steel-Bentonite Interfaces
 In the event of a canister failure, significant alteration of the bentonite immediately adjacent to the
 breached area (i.e., the steel-bentonite interface) could negatively impact the efficacy of the
 bentonite to arrest the movement of radionuclides. Higher temperatures at the clay/canister
 interface may cause a local increase in iron activity and dissolved SiO2, where the resulting chemical
 gradient causes migration of dissolved species radially away from the canister towards the colder
 rock, potentially leading to the silicification throughout the buffer during the cooling phase (Pusch &
       Madsen, 1995; Svemar, 2005).
 Full-scale demonstrations at URL sites are used to simulate the long timeframes, realistic scales,
 and water/rock and clay/steel ratios applicable to proposed repository designs. Interaction of carbon
 steel and bentonite was examined in a full-scale experiment as part of the 18-year FEBEX full-scale
 EBS heater test at the Grimsel Test Site (Martin et al., 2006). Bentonite in contact with steel likely
 reached temperatures of 30 to 60 °C as part of this experiment. After 18 years of reaction in a
 saturated system, carbon steel corrosion resulted in a less than 140 mm layered zone of iron
 enrichment extending radially into the interfacing bentonite. In the ~30 mm closest to the canister,
 iron was hosted in newly formed goethite (Fe(III)O(OH)) within the bentonite. The remainder of the
 iron-enriched zone is interpreted to be a result of the diffusion and sorption to the clay mineral edge
 site of Fe2+ (Hadi et al., 2019). Substantial alteration of the interfacing bentonite was not observed
 at the relatively low temperatures achieved (Wersin & Kober, 2017; Hadi et al., 2019). The HotBENT
 (High Temperature Effects on Bentonite) experiment is currently being conducted by Nagra in the
 same manner as the FEBEX experiment to evaluate high-temperature processes in a full-scale
 demonstration. The peak temperatures of the HotBENT experiment are expected to reach between
 175 to 200 °C and will run for 15 to 20 years (AMBERG, 2019). The HotBENT experiment is
 discussed in further detail in Chapter 2 in relation to the EBS International work package.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   154

 Lab-scale experiments provide insight into transient reaction pathways, complementary to results
 from long-term, full-scale experiments. Interaction between clay minerals/bentonite with steel/Fe-
 oxide/native Fe has been a subject of extensive characterization at a wide temperature range (25 to
 300 °C: (Cathelineau et al., 2005; Wersin et al., 2007; Mosser-Ruck et al., 2010; Pignatelli et al.,
       2014; Bourdelle et al., 2014; Kaufhold et al., 2015; Cheshire et al., 2018; Lanson et al., 2012;
 Lantenois et al., 2005; Bourdelle et al., 2017). These studies show that steel corrosion has the
 potential to have wide ranging effects on the physical and chemical properties of the bentonite
 buffer that are sensitive to bulk system chemistry. In lower temperature repository settings (<
 100 °C), cation exchange of Fe into clays during steel corrosion may reduce the self-sealing ability in
 the clay (i.e., swelling capacity) (Pusch et al., 2015). Fe3+, generated under initially oxidizing
 conditions in a repository, may replace Al3+ in the octahedral sheet of montmorillonite. This exchange
 can accelerate alteration to non-swelling phases due to high crystal stresses and mineral instability
 due to the large cation size (Nguyen-Thanh et al., 2014). In addition, the combination of heating and
 the ion exchange between the Na2+ in the clay with the Fe2+ produced from corrosion under reducing
 (anoxic) conditions promotes the development of channel-type transport pathways for solutions
 migrating through the bentonite buffer (Gueven & Huang, 1990; Nguyen-Thanh et al., 2014; Pusch
       et al., 2012).
 In > 100 °C water-saturated experimental systems with circumneutral pH values (such as where
 porewaters are buffered by clay and silicate minerals), the presence of Fe2+/3+ may result in the
 formation of Fe-rich phyllosilicate minerals (chlorite, Fe-saponite, berthierine, serpentine phases) in
 bentonite buffers (Guillaume et al., 2003; Mosser-Ruck et al., 2010; Cheshire et al., 2018; Jové
 Colón et al., 2019). Previous work completed at LANL showed that in hydrothermal experiments
 conducted at 300 °C, the surface of steels embedded in bentonite had a zone of corrosion and
 metal oxide phases formed on the steel surfaces, as well as minor pyrrhotite, which was coated with
 a layer of Fe-rich smectite (Fe-saponite) at the interface with bentonite (Figure 9-2; Cheshire et al.,
 2018; Jové Colón et al., 2019). The formation of Fe-rich silicate phases as mantle on the steel
 surface is thought to provide corrosion protection in addition to that provided by passivation layers
 (e.g., Kaufhold et al., 2015; Cheshire et al., 2018). Future results from full-scale/long term
 experiments may additionally be used to confirm and evaluate this potentially protective effect.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 155

 Figure 9-2. Conceptual diagram of high-temperature alteration products at a stainless steel-
 bentonite interface (modified from Cheshire et al., 2018).

 9.2 Methods
 9.2.1 Hydrothermal Experiments
 The set of hydrothermal experiments completed in FY24 were designed to continue reacting a well-
 characterized low-pH cement material (ESD cement) with synthetic Grimsel groundwater (GW) at
 different durations. One additional experiment completed in FY24 was performed to provide a
 baseline OPC-only experiment. The experimental temperatures were selected to range from low-
 temperature hydrothermal conditions to the maximum temperature expected at the heater surface.
 Experiments completed in FY21 and FY22 explored the reactions between Wyoming bentonite and
 different cement types including ordinary Portland cement (OPC) and an experimental low-pH cement
 composition developed at Vanderbilt University in collaboration with SNL (VU cement). In FY23, the
 ESD cement was reacted at three different temperatures for four weeks to explore the effects of
 temperature on cement alteration. An experiment completed in FY21 also characterized the
 alteration of Wyoming bentonite saturated with GW at similar conditions to the OPC experiment. This
 experiment acted as a baseline comparison for Wyoming bentonite hydrothermal alteration in the
 absence of cement. Parameters for all experiments are included in Table 9-1.
 Reactants (listed in Table 9-1) were loaded into a flexible gold reaction cell and fixed into a 500 mL
 gasket confined closure reactor (Seyfried et al., 1987). Reducing conditions were maintained by
 including a redox buffer mixture of 1:1 (by mass) Fe3O4 and Fe0 added at 0.07 wt.% of the total
 mass of solid + liquid reactants. Experiments in FY21 and FY22 reported here (Table 9-1) mixed the
 redox buffer directly with the solid reactants. In the CEM-series experiments, the redox buffer mixture
 was placed in a gold tube lightly crimped to create an enclosure where the redox buffer was in
 aqueous communication with the reactant solution but did not allow a direct Fe-cement interface. All

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 156

 experiments were pressurized to 150 to 160 bar and isothermally heated at 100 to 200 °C for 4 to
 9 weeks, with one experiment. The only experiment that differed in experimental duration was CEM-4
 which was reacted for six months. Temperature and experiment durations for each experiment are
 listed in Table 9-1. Reaction liquids were extracted periodically to investigate the aqueous
 geochemical evolution in relationship to mineralogical alterations. During the first eight experiments,
 fluid was extracted weekly, whereas CEM-0, -4 and -5 were sampled at an accelerated schedule for
 the first week before starting a weekly schedule, or bi-weekly for the six-month experiment. All
 aliquots from each experiment were stored in a refrigerator at 1 °C until analysis. Parameters for all
 experiments are included in Table 9-1. Aliquots of sampled reaction liquids were filtered through a
 0.22 μm syringe filter and analyzed by IC to quantify anions. Additional aliquots, acidified to pH < 1
 immediately after sampling, were used for ICP-OES cation analyses and ICP-MS trace metals
 analysis.
 After experiment termination (referred to here as “quench”), additional solution samples were
 collected directly from the reaction cell. Solid reactants were filtered from the solution, rinsed with
 ethanol to remove excess water and dissolved solutes, and stored in a desiccator until the weight
 stabilized. Reaction products and starting materials were characterized using X-ray diffraction (XRD),
 electron microprobe (EMP(A)), and scanning electron microscopy (SEM). Detailed analytical methods
 are listed in Rock et al. 2024, Appendix A.

 values are mass (g) except where otherwise noted.

 9.2.2 Material

 9.2.2.1 Materials Used in Experiments Completed in FY24
 ESD cement. An ESDRED-like, low-alkali shotcrete product used a formulation developed as part of
 the ESDRED (Engineering Studies and Demonstrations of Repository Designs) project (Mäder et al.,
 2017). The general formula includes ~60% CEM I (cement product having at least 95% ordinary

November 8, 2024                                                                                                           157

 Portland cement) with ~40% microsilica, with variable amounts of a set accelerator (Lothenbach et
 al., 2014). The cement product was characterized by macro-scale heterogeneity between major
 phases (Figure 9-3), consistent with previous characterizations of ESDRED-like low-pH cements used
 in full-scale demonstrations (i.e. Jenni et al., 2014).

 Figure 9-3. SEM-EDS characterization of unreacted ESD cement. a. Backscatter emission (BSE)
 image of ESD cement, showing relative areas of high-calcium (“calcite”) and high-silica
 (SiO2) regions at a mm scale. b. EDS map of calcium abundance. c. EDS map of carbon
 abundance. d. EDS map of Si abundance. e. EDS map of O abundance. Note co-
 occurrence of relative abundances of calcium with carbon, and of silica with oxygen.

 Ordinary Portland cement. Chips consisting of a mix of 100% OPC and Los Alamos municipal tap
 water (Table 9-2) were mixed according to American Petroleum Institute standards and cured for at
 least 28 days. Major mineral phases observed in the XRD pattern of the cured cement chip include
 portlandite (Ca(OH)2), belite (calcio-olivine, Ca2SiO4), alite (Ca3SiO5), and brownmillerite
       (Ca2(Al,Fe)2O5).
 Synthetic Grimsel groundwater. All experiments used a synthetic Grimsel groundwater solution based
 on chemistry reported by Kersting et al. (2012) that describes an average groundwater composition
 at the Grimsel Test Site as a Na-CO3 type water with pH ~ 8.5. The solution was synthesized using
 Milli-Q ultrapure water (18.2 MΩ·cm) and research-grade salts. Target chemistry of the synthetic fluid
 is presented in Table 9-2. All solutions were sparged with He for 30 minutes to remove dissolved
 gasses and pH-adjusted to pH 8.5 with analytical-grade HCl and/or NaOH before each experiment.
 Analyzed solution chemistries for each initial solution are listed with supplementary aqueous
 geochemistry for each experiment in Rock et al., 2024, Tables B1-B22.

 9.2.2.2 Additional Materials Used in Experiments Completed in FY21-23
 Wyoming bentonite. Previous experiments used an unprocessed Wyoming-type bentonite provided by
 Bentonite Performance Minerals LLC from Colony, Wyoming, U.S.A. The bentonite was composed
 dominantly of Na-montmorillonite (general composition: Na0.33(Al,Mg)2(Si4O10)(OH)2·nH2O), lesser
 clinoptilolite and feldspar, and minor biotite, pyrite, quartz, opal, and sulfide minerals. The QXRD
 results from unheated bentonite are presented in Rock et al., 2024, Appendix C.
 Grimsel granodiorite. The granodiorite used in the experiments was sourced from a drill core from
 the Grimsel Test Site. Major mineral phases include K-feldspar, plagioclase, and quartz. Minor
 phases are muscovite and biotite. Trace phases are allanite, zircon, titanite, and apatite. QXRD
 results from unreacted granodiorite are presented in Rock et al., 2024, Appendix C.
 VU cement. An experimental low pH cement product was included as a reactant in EBS-33 and -34
 (Caporuscio et al., 2022). The low-pH cement chips were developed and manufactured at Vanderbilt
 University using Type I/Type II cement powder (LafargeHolcim, USA), 50 wt.% silica fume
 replacement, and 2 wt.% Glenium (super plasticizer). A 0.6 water-to-binder ratio was used. The
 samples were cured for six months at 30 ºC and were at 100% relative humidity at the time of
 sample preparation.
 316SS. 316-solid steel is an iron alloy primarily with 18.37 wt.% Cr, 12.35 wt.% Ni, 2.26 wt.% Mo,
       1.619 wt.% Mn, 0.5093 wt.% Si, and 0.175 wt.% Cu.

 9.3 Results
 9.3.1 Aqueous Geochemistry
 Evolution of pH and of cation and anion concentrations in the reaction fluids are interpreted to
 reflect mineral-solution reactions. Results are grouped by experimental set: experiments that were
 conducted at 250 °C that included Grimsel granodiorite and bentonite ± OPC ± 316SS; two
 experiments that were conducted at 200 °C that reacted VU cement with bentonite ± Grimsel
 granodiorite; and three experiments conducted with ESD cement in solution at three different
 temperatures (100, 150, and 200 °C). All experiments included in this report had initial water:rock
 ratios (WRR) of 9:1 (by mass). As experiments progressed, fluids were gradually extracted from the
 reaction vessel during sampling, which slightly lowered the WRR with every fluid extraction.
 Estimated WRRs were approximately 6:1 at experiment termination.

November 8, 2024                                                                                              159

pH. The synthetic Grimsel granodiorite GW solution had a starting pH of ~8.5. In the baseline experiment IEBS-0, pH
 values initially decreased and after two weeks remained between 5.5 and 6 throughout the experiment duration (

 Figure 9-4). The inclusion of OPC chips in the baseline experiment CEM-0 caused pH values to
 increase and remain stable at about 11.8 immediately after heating until the experiment ended. The
 pH observed in IEBS-6 and -8, which also contained OPC chips, evolved to slightly higher values than
 in IEBS-0 (~7–7.5). In contrast, in experiments IEBS-9 and -10 with VU cement, the pH decreased
 slightly over the experimental duration but remained > pH 8.5. In the CEM-series experiments that
 contained ESD cement, pH increased to > 8.5 within the first week of experiment time. The pH of
 CEM-1 and -2 continued increasing throughout the experimental duration to ~9.6 just before the
 experiment terminated; the pH of samples collected from CEM-3 decreased after the initial increase
 to relative steady state at ~9.0. pH from CEM-4 and -5 increased at a slower rate than the previous
 experiments and achieved a relative steady state at a lower value of 8.5 to 8.9. All pH values
 reported here were measured at 25 °C: calculated in-situ pH values are discussed in Section 9.2.1.

November 8, 2024                                                                                               160

 Figure 9-4. Benchtop pH (25°C) from the IEBS and CEM experiments.

 Silica. Aqueous silica concentrations in all IEBS experiments increased rapidly during the first one to
 two weeks of experiment time (Figure 9-5), approaching apparent steady-state concentrations of
 400 to 600 mg L-1 after the first 1 to 2 weeks of reaction time. However, silica concentrations in the
 OPC-only experiment CEM-0 only increased to a maximum value of 22.3 mg L-1 within a week of
 reaction, then steadily decreased to a final concentration of 2.9 mg L-1. SiO2 concentrations were
 highest in the experiment that reacted only bentonite at 250 °C and generally remained slightly
 higher in the OPC experiments reacted at 250 °C than in the VU cement experiments reacted at 200
 °C. In the first three experiments reacting ESD cement and no bentonite, silica increased to relative
 steady-state concentrations achieved within the first week of experiment time. The final/steady state
 SiO2 concentrations of each CEM experiment increased with increasing experimental temperature,
 from 100 to 200 °C. SiO2 concentrations in both CEM-4 and -5 exhibited a similar initial trend but
 began to decrease again after 863 hours in CEM-4 and 358 hours in CEM-5. SiO2 concentrations in
 CEM-4 eventually achieved a steady-state of about 450 mg L-1 by 2204 hours of reaction.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 161

 Figure 9-5. Concentrations in mg L-1 of aqueous silica from the IEBS and CEM experiments.

 Aluminum. At 250 °C, Al concentrations were elevated in the OPC experiments IEBS-6 and -8
 compared to the bentonite-only experiment IEBS-0 and OPC-only experiment CEM-0. The two
 experiments reacting VU cement at 200 °C did not have elevated Al concentrations, having had an
 initial increase in Al to ~2 mg L-1 in the first week of reaction time before decreasing over the
 remaining experiment duration. Al concentrations in CEM-1 and CEM-2 were below detection limits
 (plotted at 0 mg L-1 on Figure 9-6) and increased slightly in CEM-3 in the first week of experiment
 time to almost 2 mg L-1 before decreasing throughout the experiment remainder (Figure 9-9). Al
 concentrations in CEM-4 remained stable around 1 mg L-1 apart from a sharp increase to 5.7 mg L-1
 after 1869 hours of reaction before returning to its previous concentration. Of the experiments
 containing ESD cement, CEM-5 contained the greatest concentration of Al, reaching a maximum
 concentration of 8.1 mg L-1 after 358 hours of reaction before decreasing for the remainder of the
 experiment duration.

November 8, 2024                                                                                             162

 Figure 9-6. Concentrations in mg L-1 of aluminum from the IEBS and CEM experiments.

 Iron and Magnesium. Iron concentrations in the IEBS and CEM-0 experiments were generally variable
 between the method detection limit and 0.5 mg L-1, though concentrations tended to be highest in
 both the first week of reaction and after 1000 hours of reaction (Figure 9-7). IEBS-9 and -10
 consistently contained less than 0.26 mg L-1 Fe over their experimental durations. Experiments with
 ESD cement exhibited similar trends in Fe concentrations as IEBS-6, -8, -0, and CEM-0, except for a
 large peak at 2.7 mg L-1 in CEM-4 after 1869 hours of reaction. Magnesium concentrations in all
 experiments decreased from the initial concentration in the Grimsel GW solution (> 10 mg L-1) within
 the first week of experiment time and remained near or below the method detection limits for the
 duration of most experiments (Figure 9-8). Similarly to trends in Fe concentrations, Mg
 concentrations in CEM-4 increased to a peak value of 6 mg L-1 after 1896 hours of reaction, then
 returned to near-zero concentrations.

November 8, 2024                                                                                              163

 Figure 9-7. Concentrations in mg L-1 of iron from the IEBS and CEM experiments.

November 8, 2024                                                                           164

 Figure 9-8. Concentrations in mg L-1 of magnesium from the IEBS and CEM experiments.

 Alkali/alkaline earth metals. Concentrations over time of major alkali and alkaline earth metal
 cations (K+, Na+, Ca2+, Mg2+) follow different trends between the three groups of experiments. In the
 experiments completed at 250 °C (IEBS-0, -6, and-8), Ca concentrations initially decreased from the
 concentration of the starting Grimsel granodiorite synthetic GW (~7 mg L-1) before arriving at
 apparent steady-state concentrations of 1 to 2 mg L-1 in each experiment after about two to four
 weeks (Figure 9-9). With only OPC cement, Ca concentrations in CEM-0 continuously increased to
 44 mg L-1 by the termination of the experiment, further increasing to nearly 300 mg L-1 after the
 experiment was cooled and depressurized. In the two experiments reacting VU cement at a lower
 temperature (IEBS-9 and -10), Ca concentrations increased slightly throughout the experimental
 duration without achieving any apparent steady-state concentration, with final concentrations ~5 to
 6 mg L-1. In contrast, Ca concentrations increased in the first three experiments reacting ESD
 cement. In CEM-1 and CEM-2, Ca concentrations increased to > 50 mg L-1 by the end of the
 experiment without indication of reaching any steady-state. After week 2, Ca increased at an
 increasing rate in CEM-1 and Ca concentrations in CEM-2 appear to have increased linearly with
 time. Calcium concentrations in CEM-3 and -4 also increased, though less dramatically;
 concentrations appeared to level off between weeks two and four at ~15 to 20 mg L-1. Ca
 concentrations in CEM-5 exhibited a similar trend to CEM-1 and -2, though achieving a steady-state
 around 25 mg L-1.

November 8, 2024                                                                                               165

 In IEBS-0, -6, and -8, as well as in all experiments with EDS cement, Na behaved conservatively and
 remained similar in magnitude to the concentrations of initial groundwater solution (~116 mg L-1 for
 the IEBS series and ~130 for the CEM series) (Figure 9-10). CEM-4 experienced a gradual decrease
 in Na concentration after 863 hours until Na was below the method detection limit but increased
 again to its previous steady-state concentration (~145 mg L-1). In contrast with the other sets of
 experiments, Na concentrations in IEBS-9 and -10 increased continually over the first two to four
 weeks before coming to a comparatively elevated quasi-steady-state concentration (~300–
       350 mg L-1) after week 4.
 Potassium concentrations in IEBS-6, -8, and -0 behaved conservatively, remaining below 10 mg L-1
 for the duration of the experiments (Figure 9-11). CEM-0, however, contained greatly elevated
 concentrations compared to all other experiments, with an increase in the first week of reaction to
 over 100 mg L-1. K concentrations in IEBS-9 and -10 initially increased by an order of magnitude
 after three weeks of reaction time, then decreased slowly to final concentrations of 10 to 11 mg L-1.
 CEM experiments contained elevated K concentrations compared to all IEBS experiments, which,
 except for CEM-4, increased from groundwater concentrations to over 40 mg L-1 within the first week
 of reaction. Potassium concentrations in CEM-4 remained stable around 20 mg L-1 for the duration of
 the experiment except for an apparent decrease around 1500 hours to 4 mg L-1.

 Figure 9-9. Concentrations in mg L-1 of calcium from the IEBS and CEM experiments.

 Figure 9-11. Concentrations in mg L-1 of potassium from the IEBS and CEM experiments.

 Chloride. Chloride concentrations in the baseline experiment with Wyoming bentonite and Grimsel
 synthetic GW (IEBS-0) generally remained between 10 and 15 mg L-1 (Figure 9-12). In the
 experiments with the OPC, Cl concentrations were initially elevated in IEBS-6 starting around 33 mg
 L-1 and lower in IEBS-8 and CEM-0 (~13 mg L-1) before converging by the end of each experiment to
 values between 15 and 20 mg L-1. Cl concentrations in IEBS-9 and -10 initially increased in the first
 week then remained at an apparent steady-state concentration of ~25 mg L-1. In the CEM- series, Cl
 concentrations initially increased in the first week in all experiments, with greater increases at lower
 temperatures. However, CEM-5, conducted at 200 °C, behaved most similarly with CEM-1,
 conducted at 100 °C, with an additional peak concentration of > 120 mg L-1 after 694 hours of
 reaction.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  168

 Figure 9-12. Concentrations in mg L-1 of chloride from the IEBS and CEM experiments.

 Sulfate. Sulfate concentrations in IEBS-0 initially increase sharply from the first to the second
 sampling, remain between 200 and 300 mg L-1 for the remainder of the experiment, and drop to
 100 mg L-1 on experiment quench (Figure 9-13). SO42- concentrations were less variable in the
 experiments with OPC (IEBS-6 and -8), mostly remaining between 150 and 250 mg L-1 and were
 higher in the two experiments reacting VU cement at 200 °C with final concentrations from ~550 to
 650 mg L-1. The OPC baseline experiment CEM-0 contained gradually decreasing SO42-
 concentrations to a minimum of 40 mg L-1. In all experiments reacting ESD cement except CEM-4,
 SO42- increased sharply in the first week of experiment time and continued increasing at a slower rate
 for the remainder of all experiments. Concentrations were relatively similar in CEM-1, -2, and -3, with
 final concentrations at ~400 mg L-1. In CEM-4, SO42- concentrations slowly increased to a maximum
 value of 391 mg L-1 before achieving a steady-state at 200 mg L-1. SO42- concentrations in CEM-5
 peaked at ~670 mg L-1, then decreased to steady-state concentrations similar to CEM-4.

 9.3.2 QXRD
 Quantitative X-ray diffraction (QXRD) analyses of the starting material mixture and the bulk reaction
 products from IEBS-0, -6, -8, and -10 are presented in Figure 9-14 and Rock et al., 2024, Appendix
 C. The results show changes in bulk mineralogy as the result of the hydrothermal reaction. The
 approximate starting reaction mixture for each experiment is listed in Table 9-1. The bulk mineralogy
 of a representative sample of Wyoming bentonite, Grimsel granodiorite, and the starting mixture is
 reported in Rock et al., 2024, Appendix C.
 IEBS-0. Experiment IEBS-0 was a baseline experiment to explore Wyoming bentonite alteration in the
 synthetic Grimsel GW solution. Observed mineralogy changes include the reduction in feldspar and
 clinoptilolite and an increase in smectite/illite/illite-smectite.
 IEBS-6 and IEBS-8. The inclusion of a cured OPC cement chip with Wyoming bentonite and Grimsel
 granodiorite reactants resulted in different mineralogical changes. The sample analyzed included
 bentonite and granodiorite components; the cement chip was intact and removed from the mixture
 for separate post experiment characterization. The main mineralogical changes in the bentonite
 groundmass included a reduction in clinoptilolite and the formation of analcime and amorphous
 material (Figure 9-14).

November 8, 2024                                                                                               170

 IEBS-9 and IEBS-10. IEBS-9 and -10 were conducted at a lower temperature than the other IEBS
 experiments (200 instead of 250 ºC) and included VU cement with Wyoming bentonite and Grimsel
 GW solution. IEBS-10 additionally included Grimsel granodiorite in the reactants. The major post-
 reaction change observed in the Wyoming bentonite in IEBS-9 was the reduction of the mass percent
 of clay phases coupled with an increase of heulandite-clinoptilolite. In EBS-10, the main
 mineralogical changes include the reduction in mass percent clay and the increase of heulandite-
 clinoptilolite along with the formation of analcime and an amorphous material (Figure 9-14).

 Figure 9-14. QXRD patterns of reacted bentonite and granodiorite from IEBS-0, -6, -8, -9, and -10
 compared to unreacted bentonite and granodiorite. Peaks correspond to smectite
 (Sme), mica, clinoptilolite-heulandite (Cpt-Heu), albite (Ab), corundum (Crn), feldspar
 (Fsp), quartz (Qtz), pyrite (Py), magnetite (Mag), and smectite-feldspar (Sme-Fsp).

 9.3.3 Cement Chip XRD
 OPC. XRD of the OPC indicated the presence of portlandite, calcite, and minor amounts of
 brownmillerite, heulandite, and larnite in the starting mineralogy (Figure 9-15). Portlandite was
 completely absent in the reacted cements from IEBS-6 and -8. Interaction with EBS components (WB
 and GG) produced zeolites, clinoptilolite, and feldspars on the cement. Zeolites were particularly
 abundant after reaction with 316SS in IEBS-8. The XRD scan of CEM-0 (OPC only) had only calcite
 peaks present on the surface of the cement.
 VU Cement. The VU cement initially contained calcite and brownmillerite with minor amounts of a
 calcium aluminum silicate phase, potentially wollastonite (Figure 9-16). A broad hump between 20º
 and 40º 2θ represents the amorphous silica fume replacement. Reacted cement from IEBS-9
 and -10 contained relatively less calcite and brownmillerite but included smectites, derived from the
 Wyoming bentonite included in both experiments. The amorphous signal is reduced or absent from
 the reacted cements.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                171

 ESD Cement. The unreacted ESD cement contained calcium carbonate and silicon oxide (Figure
 9-17). In the five reacted CEM cement, calcium carbonate and silicon dioxide were still present,
 along with the addition of tobermorite.

 (Cpt), portlandite (Por), albite (Ab), calcite (Cal), brownmillerite (Bmlr), heulandite
 (Hul), and larnite (Lrn).

       9.3.4     Clay XRD
 The clay-size fraction (< 2 µm) was separated via density separation in DI water from a crushed
 portion of the reaction products from each experiment. While crushing, discrete particles of Grimsel
 granodiorite were removed. In the case of IEBS-6, -8, -9, and -10, the cement chips were separated
 before the clay sample was selected. The XRD patterns of the ethylene glycol-saturated, oriented clay
 fractions can be used to determine alterations to the clay mineral structure through shifts in the
 peak positions, which were used to calculate clay mineral expandability. The ethylene glycol-
 saturated peak positions are presented in Table 9-3 and the resulting XRD patterns are plotted in
       Figure 9-18.
 Results from IEBS-0 show decreased clay expandability calculated as evidenced by the difference
 between the d002 and d003 peaks from these samples show an increase in clay expansion. In
 comparison, results from IEBS-6 and IEBS-8 through -10, which included OPC and low pH cement
 chips, show slightly lower expandability than the clay fraction from experiments without cement, but
 still slightly increased in comparison to unreacted Wyoming bentonite. However, differences of less
 than several percent are not considered statistically significant given the natural variability of the
 Wyoming bentonite mineral assemblage.

 9.3.5 XRF
 XRF analyses for bulk rock oxide chemistry were performed on the unreacted starting material and
 the bulk reaction products (Table 9-4). Fe-oxides increased in all samples relative to the initial
 Wyoming bentonite due to the addition of iron buffers (Table 9-1).
 In the Wyoming bentonite control experiment (IEBS-0), the bulk chemistry of the reaction products
 was generally unchanged from the composition of the unreacted bentonite. The Wyoming bentonite
 + Grimsel granodiorite + cured OPC chip experiments (IEBS-6 and -8) were identical except for the
 stainless steel included in IEBS-8. The measured weight percent (wt.%) oxide values were consistent
 between the two experiments. There were only slight variations in CaO, Fe2O3, and loss on ignition
 (LOI). In comparison to the starting mixture chemistry, wt.% SiO2 and Al2O3 decreased and wt.% Fe2O3
 increased.
 The XRF results for the Wyoming bentonite ± Grimsel granodiorite + VU cement chip experiments
 (IEBS-9 and -10) varied in the measured weight percent oxide values due to the absence of Grimsel
 granodiorite in IEBS-9. Both IEBS-9 and -10 had increased CaO and Fe2O3 with decreased LOI, SiO2,
 and Al2O3.
 Bulk geochemistry (XRF) was not performed on the reacted cement cylinders or chips as of the end
 of FY23: due to the heterogeneity of the ESD cement (as described in Section 9.2.2.1), the mass of
 XRF sample needed to ensure reproducibility of measurement for the ESD cement is currently being
 evaluated.
