 9.3.6 Electron Microprobe
 Reaction products were analyzed via electron microprobe (EMP) to determine the major element
 composition of mineral phases. The EMP analyses primarily targeted the clay matrix, steel alteration
 products, altered glass shards, and other secondary minerals. Analysis of the reaction products from
 IEBS-6 were completed in FY22 and are reported in Rock et al., 2024, Appendix D. Analyses of
 reactants from IEBS-8, -9, -10, and -0 are complete or ongoing, and reactants from the CEM
 experiments will be analyzed in FY25. The data reduction and interpretation are ongoing and will be
 included and discussed in the FY25 report.

       9.3.7     SEM-EDS

 9.3.7.1 Bentonite ± Grimsel Granodiorite Matrix and Steel
 IEBS-0. Clay in the reaction products of IEBS-0 had a foily texture (Rock et al., 2024, Figure F-4).
 Analcime spheres were observed locally. Feldspar with dissolution texture was also observed.
 IEBS-6 and IEBS-8. The clay matrix of IEBS-6 and IEBS-8 was altered throughout to include analcime,
 garronite, feldspars, and erionite (Rock et al., 2024, Figures F-5 through F-8). The Grimsel
 granodiorite included in the experiments did not experience any significant mineral reactions. The
 316SS included in IEBS-8 The surface of the 316SS reacted in IEBS-8 was also coated in Fe-
 saponite. Clusters of analcime, garronite, and clay were locally attached to the mineralized surface
       (Rock et al., 2024, Figure F-8[D]).
 IEBS-9 and IEBS-10. The montmorillonite from the experiments reacted with low pH cement was foily
 in texture with minor feldspars (Rock et al., 2024, Figures F-9, F-10). In IEBS-10 only, there was no
 alteration texture or new mineral growth on the Grimsel granodiorite fragments.

       9.3.7.2    Cement
 OPC. SEM and XRD analyses of the unreacted OPC material showed various cement phases. In
 IEBS-6 and IEBS-8, which contained OPC in the starting materials, Na-rich analcime was prevalent
 throughout the reaction product (Rock et al., 2024, Figures F-5[A], F-7[B]) along with minor erionite-
 Ca and garronite (Rock et al., 2024, Figures F-5[C], F-8[A]). The fragments of the Grimsel
 granodiorite did not experience significant alterations and only occasional dissolution textures on the
 surface of feldspar grains were observed (Rock et al., 2024, Figure F-5[D]). The OPC chips extracted
 from IEBS-6 and IEBS-8 both had a layer of clay with zeolite embedded on the surface (Rock et al.,
 2024, Figures F-6, F-8[A]). The OPC-only experiment (CEM-0) had a calcium hydrate dominated
 surface with minor calcium carbonate, sulfates, and a fibrous/acicular CSH mineral, likely
 wollastonite or ettringite (Rock et al., 2024, Figure F-11D).
 VU cement. Backscatter emission (BSE) images of the unreacted low pH cement consisted of a
 calcium silicate with a minor amount of lime and ferrite embedded in the matrix (Rock et al., 2024,
 Figure F-2). SEM of the low pH cement included in IEBS-9, has a calcium silicate matrix with the
 formation of platy calcium silicates on the surface and dimples on the surface. Ferrite was exposed
 at the surface. The reacted low pH cement from IEBS-10 was partially covered in a layer of calcium
 silicate spheres with patches of smectite (Rock et al., 2024, Figure F-10).
 ESD cement. SEM-BSE imaging of ESD cement rounds embedded in epoxy and prepared cut in
 cross-section showed the formation of a reaction rim that formed at the cement-solution interface at
 all reaction temperatures (100, 150, and 200 °C). The reaction rim appeared to completely cover
 the surface of the cement, forming a 10 to 20 µm rim of secondary alteration products at the
 surface. The apparent thickness of this rim in cross-section was not substantially different or
 increased with increasing temperature. Positive identification of the chemistry of the reaction rims
 was complicated by the carbon-rich epoxy intermingled with the phases at the cement surfaces in

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 176

 the epoxy-mounted samples. Complementary investigation of the surfaces of cement chips
 additionally reacted in all the CEM experiments indicated that the secondary minerals formed at the
 surfaces of experiments CEM-1 and CEM-2 (reacted at 100 and 150 °C, respectively) was largely
 calcite, while the secondary reaction rim at the surfaces of ESD cement chips reacted in CEM-3
 through -5 (200 °C) was dominated by CSH minerals (Figure 9-19).
 The SEM-SE images on the rough surface of the cement chips were also collected (see Rock et al.,
 2024, Appendix E, Figures F-12 through F-16). The unreacted ESD cement surface was mostly CSH
 minerals (i.e., portlandite) with minor quartz and calcite. CEM-1 (100 °C) was dominated by calcium
 carbonate on the surface, with lesser amounts of CSH minerals and SiO2 observed. At 150 °C
 (CEM-2), CSH rosettes (tobermorite) were sporadically on the cement surface with the surface of the
 cement predominately composed of CSH with CaCO3 and minor M-C-S-H (magnesium-calcium-
 silicate-hydrate). The 200 °C experiments (CEM-3 to -5) showed a transition to CSH secondary
 minerals completely dominating the surface of the cement with mats of the CSH rosettes
 (tobermorite). The area of the cement covered by the rosettes increased with increasing duration of
 the experiment. Other parts of the surface were covered by other CSH minerals and calcium
 carbonate (calcite) with minor amounts of SiO2.

 Figure 9-19. SEM-SE micrographs of ESD cement chips showing the morphology of phases at the
 cement surfaces. The Unreacted ESD shows a CSH minerals (portlandite) with SiO2
 and calcium carbonate. CEM-1 secondary phases are primarily calcium carbonate
 species (calcite, aragonite). CEM-2 begins to show CSH rosettes on a CHH-calcium

November 8, 2024                                                                                             177

 carbonate surface. CEM-3 through -5 show an abundance of CSH minerals with varying
 degrees of the mat of the CSH rosettes.

 9.4 Discussion
 9.4.1 Hydrothermal Interaction of Wyoming Bentonite, Grismel Granodiorite, and
 Synthetic Groundwater Solution
 Interaction of Wyoming bentonite and synthetic Grimsel groundwater was documented in IEBS-0.
 QXRD results showed a decrease in clinoptilolite and plagioclase and an increase in smectite. XRD
 results showed that smectite structures were not altered during the eight-week experiment. Locally,
 analcime crystals were observed. Measured pH values were around 6. Overall, at 250 ºC, smectite
 appeared to remain stable, and form from the alteration of minerals such as plagioclase feldspar
 during the experiment.
 Secondary electron SEM imaging of loose powder mounts of the reaction products from all
 experiments reacting Wyoming bentonite revealed the development of a foily texture in the
 fine-grained clay matrix. Recrystallization of montmorillonite to non-swelling phases, such as illite or
 muscovite, was not observed. XRD patterns from the oriented clay fraction indicated that there was
 no substantial alteration to the montmorillonite structure or expansion capacity (Figure 9-18, Table
 9-3). EMP analyses from the clay matrix of all the IEBS experiments had very similar compositions
 (Rock et al., 2024, Appendix D). In terms of alkali elements, the matrix was most enriched in Na
       (0.15-0.17 atoms per formula unit (apfu)) compared to K (0.02–0.03 apfu) and Ca (0.02–0.03
 apfu). Previous hydrothermal studies have illustrated that Na-montmorillonite is not subject to
 substantial illitization or loss of swelling properties where the bulk system is limited in K availability
 (Cheshire et al., 2014; Mills et al., 2023). This has been observed in previous experimental work
 completed at LANL relevant to conditions in argillaceous systems with Wyoming bentonite ± Opalinus
 Clay (Cheshire et al., 2014; Sauer et al., 2020) and for crystalline systems with Wyoming bentonite ±
 Grimsel granodiorite (Zandanel et al., 2022; Caporuscio et al., 2020).
 Elevated pH may also have a negative impact on the swelling properties of clays even in systems
 with limited K, particularly by promoting the formation of non-swelling clays. Chermak (1992) showed
 that under pH conditions of 11 to 13, Na-rectorite was formed at 150 to 200 °C within 17 days and
 fully formed Na-mica (paragonite) developed after 32 days. At relatively high WRRs used in the
 present cement alteration experiments, such extreme bulk system pH was not achieved. The results
 from our experiments which did not identify significant alteration of montmorillonite in the bentonite
 groundmass to non-swelling clay species is then consistent with previous work and adds to a growing
 body of literature that confirms the possible long-term stability of bentonite in repositories that may
 experience heating events over their lifetimes.

       9.4.1.1    H2S Generation
 Sulfide-induced corrosion of the waste canisters is the primary concern for the Swedish repository
 systems (Börjesson et al., 2010). Therefore, the Swedish Nuclear Fuel and Waste Management
 Company (SKB) have emplaced strict sulfur specifications (sulfide content < 0.5 wt.%; total sulfur
 < 1 wt.%) for the bentonite buffer used in their repositories (Börjesson et al., 2010). Fluid extraction
 from the IEBS experiments was accompanied by strong H2S (aq, g) smells during the 250 °C
 experiments. The reducing conditions in the experiments preserved the H2S (aq, g) species. Pyrite
 content of the Colony Wyoming bentonite used in the experiments was ~0.4 wt.% (Table 9-3) and the
 synthetic Grimsel GW solution contained appreciable SO42- (Table 9-2). The H2S (aq, g) is then likely
 derived from pyrite dissolution within the starting Wyoming bentonite in a Cl-bearing solution and the
 SO4 concentration of the initial solution (Crerar et al., 1978; Ohmoto et al., 1994). Pyrite dissolution
 is tentatively corroborated by QXRD results that showed a slight decrease in the abundance of pyrite

November 8, 2024                                                                                                    178

 in the reaction products; however, the low bulk abundance introduced some uncertainty in this result
 (additionally discussed in Section 9.3.2 on QXRD results).

       9.4.1.2      Summary
 The mineralogical and geochemical changes observed in the Wyoming bentonite-Grimsel
 granodiorite experiments can be applied to understanding potential material interactions in a
 high-temperature, crystalline repository. Montmorillonite remained stable in the 250 ºC hydrothermal
 experiments, indicating the relatively K-poor bulk composition of the system likely prevented
 smectite illitization. Preliminary findings suggest that the clay barrier material (Wyoming bentonite)
 may only experience slight alteration in the initial thermal pulse in a repository setting. These
 findings will be compared to results after the initial and final stages are completed of the long-term,
 full-scale demonstrations (HotBENT).

 9.4.2 Steel-bentonite Interface
 The findings from our previous investigations on mineral precipitation at the steel-bentonite interface
 in hydrothermal experiments mimicking a crystalline rock environment have been described in
 Caporuscio et al. (2019). These previous reports describe the layered alteration sequence observed
 on the surface of steel coupons included in experiments with Wyoming bentonite, Grimsel
 granodiorite, and synthetic Grimsel GW. In general, an Fe-oxide layer is observed to form an
 alteration layer on the surface of the steel coupons after six to eight weeks of hydrothermal
 conditions. The Fe-oxide layer is followed by a thin layer of chlorite and sulfides precipitated from
 H2S-bearing fluids. This thin layer is followed by a ~0.30 to 150 µm thick layer of newly crystallized
 Fe-saponite. The observation of Fe-saponite and sulfide formation was consistent with anticipated
 results of the Fe-bearing/Si-rich fluids from the leaching of the steel and bentonite dissolution
 (Cheshire et al., 2018; Jové Colón et al., 2019) as described by the following equation:

        Fe2+ + Ni2+ + Cr3+ +H2 S(aq) + (Na,K,Ca)0.33 (Al1.67 ,Fe3+
                                                                0.20 ,Mg0.13 )Si4 O10 (OH)2 (Sme)
 Eqn. (9-4)
                 → (Fe,Ni,Cr)9 S8 (Pn) + (Na,K,Ca)0.33 Fe3 (Si3.67 ,Al0.33 )O10 (OH)2 (Fe-Sap)

 In previous experiments completed at LANL, the formation of chlorite and CSH minerals were also
 observed locally attached to steel surfaces. Fe-saponite alteration to chlorite has been achieved by
 Mosser-Ruck et al. (2016) through long duration experiments (up to nine years), demonstrated by
 the following equation showing that smectite consumed by dissolution may produce chlorite
 (chamosite):
 3 smectite + 3Fe + 4H2 O → 1 chlorite + 3 quartz + 2 albite + 1 zeolite + 3H2 Eqn. (9-5)

 In general, higher pH environments associated with cement are expected to inhibit steel corrosion
 (Kursten et al., 2004). IEBS-8 included 316SS reacted with GG+WB+OPC in a Grimsel GW solution.
 The results of this reaction showed a layer of Fe-smectite (likely saponite) formed on the steel
 surface (Figure 9-20, Rock et al., 2024, F-8[B]). Na-smectite, apparently unaltered from the Wyoming
 bentonite matrix, was found in several sections overlying the Fe-smectite at the steel surface. C(A)SH
 minerals (Figure 9-20b) and potential zeolites (Rock et al., 2024, Figure F-8[A, D]) were also
 embedded in the Na-smectite found at steel surfaces, consistent with the widespread occurrence of
 zeolites identified in the clay matrix. Cross-sectional BSE analyses from a steel billet embedded in
 epoxy show a lack of leached layer at the steel surface underlying the secondary precipitates (Figure
 9-20c).

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 179

 Figure 9-20. SEM micrographs of 316SS reacted in IEBS-8: (a) SE image of smectite or Fe-
 smectite directly at the steel surface with an overgrowth of Fe-smectite directly
 overlying initial formation of smectite. Chemical identification of the smectite directly
 overlying steel was complicated by BSE signal from the steel itself, (b) SE image of
 Na-smectite and C(A)SH phases locally identified overlying the Fe-smectite at steel
 surface, and (c) BSE image of reacted 316SS billet embedded in an epoxy mount
 post-reaction and cut in cross-section. Note: smectite overlying the steel directly with
 no apparent change in density of the steel approaching the reacted surface.

 The results from the experiments discussed here indicate that the waste container may act as a
 substrate for mineral growth in response to steel corrosion. In IEBS-8, a lack of Fe-smectite identified
 in the bentonite matrix compared to the abundance of Fe-smectite formed directly at the reacted
 316SS surface indicates that the Fe in the newly precipitated mineral layers at the steel interface
 was sourced from the corroding steel. Future work is needed to address the extent these mineral
 precipitates influence the EBS performance or the repository system, and examine whether the
 minerals formed at the steel surface, such as Fe-saponite, will act as a passive protecting layer
 against further corrosion of the waste containers.

 9.4.3 Effects of cement on EBS alteration (IEBS-6 and -8 through -10)
 The four completed experiments containing cement chips (IEBS-6 and -8 containing OPC; and IEBS-9
 and -10 containing low-pH cement) introduced multiple mineral phases and chemical species that
 were reactive at the high temperature and pressure conditions of the experiments. Both OPC and
 low-pH cement chips increased solution pH values and resulted in diverse mineral reaction products
 in contrast to bentonite-only (IEBS-0) or bentonite in contact with other EBS materials without the
 presence of cement (Zandanel et al., 2022; Jové Colón et al., 2019; Caporuscio et al., 2020).
 Compared to the OPC-only experiment, the addition of EBS material helped buffer the pH of the
 system to circumneutral values (pH of ~7 versus a pH of ~12).

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   180

       9.4.3.1    OPC
 In the experiment with OPC + Grimsel groundwater (CEM-0), the pH of the system went from 8.5 to
 ~11.5 within the first four hours and remained elevated throughout the experiment (pH of 12.23 by
 653 hours). This is the result of reaction (1) when the calcium hydroxide, which is highly soluble in
 water, dissociates into calcium ions (Ca²⁺) and hydroxide ions (OH⁻), the latter of which increases pH.
 On the cement surface collected from CEM-0 was predominately CH (calcium hydroxide) with minor
 amounts of calcium carbonate and sulfate (gypsum) (Figure 9-21). The formation of calcium
 carbonate is likely due to dissolution of CH into solution which allowed carbonation to occur. This
 carbonation process can be summarized in the reaction:
                       Ca(OH)2 (Por) + CO2 (aq,g) →CaCO3 (Cal) + H2O (l)                            Eqn. (9-6)

 In addition, a fibrous/acicular CSH mineral was sporadically seen on the surface, likely ettringite. The
 sodium sulfate in solution reacted with the CH minerals in the OPC to produce gypsum (Rock et al.,
 2024, Figure F-11). The gypsum then reacted with CAH (calcium aluminate hydrate) to form
 ettringite. A study from Lui et al. (2022) showed that OPC soaked over a 15-hour cycle in a 5%
 Na2SO4 solution exhibited an improved density, mass, and strength. However, after repeated soaking
 and drying cycles of the cement, the growth of the dihydrate gypsum and ettringite generated
 expansion stresses, which lead to continuous growth of microcracks, decreasing the quality and
 strength of the OPC.
 The inclusion of OPC in IEBS-6 and -8 resulted in elevated bulk solution pH compared to IEBS-0
 (pH = ~7.5 versus pH = ~6, respectively: Figure 9-4). It appears that OH– derived from the OPC chips
 was either quickly consumed by mineral-forming reactions and/or the system was affected by
 zeolite/clay mineral-forming reactions. SEM characterization of the reaction products from IEBS-6
 and -8 show zeolite formed throughout the reacted groundmass (Figure 9-21) as well as on the
 cement chip surface and on the surface of the steel coupon included in IEBS-8 (Figure 9-20, Rock et
 al., 2024, F-7). Zeolite phases identified by SEM-EDS include garronite and analcime as well as Ca-
 bearing analcime (Figure 9-21). The identification of Na-Ca bearing zeolites is consistent with the
 partial solid solution understood to occur between analcime [Na(AlSi2O6)·H2O] and wairakite
 [Ca(Al2Si4O12)·2H2O]. In addition, QXRD results from the clay groundmass show the proportion of clay
 and other precursor mineral phases that were consumed in zeolite and CSH mineral forming
 reactions (Rock et al., 2024, Appendix C and Results Section 9.3). The formation of 3 to 6 wt.%
 analcime in the clay groundmass of experiments IEBS-6 and -8 was observed, coupled with a
 decrease of similar magnitude in wt.% of smectite. Changes in the abundance of other mineral
 phases was similar to those observed in the experiments without cement. Thus, the formation of
 zeolite reaction.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  181

 Figure 9-21. OPC-only experiment versus OPC reacted with Wyoming bentonite and Grimsel
 granodiorite: (a) Reacted OPC-only (CEM-0) cement surface predominately CH
 minerals with sulfate, (b) reacted OPC surface from CEM-0 with CH and CaCO3
 overlayed with a fibrous CSH mineral, (c) Zeolites (Na-Ca bearing analcime) formed in
 the clay groundmass reacted in IEBS-6, along with collocated garronite, and
 (d) reacted OPC from IEBS-6 with Na-Ca bearing zeolite noted as analcime/wairakite
 on the surface.

       9.4.3.2    VU Cement
 The addition of VU cement in IEBS-9 and -10 resulted in increased pH of the bulk solution compared
 to both the experiments without cement and to the experiments reacting OPC chips (Figure 9-4).
 C(A)SH phases of spherical and platey morphologies were identified at the low-pH cement chip
 surfaces. In contrast to the IEBS experiments that reacted OPC, extensive formation of zeolites
 (analcime, analcime-wairakite, garronite, erionite) were not observed at the cement surface or in the
 clay groundmass. In addition, the apparent extent of alteration was substantially greater than that
 observed of the reacted OPC in previous experiments (Figure 9-22). The formation of zeolites in the
 IEBS-6 and -8 and C(A)SH phases in IEBS-9 and 10 is consistent with previous work showing that
 zeolite formation within bentonite in contact with cement is favored at comparatively lower pH
 values, while C(A)SH mineral formation occurs at higher pH. However, the impact of system pH
 versus temperature on the experimental results is still being evaluated as part of the ongoing
 experimental collaboration.

November 8, 2024                                                                                               182

 C(A)SH Formation
 Previous experiments completed at LANL that reacted Grimsel granodiorite with Wyoming bentonite
 and without cements resulted in the abundant formation of CSH minerals, such as tobermorite
 (Ca5Si6O16(OH)2•4(H2O)) (Caporuscio et al., 2020). C(A)SH phases identified in CEM-0 and IEBS-6
 and -8 that included OPC as a reactant were comparatively scarce. C(A)SH phases of varying
 morphologies were identified at the surfaces of the VU cement chips reacted in IEBS-9 and -10 (Rock
 et al., 2024, Figures F-9 and F-10, additionally discussed in Section 9.4.3.2). The formation of
 tobermorite has previously been experimentally shown to form through bentonite-cement
 interactions in highly alkaline bulk solutions where pH > ~10 (Savage et al., 2007), and as part of
 low-temperature (< 120 °C) hydrothermal reactions (Fernandez et al., 2017). Our results where
 C(A)SH minerals were more abundantly observed in the experiments with VU cement (IEBS-9 and -
 10) may then reflect the influence of the elevated bulk system pH achieved in IEBS-9 and -10, as
 compared to IEBS-6 and -8.
 The formation of C(A)SH minerals may also affect the pH of the system. Savage et al. (2002)
 described the formation of the CSH mineral tobermorite with the generalized reaction:

                           Ca2+ + SiO2 (aq) + H2 O → tobermorite + H+                            Eqn. (9-7)

 in which pH is lowered concurrently with C(A)SH mineral precipitation. Savage (1997) reported that
 C(A)SH mineral formation is favored at high pH (> 11.5). It is notable that IEBS-9 and -10 had bulk
 system pH at or below ~9 for their experimental durations (as measured at 25 °C, Figure 9-4). This
 suggests a potential pH gradient may have occurred at cement surfaces where the release of Ca2+
 and OH- from portlandite alteration created a local environment that drove the formation of CSH or
 C(A)SH minerals, which in turn lowered the bulk pH as per Eqn. (9-7).
 Additional characterization of the reacted cement was performed by researchers at Vanderbilt
 University and SNL in preparation for future work.

 Figure 9-22. VU cement reacted in IEBS-9. Substantial deterioration of the cement product is
 visible at the macroscale.

November 8, 2024                                                                                              183

 Summary
 The comparison of results between OPC and reacted VU cement may indicate alteration pathways of
 VU cement at elevated temperature (200 ºC) conditions, both distinct from the alteration pathways
 of OPC and potentially more rapid. Further study of low-pH materials at elevated temperatures may
 define temperature thresholds or solution chemistry parameters that are critical to the success of
 low-pH cement in a crystalline environment.
 In full-scale EBS demonstrations, such as FEBEX, the alteration zones observed in bentonite at the
 cement-bentonite interfaces (at ambient temperatures) only extended several cm into the buffer
 even after years of interaction at saturated conditions (Fernández et al., 2017). The experimental
 results described here provide insight into mineral alteration that may be expected at higher
 temperatures, such as the observed differences in zeolite types and abundances and sensitivity of
 solution pH to the reacted cement type, at the timescales of the experiments. The initial results
 comparing OPC to VU cement alteration processes suggest that, in repository conditions where
 elevated temperature conditions are possible, the choice of cement products is a factor that should
 be carefully vetted to optimize long-term performance of the EBS.

 9.4.3.3 ESDRED-like low pH Cement Alteration
 Three experiments completed in FY23 and an additional two experiments in FY24 reacted ESD
 cement with the synthetic GW at three different temperatures and three different durations at
 200 ºC. Comparing results between experiments then may isolate effects of temperature and time
 on the extent of reaction expected from ESDRED-like low-pH cement types over the course of a
 heating event in a groundwater-saturated environment. The experiments can additionally be
 compared to previously completed experiments reacting OPC and VU cement at 250 and 200 ºC,
 respectively. The CEM-series experiments differ from the IEBS experiments reported above in that
 they include cement and solution as the sole reactants, without including other materials (bentonite,
 steel, host rock). The results from these experiments are not then directly analogous to the results of
 the IEBS experiments reacting OPC and VU cement; however, comparisons can be made between
 the experiments to isolate trends correlated to different reactants in the interest of informing future
 work on the stability of cements in the context of repository EBS materials.

 Effects of Temperature and Time on Formation of Reaction Rims
 Cross-sectional analyses of the reacted cements from all five experiment showed the formation of a
 reacted rim at the cement-solution interface (Figure 9-23). The apparent extent or thickness of this
 reaction rim was heterogeneous, with the linear extent of reaction at the cement surfaces being
 similar (~10-30 µm) in each experiment, regardless of the reaction. Cement chip XRD corroborated
 the heterogeneity of secondary phases, showing that the overall mineralogy of the ESD cement as
 analyzed by XRD was largely unchanged from the unreacted cement (described in Section 9.3.2).

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 184

 Figure 9-23. BSE images unreacted ESD cement and of the reaction rims at formed at three
 different temperatures and/or reaction duration, as viewed from cross-sections
 formed from the ESD cement cylinders. From top to bottom: Unreacted ESD cement;
 ESD cement reacted at 100 ºC in CEM-1; ESD cement reacted at 150 ºC in CEM-2;
 ESD cement reacted at 200 ºC (4 weeks) in CEM-3; ESD cement reacted at 200 ºC
 (8 weeks) in CEM-5; ESD cement reacted at 200 ºC (6 months) in CEM-4.

November 8, 2024                                                                                        185

 Effect of Temperature and Time on Secondary Mineral Formation
 The types of secondary minerals (calcite, CSH) formed at the surfaces of the ESD cement in
 experiments were correlated to the temperature of reaction. Carbonatation of cement is a known
 process where portlandite in contact with atmospheric CO2 is altered through the reaction 7 above.
 During the curing process, carbonatation through exposure to atmospheric or supercritical CO2 has
 been extensively studied for industrial applications to tune cement parameters (Šavija & Luković,
 2016; Zhang et al., 2017). In hydrothermal conditions, the carbonatation process is dependent on
 the local availability of CO2 in solution which will be dynamically responsive to changes in pH and
 temperature (i.e., Marx et al., 2017). Additionally, carbonatation in hydrothermal conditions will
 compete with other processes facilitated by the infiltrating solution chemistry. Figure 9-24 illustrates
 some of these competing pressures: in all experiments the solution chemistry just before experiment
 termination is undersaturated with respect to portlandite and oversaturated with respect to calcite.
 At higher temperatures with lower Ca concentrations, the degree of fluid oversaturation with respect
 to calcite decreased; in other words, the thermodynamic drive toward calcite precipitation
 decreased. In the CEM-1 through -5, CaCO3 species formed at the lower temperatures of reaction
 (100 and 150 ºC), while dominantly CSH species formed at 200 ºC. Generally, CaCO3 (calcite,
 aragonite) and CSH minerals (here illustrated by tobermorite) both exhibit decreasing solubility with
 increasing temperatures (Figure 9-24). At lower temperatures (100 and 150 ºC), fluid is
 oversaturated with respect to calcite and tobermorite, while at 200 ºC the Ca concentration
 measured from CEM-3 plots as undersaturated with respect to tobermorite and oversaturated with
 respect to calcite. In CEM-1 (100 ºC) and CEM-2 (150 ºC), the Ca concentrations increased up to the
 experiment termination and did not achieve a steady state, indicating that carbonatation as a
 process did not consume all the Ca released from the ESD cement.
 The Ca concentrations of the previously completed IEBS experiments plotted against the selected
 mineral solubilities in Figure 9-24 illustrates the potential impact of CaSO4 minerals (here plotted as
 anhydrite) on the aqueous Ca concentrations. Though CaSO4 minerals were identified in previous
 experiments, none were identified through XRD or SEM-EDS analyses of the reacted ESD cement
 from CEM-1 through -3. It is notable that Ca concentrations in CEM-1 and -2 had not achieved an
 apparent steady state by the experiment termination. Small amounts of minerals formed proximal to
 the cement surface in the reaction rims may not have been identified by these methods.
 Zeolite minerals were identified in previously completed IEBS experiments that reacted OPC at
 250 ºC (IEBS-6 and -8). Analcime and analcime-wairakite were identified extensively at the cement
 surfaces and in the bentonite matrix reacted in those experiments, and were not identified in any of
 the experiments reacting VU cement or ESD cement. All the experiments, however, had solution
 chemistry that was oversaturated with respect to analcime (Figure 9-24) and wairakite by the time of
 experiment termination. The lack of formation of zeolite minerals in the experiments is then
 attributed to lower temperatures, although our results do not preclude the possibility that the zeolite
 mineral formation is kinetically rather than thermodynamically inhibited at temperatures < 250 ºC.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  186

 Figure 9-24. Ca and Si solubility with respect to select minerals of interest at the conditions of the
 initial GW solution. Each curve represents a concentration where the saturation index
 (SI)=0 in the GW solution over temperature. The area above a curve then indicates
 oversaturation and thermodynamic pressure toward precipitation, while the area
 below a curve indicates undersaturation and tendency toward dissolution. The
 symbols mark the concentration of Ca or Si in each experiment as analyzed in the
 penultimate sample collected before experiment termination. a. Geochemical
 stability with respect to Ca-bearing minerals portlandite, anhydrite, tobermorite, and
 calcite. b. Geochemical stability with respect to SiO2-bearing minerals quartz,
 analcime, and tobermorite

 In contrast to Ca solubility results, Si concentrations in all experiments plot above solubility curve for
 the tobermorite. As tobermorite stability increases with increasing temperature, the increased Si
 concentrations in CEM-3 then resulted in a greater degree of fluid oversaturation with respect to that
 mineral, and greater thermodynamic pressure toward precipitation. We note that tobermorite is used
 in these calculations as a reference CSH mineral with a single idealized formula:
                       Ca5Si6H21O27.5 + 10H+ = 5Ca2+ + 6H4SiO4 + 3.5H2O                                Eqn. (9-8)

 During cement alteration, CSH minerals are known to form with a wide range of Ca/Si ratios (0.7 to
 2) (Mindess et al., 2003). The analyzed Ca concentrations plot below the calculated solubility curve
 of tobermorite while Si concentrations plot above it in the experiments reacted > 150 ºC, suggesting
 that the CSH minerals formed at 200 ºC had lower Ca/Si ratios than the reference formula for
 tobermorite used in Figure 9-24. This is consistent with the results calculated from EDS analyses
 that showed atomic Ca/Si ratios of ~0.75 in the CSH minerals formed at the cement chip surfaces
 reacted at 200 ºC, compared to the Ca/Si of ~0.83 in tobermorite. At elevated temperatures the
 increased Si released from ESD cement in our experiments appears to result in the preferential
 formation of high-silica CSH minerals. In EBS systems where solution chemistry will be heavily
 influenced by contributions from silicate and/or carbonaceous host rocks as well as bentonite buffer
 materials, the Ca/Si ratio may be affected by these inputs. In addition, Al contributions from EBS
 materials and host rock may result in the formation of C(A)SH phases in addition to CSH phases, as
 observed in IEBS-9 and -10 and discussed in the section above.
 The replicate experiments at 200 ºC showed alteration of the low-pH cement over time and several
 differences due to the prolonged exposure to heat and moisture may affect the hydration kinetics
 and microstructure of the cement. At four weeks of reaction (CEM-3), the hydration reaction was
 accelerated due to the increased thermal energy, and a significant portion of the cement reacted.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                    187

 The primary hydration products observed were calcium hydroxide (CH, portlandite) and calcium
 silicate hydrate (CSH, tobermorite). At six months and eight weeks (CEM-4 and -5, respectively), the
 degree of hydration increased further leading to a more mature microstructures on the cement
 surface (20 µm versus 40 µm tobermorite rosettes) (Figure 9-5, CSH mats on cement surface).
 However, the process does slow down over the reaction progress, and the difference between four
 and eight weeks is greater than eight weeks and six months, in terms of increased mineralization of
 CSH. Prolonged exposure resulted in the formation of additional phases such as ettringite and
 monosulfate, which are typically not prominent in standard hydration processes at lower
 temperatures (Lu et al., 2021). The porosity of the cement samples increased significantly after
 prolonged exposure to 200 ºC. This is attributed to the formation of microcracks and voids due to
 thermal expansion and subsequent contraction upon cooling (Zhao, 2023). Prolonged exposure to
 the high temperature can intensify these issues, leading to further microcracking (Figure 9-23).
 Overall, the cement experiences rapid hydration in the first few weeks which leads to more mature
 and denser microstructure as time progresses (Irshidat et al., 2021). There is a potential increase of
 the risk of long-term thermal degradation due to microcracks and increased porosity. Future work will
 formulate the kinetic rates of alteration of low-pH cements at elevated temperatures at the different
 durations.

 Figure 9-25. BSE images the ESD cement cross-sections. [Left]: ESD cement reacted at 200 ºC for
 8 weeks from CEM-5 showing little cracking on edges and some porosity. [Right]. ESD
 cement reacted at 200 ºC (6 months) from CEM-4 showing increased cracking and
 porosity at the edge of the cement.

 ESD Cement Impacts on Solution Chemistry
 The aqueous chemistry additionally illustrates the impact of the ESD cement on bulk system
 chemistry in the absence of bentonite or host rock minerals. As discussed in Section Error!
 Reference source not found., alteration of portlandite in water is known to result in elevated
 porewater pH (Eqn. (9-1)). ESD cement and other low-alkali cement formulas are designed to have
 lower porewater pH by decreasing the portlandite-to-silica ratio, as discussed in Section Error!
 Reference source not found. as well as in Rock et al., 2024, Chapter 10 below. However, bulk
 solution pH in the ESD cement experiments and previously completed VU cement experiments was
 elevated compared to the previously completed OPC experiments (Figure 9-4). The elevated pH in
 the ESD and VU experiments may have been driven by increased portlandite dissolution in the
 cements with less portlandite, however, this in turn suggests water-portlandite interaction may be
 more intense in low-alkali cements than in OPC. In OPC, portlandite replacement by calcite during
 carbonatation (Equation 8) results in volumetric expansion of the minerals in the cement structure
 and a systematic loss of porosity in the carbonated zone (Zhang et al., 2017; Šavija & Luković, 2016;
 Rimmelé et al., 2008; Mindess et al., 2003). In CEM-1 and -2, the calcite-rich reaction rim on the

November 8, 2024                                                                                                188

 ESD cement may indicate the incipient phase of a carbonatation front advanced into the cement
 structure that did not form in the cement reacted at 200 ºC. The formation of different types of
 reaction products at elevated temperatures may then indirectly result in increased porosity or
 permeability in low-alkali cements, facilitating faster overall dissolution rates for minerals in low-
 alkali cements than in OPC types.
 Porosity and/or permeability may also change dynamically during the experiments due to alteration
 of the non-portlandite fraction in the low-alkali cement. Silica concentrations increased over the
 course of the first three CEM experiments, and the steady-state silica concentrations of the
 experiments were positively correlated with increasing temperature (Figure 9-5). This is consistent
 with the increased solubility of many silicate minerals with increasing temperature, including that of
 amorphous silica and quartz that comprise part of the ESD cement that was reacted in these
 experiments. XRD results also showed that the quartz fraction identified at the ESD cement surfaces
 decreased with increasing temperatures (Section 9.3.2). At elevated temperatures, then, low-alkali
 cement formulations that include increased silica proportions may experience a greater overall mass
 loss through increased silicate dissolution, and a resulting increase in porosity and permeability
 throughout the cement structure. This may then increase the susceptibility of portlandite and other
 highly reactive cement minerals to increased dissolution.

 9.4.3.4 Implications for Cement-Bentonite Interactions
 The K and Na concentrations are of interest in these experiments as Na/K ion exchange in the
 montmorillonite fraction of bentonite assemblages may result in loss of some swelling properties.
 Previous work has shown that K-limited environments result in little exchange and largely preserve
 the swelling properties of the bentonite matrix, even in hydrothermal conditions (Cheshire et al.,
 2014; Sauer et al., 2020; Mills et al., 2023; Zandanel et al., 2022). Sources for K in these
 experiments include the cement reactants, Grimsel granodiorite, and accessory minerals in the
 bentonite matrix (feldspars, clinoptilolite). The results from the CEM-series experiments indicate a
 contribution of K from low-alkali cement in the experiments that may impact swelling properties of
 montmorillonite in the bentonite barrier over time. Comparing results of the low-alkali cement (VU
 and ESD) experiments with the bentonite-only experiment illustrates that K increased to > 40 mg L-1
 in CEM-3, while the K increased to ~10 mg L-1 in IEBS-9 and did not substantially increase above the
 initial concentration in IEBS-0 (Figure 9-26). In contrast, Na concentrations in CEM-3 behaved
 apparently conservatively, while in IEBS-0 Na concentrations decreased following an initial increase.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                189

 Figure 9-26. Comparison of K and Na concentrations from three selected experiments: IEBS-0
 (bentonite and GW at 250 ºC), IEBS-9 (bentonite, GW, and VU cement at 200 ºC), and
 CEM-3 (ESD cement and GW at 200 ºC).
 The paired increase in Na with the comparatively minor increase in K in IEBS-9 (and -10) suggest
 that the K released from the reacted cement was incorporated into the montmorillonite adjacent to
 the reacted cement resulting in Na increases in the bulk solution. Clay swelling XRD of the bentonite
 matrix reacted in IEBS-9 and -10 did not show substantial changes to the swelling properties of the
 bentonite. However, such ion exchange processes may become of concern at industrial scales and
 on longer timescales. Future work may explore ways of meaningfully applying the results of these
 experiments to evaluating potential impacts of the observed lab-scale phenomena to the field scale,
 including extraction of reaction kinetics and comparisons to full-scale experiments conducted in
 repository URL sites.

 9.5 Conclusions
 This document summarizes the evaluation of the effect of temperature on hydrothermal alteration of
 a cement, especially low-pH cement. The FY24 experiments combined an ESDRED-like low-pH
 cement with a Grimsel granodiorite synthetic groundwater solution at different durations at 200 ºC (8
 weeks, CEM-5 and 6 months, CEM-4), along with an additional OPC-only experiment (CEM-0) to
 compare to the IEBS results for a previous FY. Results presented and discussed include SEM-EDS
 analyses, aqueous geochemistry data, XRF, and XRD. EMP analyses are ongoing, and interpretation
 will be included in the report for FY25. Results from experiments completed in previous years at
 LANL are also discussed where interpretation of results is ongoing, specifically for QXRD, clay
 mineral XRD, and EMP analysis. Experiments discussed include IEBS-0, that reacted Wyoming
 bentonite with Grimsel groundwater solution at 250 ºC and 150 bar; IEBS-6 and -8, that reacted
 Wyoming bentonite and Grimsel GW solution with OPC at 250 ºC and 150 bar; IEBS-9, and -10, that
 reacted Wyoming bentonite and Grimsel GW solution with an experimental low-pH cement at 200 ºC
 and 150 bar; and CEM-1 through -3 combined an ESDRED-like low-pH cement with a Grimsel
 granodiorite synthetic groundwater solution at different temperatures (100 – 200 ºC) for 4 weeks.

November 8, 2024                                                                                               190

 9.5.1 Research Concepts Developed
 The results of experiments discussed that reacted Wyoming bentonite showed that no substantial
 alteration of the montmorillonite in bentonite to secondary non-swelling clay phases occurred at
 experimental conditions. Experimental conditions included alteration of bentonite in the GW solution
 with no co-reactants, co-alteration of the bentonite + OPC + Grimsel granodiorite ± steel, and co-
 alteration of bentonite + low-pH cement ± Grimsel granodiorite. The alteration of the swelling clay
 montmorillonite to non-swelling illite (illitization) in Wyoming bentonite in a Grimsel granodiorite wall
 rock environment is interpreted to be restricted due to the bulk geochemistry of the overall system
 (i.e., low K) and/or kinetics.
 • Hydrothermal alteration of 316 stainless steel in IEBS-8 (bentonite + OPC + Grimsel granodiorite)
 resulted in newly crystallized Fe-saponite formed at the steel-bentonite interface. Saponite was
 observed to form perpendicular to the steel surface, morphologically similar to secondary phases
 formed in experiments without cement reactants. Fe enrichment of the bentonite due to
 interaction with steel corrosion products was observed to extend < 50 μm from the steel surface.
 • In the experiment with OPC + Grimsel groundwater (CEM-0), the pH of the system went from 8.5
 to ~11.5 as a result of the release of hydroxide ions into the water increases its pH, making it
 more alkaline. The cement surface was predominately CH (calcium hydroxide) with minor
 amounts of calcium carbonate and sulfate (gypsum) with a fibrous/acicular CSH mineral that
 was sporadically seen on the surface, likely ettringite. The addition of OPC with bentonite +
 granodiorite + groundwater solution experiments had steady-state pH values slightly increased
 (by ~1.5) to those of analogous experiments that did not include cements. The inclusion of OPC
 resulted in the abundant formation of zeolite minerals (dominantly analcime/wairakite) at the
 experimental conditions (250 ºC and 150 bar). Clay is interpreted to be consumed as part of the
 zeolite-forming reactions, facilitated by the conditions created by OPC (elevated pH and Ca
 concentration).
 • The addition of VU cement (experimental low-pH cement) resulted in elevated pH of the
 experimental solutions in IEBS-9 and -10, as compared to experiments with no cement reactants
 as well as analogous experiments with OPC. C(A)SH phases were also observed to have formed
 at the surface of the low-pH cement chips reacted in IEBS-9 and -10; abundant zeolite formation,
 such as had formed in experiments with OPC chips, was not observed. The comparatively
 elevated pH of these experiments is interpreted to be a driver of the increased C(A)SH formation.
 • ESD cement reacted at three experimental temperatures (experiment CEM-1 at 100, CEM-2 at
 150, and CEM-3 200 ºC) in Grimsel GW solution with no other reactants resulted in elevated
 steady-state pH values similar to those observed in VU cement experiments IEBS-9 and -10. Ca
 concentrations decreased with increasing temperature while Si concentrations increased with
 increasing temperature. At all temperatures, a reaction rim of similar heterogenous thickness
 (10-30 μm) formed at the cement-solution interface. At 100 and 150 ºC, the reaction rim
 mineralogy was dominated by calcite; at 200 ºC, the reaction rim was observed to be composed
 largely of CSH phases. The time series performed at 200 ºC showed the cement experienced
 rapid hydration in the first few weeks, leading to more mature and denser microstructure as time
 progresses. There is a potential increase of the risk of long-term thermal degradation due to
 microcracks and increased void space.
 • Comparison of the Na and K trends in all discussed experiments suggests that K contributed by
 the cement reactants was exchanged for sodium in the Na-montmorillonite present in the
 reacted bentonite matrices of IEBS-9 and -10. The extent of reaction did not quantifiably reduce
 the swelling properties of the reacted bentonite as evaluated through clay XRD methods.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   191

 9.5.2 Research Avenues to be Emphasized in FY25
 • Continue incorporating results into a database of Grimsel granodiorite and EBS experiments for
 use in evaluation of EBS materials performance at elevated temperatures in crystalline
 environments.
 • Continue data analysis and design future experiments to isolate rates of formation of secondary
 minerals.
 • Complete further work to understand formation of C(A)SH and zeolite minerals at unexpectedly
 low pH (< 9.0) in EBS systems.
 • Extract reaction rates of low-pH cement alteration responsive to increased temperatures that can
 be incorporated into sensitivity analyses.
 • Develop conceptual models of alteration pathways of low-pH cement through hydrothermal
 experiments at different temperatures and solution compositions.
 • Complete EMP analysis of all reactants to date to identify reaction pathways from EBS material
 alteration to secondary mineral formation.
 • Continue to build a database of Grimsel granodiorite and EBS experiments.
 • Incorporate results into geochemical modeling codes.
 The development of the database of reacted EBS materials, along with summary conclusions of
 alteration of EBS materials in a crystalline rock environment, will be of use to other experimental
 teams in the DOE complex, system modelers, and the international repository science community in
 the advancement of concepts related to high-temperature crystalline repository environments.

November 8, 2024                                                                                             192

 10 HYDROTHERMAL TESTING TO SUPPORT THE HOTBENT FIELD TEST
 10.1 Introduction
 To address the temperatures involved with the disposal of DPCs from elevated-temperature, full-
 scale experiments are invaluable for characterizing the alteration of barrier materials that may occur
 in future repository sites. One such long-term experiment is HotBENT (High Temperature Effects on
 Bentonite), performed by the Swiss National Cooperative for the Disposal of Radioactive Waste
 (NAGRA) at the Grimsel Test Site to simulate a generic crystalline repository concept (Blechschmidt
 et al., 2020; Matteo et al., 2018). The HotBENT experiment is divided into two physical sectors to
 allow phased dismantling that are separated by plugs constructed of low-pH cement, also known as
 shotcrete (Figure 10-1). To simulate a heat source, heaters are encased in bentonite in each sector.
 Sector 2 of HotBENT, located between the plugs 1 and 2, hosts two heaters with estimated high
 temperatures of 175 ºC and is planned to be dismantled after 5 years. Sector 1, located behind plug
 1, hosts two heaters with estimated temperatures of 200 °C and 175 °C, respectively, at the heater
 surface and will be dismantled after 15 to 20 years. Construction for HotBENT started in 2019, and
 phases of the experiment are expected to run for 5 to 20 years before dismantling (Grimsel Test Site,
 2023). Table 10-1 lists the expected temperature gradient for the 200 ºC heater scenario (AMBERG,
 2019).

 Figure 10-1. Schematic of the HotBENT heater test: (a) Location of the HotBENT experiment at the
 Grimsel Test Site, (b) the HotBENT gallery, and (c) layout of the HotBENT experiment.
 Heaters are depicted in brown with the temperature indicated (from Kober et al.,
 2023)

 Table 10-1. Summary of the expected temperature gradient for the 200 ºC heater scenario of the
 HotBENT long-term (5-20 year) experiments (AMBERG, 2019).
 Radial distance from heater surface (cm) Expected max. temperature values (ºC)
 10 178
 20 147
 30 128
 40 114
 50 103
 60* 93
 70 83
 *Interface between the buffer and rock. With a heater of 1.05 m in diameter, the buffer has a
 thickness of 61.5 cm.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                193

 Specific objectives of the HotBENT experiment include: (1) studying the effects of high thermal
 loading (temperatures > 150 °C) on the performance of bentonite and/or bentonite mixtures as
 buffer materials at realistic scales and in-situ conditions; (2) enhancing the existing geochemical
 databases and the understanding of buffer performance, in particular in terms of robustness;
 (3) assessing the effect on the generally agreed safety functions at high temperatures; (4) evaluating
 the corrosion of potential canister materials and potential microbial activities; and (5) characterizing
 interactions between bentonite buffers with cement-based materials used for liners and/or plugs.
 The HotBENT experiment is emplaced in the tunnel excavated from Grimsel granodiorite that hosted
 the previous FEBEX experiment and so benefits from extensive previous characterizations (Alonso et
 al., 2005; Huertas & Santiago, 1997; Lanyon & Gaus, 2016; Villar, 2021). A focus of both the
 HotBENT study and the experiments performed at LANL is then the alteration and co-alteration
 behaviors of EBS materials in a crystalline host rock system.
 EBS materials used in the HotBENT experiment include two types of bentonites – a Czech bentonite
 (BCV) and a Wyoming bentonite denoted here as full-scale experiment (FE) bentonite. Cement liner
 (Figure 10-1) included in the HotBENT heater test is based on low-pH cement compositions first
 developed as part of a 5-year project that focused on evaluating the feasibility of upscaling proposed
 technical approaches and materials to the industrial scales needed for geologic repositories, the
 Engineering Studies and Demonstrations of Repository Designs (ESDRED) project (De Bock et al.,
 2008; Verstricht, 2009). The stability of ESDRED-like concrete materials in saturated repository
 conditions at ambient temperatures has been investigated in laboratory- and full-scale experiments.
 Co-alteration of bentonites in contact with both OPC and ESDRED-like low-pH cements in a clay-rock
 URL were investigated as part of the full-scale experiments hosted in Mont Terri URL in Switzerland.
 Results after over two years of interaction at ambient conditions in the URL showed zoned alteration
 in all types of cement studied, on the scale of hundreds of microns, extending into the cement from
 the cement-Opalinus Clay boundary (Jenni et al., 2014). After five years of interaction, a Mg-enriched
 zone was observed that extended several millimeters into the cement from the cement-Opalinus Clay
 interface (Dauzeres et al., 2016). Increased porosity in the ESDRED-like low-pH cement also
 indicated increased potential for cement carbonation further into the low-pH cements than into
 ordinary Portland cement (Jenni et al., 2014).
 Use of low-carbon steel is considered favorable in some repository designs for its low cost and
 positive long-term performance by corrosion, mechanical strength, and structural reliability metrics
 (King, 2012; Marsh & Taylor, 1988). Interactions between steel corrosion products and bentonite
 barrier materials are naturally dependent on the characteristics of the bentonite, as different
 mineralogical compositions may result in the availability of different components to the geochemical
 system that have different impacts on steel stability. For example, increased sodium chloride in the
 system, as through cation exchange within bentonite minerals, is expected to result in initially
 increased steel corrosion rates (Möller et al., 2006; Smart, 2008; You et al., 2020). In saline
 solutions, corrosion rates are expected to increase with increasing temperature, highlighting the
 importance of characterizing alteration properties of low-carbon steel at elevated temperatures. After
 an initial corrosion period, however, increased salinity may speed the formation of a passivating
 oxide layer at the steel surface that then slows corrosion rates (Cáceres et al., 2009). In addition,
 secondary minerals that form on steel alteration in natural systems may form a barrier to corrosion
 that is not well reflected in laboratory corrosion studies (Möller et al., 2006; Nogara & Zarrouk,
 2018). The feedback interactions between steel, bentonite barrier minerals, and host rock
 formations during an elevated-temperature event in a repository may then be specific to the
 combination of reactants in a way not captured by simplified scenarios.
 LANL’s EBS International experiments are designed to complement the complexity of the HotBENT
 experiments by using similar materials and parameters at lab timescales to isolate transient
 alteration processes occurring in the early part of the heating event. The previous experiments
 combined a Wyoming bentonite (FE bentonite), Czech bentonite (BCV), Grimsel granodiorite, and

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  194

 steel in a simulated groundwater (GW) solution at the maximum HotBENT heater temperature
 (200 °C). HBT-1 and -2 are included in this report and HBT-3 and -4 are summarized in FY23. Two
 experiments in FY24 and one additional experiment completed in FY23 combined the same FE or
 BCV bentonite as used in previous years’ experiments with a low-pH cement modeled on the
 composition used for the shotcrete liner in HotBENT. The results of this experiment are discussed in
 the context and applications to the HotBENT long-term experiment.

 10.2 Methods
 10.2.1 Hydrothermal Experiments
 Experiments completed within the scope of LANL EBS International R&D were designed to
 characterize reaction products of barrier materials used in the ongoing HotBENT experiments. The
 experiment completed in FY23 and FY24 focused on the reaction between FE or BCV bentonite and
 a low-pH cement manufactured using the composition of the cement used as the liner material in
 HotBENT (ESDRED-like low-pH cement) and was conducted at 200 °C for 4 and 6 weeks to allow
 characterization of reactants during the initial stages of alteration.
 Reactants in all experiments were saturated with synthetic Grimsel GW and heated isothermally
 using the methodology described in Section 9.2.1 and Rock et al., 2024, Appendix A. In brief,
 reactants (Table 10-2) were loaded into a flexible gold reaction cell and fixed into a 500 mL gasket
 confined closure reactor (Seyfried et al., 1987). A 1:1 mixture (by mass) of Fe3O4 and Fe° was added
 at 7 wt.% of the total mass of solid reactants to buffer redox conditions. Fe buffers were included in a
 gold capsule lightly crimped at the ends to allow fluid communication while preventing an interface
 between the Fe buffer and other reactants. Experiments were pressurized to 150 to 160 bar and
 were heated isothermally to 200 °C for 4 or 6 weeks. Reaction liquids were extracted periodically,
 and sampled reaction liquids were split into pH, and both filtered (0.22 μm) and filtered + acid for
 cation and anion analyses, respectively. All aliquots from each experiment were stored in a
 refrigerator at 1 °C until analysis. Parameters for all experiments are included in Table 10-2.
 Table 10-2. Parameters for the HBT experiment completed in FY24 as well as those completed in
 FY21-23. Detailed information on reactants is included in Section 8.2 below. All values
 are mass (g) except where otherwise noted.
 Run Temp Pressure
 Exp.  Reactants Time (°C)  (bars) GW FE BCV ESD LCS Fe° Fe3O4 WWR
       HBT-1 FE + LCS     8 weeks    200     150    132.98   21.01                    5.05    0.55   0.55  6.0
       HBT-2 BCV + LCS    8 weeks    200      150   127.14           21.00            5.03   0.55   0.55      5.8
       HBT-5 FE+ESD       4 weeks    200      150   107.94    9.75            9.75           0.48   0.48      5.3
       HBT-6 BCV+ESD      6 weeks    200      150   125.13            8.85    8.88           0.67   0.67      6.6
       HBT-7 BCV+ESD      4 weeks    200      150   107.35            8.31    8.31           0.63   0.63      6.0
 Abbreviations: GW, Grimsel groundwater; BCV, Czech bentonite; ESD, “ESDRED-like” low-pH cement; WRR,
 water-rock ratio (by mass).

       10.2.2    Materials
 ESDRED-like low-pH cement (ESD). ESD is a low-alkali shotcrete product using a formulation
 developed as part of the ESDRED (Engineering Studies and Demonstrations of Repository Designs)
 project (Mäder et al., 2017). The general formula includes ~60% CEM I (cement product having at
 least 95% OPC) with ~40% microsilica, with variable amounts of a set accelerator (Lothenbach et al.,
 2014).

November 8, 2024                                                                                                    195

 Wyoming (FE) bentonite. The bentonite used in the present study is an unprocessed, Na-rich
 Wyoming bentonite that was leftover material from the FE experiment at the Mont Terri URL, which
 closely replicates the Wyoming-type bentonite that will be used in HotBENT. The bentonite is required
 to contain: > 75% smectite (Na-montmorillonite), < 1% pyrite, < 0.5% sulfur, and < 1% organic
 carbon as specified in Karnland (2007), and Leupin and Johnson (2013). No additives to the
 bentonite are allowed. Granulated bentonite material and compressed block material are both used
 in the experiments.
 Czech (BCV) bentonite. BCV bentonite is an unprocessed Czech bentonite enriched in iron. This
 bentonite formed from the in-situ alteration of Fe-rich tuffs and augite-biotite-type tuffites (Villar et
 al., 2021). The smectite content in the BCV is ~90 wt.%. BCV contains minor amounts of quartz,
 calcite/dolomite, apatite, goethite, kaolinite, anatase, and mica.
 Synthetic Grimsel groundwater. The synthetic groundwater used was identical to that of the IEBS
 experiments described in Section 9.2.1, selected as an approximation of the groundwater chemistry
 from the Grimsel Test Site as reported in Kersting et al. (2012). Chemistry of the synthetic fluid is
 presented in Table 9-2. The groundwater at the Grimsel Test Site is a Na-CO3 type water and has a
       pH of ~8.6 to 8.8.
 Low carbon steel (LCS). LCS is mostly composed of Fe with ~0.2 wt.% C, 0.9 wt.% Mn, < 0.04 wt.% P,
       and < 0.05 wt.% S.

 10.3 Results
 10.3.1 Aqueous Geochemistry
 The planned weekly sampling schedules for HBT-1 and -2 were affected by COVID-19 and limited
 aqueous samples were collected from these experiments. Reaction fluids were sampled twice during
 the HBT-1 experiment: first, after one week of heating, and second, when the experiment was
 dismantled. HBT-2 was only sampled pre- and post-experiment. HBT-3 and -4 were sampled every
 week of the experiment duration. HBT-5 had a modified sampling schedule that included an
 accelerated sample schedule during the first week to capture rapid changes in aqueous
 geochemistry and was sampled once a week for the remainder of the experimental duration. The
 analyzed aqueous geochemistry is summarized below and reported in Rock et al., 2024, Appendix B.
 pH. All experiments started with a pH from ~8 to ~8.5, illustrated in Figure 10-2 and in
 supplementary Rock et al., 2024, Tables B17-B26. In experiments HBT-1 through -4 which did not
 include cement as a reactant, the pH decreased rapidly to a relative steady-state pH of ~6.
 The pH of HBT-5 (containing FE bentonite) initially decreased rapidly within the first three days from
 8.5 to 6.9, then more gradually increased to an apparent steady state of about 8.8 before the
 experiment ended. The pH of HBT-6 and -7, both containing BCV bentonite, experienced a similar
 decrease from 8.5 to 6.8 and 6.3, respectively. After this initial decrease, pH in HBT-6 remained
 constant between 6.4 and 6.7 while pH in HBT-7 increased slightly to a maximum value of 7.0 by the
 end of the experiment.
 Silica. An increase in aqueous silica concentration in HBT-1 was observed in the unfiltered samples
 (403 to 513 mg L-1) but decreased in the filtered samples (390–174 mg L-1), between the initial
 sampling to the end of the experiment (Figure 10-2). The SiO2 concentration in HBT-2 increased in
 both the filtered and unfiltered samples at experiment termination.
 Beginning with HBT-5 and continuing with the experiments conducted this FY, collected samples
 were preserved with nitric acid in accordance with the EPA 200.7 method for cation determination.
 This change resulted in an increase in measured Si compared to previous results that is more
 representative of the in-situ chemistry of the experimental fluid. HBT-5, -6, and -7 all exhibited a large

November 8, 2024                                                                                                    196

 increase in aqueous silica concentrations within the first 400 hours of each experiment: 0 to 694 mg
 L-1 in HBT-5; 9.3 to 384 mg L-1 in HBT-6; and 0 to 550 mg L-1 in HBT-7. After this point, aqueous silica
 concentrations began to approach steady states, ending with 655 mg L-1 in HBT-5, 500 mg L-1 in
       HBT-6, and 583 mg L-1 in HBT-7.
 Iron. In both HBT-1 and -2, iron concentrations increased in the final samples collected after
 experimental termination. Increased Fe concentrations in the final samples (collected at bench
 conditions after experiment termination) are interpreted to be a quench effect, given the relatively
 low Fe concentration in the single sample collected from HBT-1 at experimental pressure and
 temperature (Figure 10-2).
 HBT-5 exhibited a small increase in Fe concentration from 0 to 0.3 mg L-1 at 355 hours, then a
 decrease back to 0 mg L-1 by 691 hours. Fe concentrations remained below ~0.2 mg L-1 for the
 duration of HBT-6 and -7, except for a sudden increase to 1.2 mg L-1 at 75 hours and decrease back
 to 0.03 mg L-1 by 193 hours in HBT-7. This anomalous spike may be caused by sample
 contamination during the analysis process, as the spike was not observed in HBT-6, which contained
 identical components.
 Aluminum. Aluminum concentrations in the filtered samples from HBT-1 were elevated in the sample
 collected after one week of reaction time and decreased again to < 0.2 mg L-1 in the final sample
 taken after experimental termination (Figure 10-2).
 Aluminum concentrations in HBT-5 increased within the first day of reaction time from 0 to a
 maximum of 4.8 mg L-1 before decreasing to 1.4 mg L-1 within the first week of reaction time.
 Aluminum concentrations then increased in an approximately linear trend to a final concentration of
 2.1 mg L-1 just before the experiment terminated. HBT-6 and -7 also exhibited rapid increases in Al
 concentrations within the first day (5.1 and 5.4 mg L-1, respectively). Though measured Al
 concentrations in these last two experiments were far more variable over time, they followed an
 apparently increasing trend like that of HBT-5.

 Figure 10-2. pH and concentrations in mg L-1 of SiO2, Fe, and Al in HBT-1, -2, -5 through -7.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  197

 Sodium. Sodium concentrations increased to higher values in the FE bentonite-only experiment
 (HBT-1); after one week of reaction time, Na appears to have increased rapidly in the first week and
 continue increasing throughout the experimental duration (Figure 10-3). Not much can be said for
 HBT-2, except that there was little difference between the starting Na concentration and the post-
 reaction sample.
 Sodium concentrations of HBT-5 increased steadily from 150 to 338 mg L-1 by 355 hours, after
 which the trend became approximately stable until the end of the experiment. HBT-6 experienced a
 slight increase in Na concentration from 124 to 175 mg L-1 by 192 hours, which then decreased to
 131 mg L-1 and remained stable for the remainder of the experimental duration. Na concentrations
 of HBT-7 initially decreased from 176 mg L-1 and became stable at 121 mg L-1 by 193 hours.
 Potassium. Potassium increased to greater concentrations in the experiment with BCV bentonite-only
 (HBT-2) versus the experiment with FE bentonite-only (HBT-1) (Figure 10-3).
 Potassium concentrations of HBT-5 remained below method detection limits until 187 hours, with
 the exception of a sudden spike to 7.4 mg L-1 in the first day of reaction. After 187 hours, K
 concentration increased to a maximum of 12.7 mg L-1 just before the experiment terminated. HBT-6
 and -7 exhibited nearly identical trends in K concentration, characterized with a rapid increase to
 ~19 mg L-1 within the first 75 hours of reaction and a gradual increase after 192 hours to a final
 concentration of 20.4 mg L-1 in HBT-6 and 22.7 mg L-1 in HBT-7. HBT-6 also experienced spikes in K
 concentration to 32.8 mg L-1 at 192 hours of reaction and 37.6 mg L-1 at 526 hours of reaction.
 Calcium. Similar to the K concentrations, Ca concentrations increased to greater concentrations in
 the experiment with BCV bentonite-only (HBT-2) versus the experiment with FE bentonite-only (HBT-1)
       (Figure 10-3).
 Calcium concentrations of HBT-5 initially decreased from 3.9 to 1.4 mg L-1 during initial heating of
 the experiment, then increased throughout the experimental duration to a final concentration of
 11.1 mg L-1. In contrast, Ca concentrations of HBT-6 and -7 increased rapidly to 35.2 and 19.8 mg L-
 1, respectively, by 192 hours, after which both trends began to approach a steady state. HBT-6

 experienced a sudden increase in Ca concentration between 1200 and 1360 hours from 24.8 to
       49.1 mg L-1.
 Magnesium. Samples collected from HBT-1 at experimental conditions show decreased Mg
 concentrations (< 2 mg L-1) compared to the Mg concentrations in the initial and final samples
 collected at bench conditions (Figure 10-3).
 In contrast with the other major alkali/alkali-earth elements, Mg concentrations in all experiments
 rapidly decreased during the initial heating process and remained stable at or below 0.5 mg L-1. Mg
 concentrations in HBT-6 decreased from 37.6 to 2.9 mg L-1 during the initial heating process, then
 continued to decrease until stable below 0.5 mg L-1 after 360 hours.

November 8, 2024                                                                                              198

 Figure 10-3. Concentration in mg L-1 of Na, K, Ca, and Mg in HBT-1, -2, -5 through -7

 Chloride. Chloride concentrations behaved relatively conservatively in most experiments, with final
 concentrations within ~1 mg L-1 of the concentration in the initial solution (Figure 10-4).
 Concentrations in HBT-1 were higher throughout the experimental duration (~20 mg L-1 compared to
       ~10 mg L-1 in HBT-2 through -4).
 Chloride remained a relatively conservative analyte in HBT-5 (~19 mg L-1) and HBT-7 (~30 mg L-1)
 throughout the experimental durations, apart from an initial increase in HBT-5 from 10.7 to ~19 mg
 L-1 within the first day of reaction. The starting fluid in HBT-6 contained far more Cl (65.7 mg L-1) than
 in the other two experiments, and nearly doubled in concentration after 192 hours to 121 mg L-1. Cl
 concentrations in HBT-6 then decreased until 526 hours where they became approximately stable at
 ~74 mg L-1.
 Sulfate. HBT-1 increased in sulfate over the experiment from the initial to final sample from ~245 mg
 L-1 to ~300 mg L-1 (Figure 10-4). Sulfate trends in HBT-2 appeared more stable: the SO4
 concentrations in the initial and final samples collected from HBT-2 were relatively unchanged.
 Sulfate concentrations in HBT-5 increased rapidly in the first 187 hours of reaction from 166 to 409
 mg L-1, then began to approach a steady state at 525 mg L-1 by the termination of the experiment.
 HBT-6 exhibited a similar increase in sulfate concentrations in the first 43 hours to a maximum of
 302 mg L-1, which then decreased to 139 mg L-1 by 693 hours followed by a slow increase to a final
 concentration of 146 mg L-1. Sulfate concentrations in HBT-7 increased from 96.7 to 142 mg L-1 in
 the first 75 hours of reaction and remained stable at ~155 mg L-1.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                    199

 Figure 10-4. Concentration in mg L-1 of Cl and SO4 in HBT-1, -2, -5 through -7.

 10.3.2 XRD analyses

       10.3.2.1   QXRD
 QXRD analyses of the starting materials and the bulk reaction product of the bentonite fractions are
 presented in Table 10-3, Figure 10-5, and Figure 10-6. The results illustrate changes in the bulk
 mineralogy of the bentonite as a result of hydrothermal interaction with the ESD cement and
 synthetic groundwater at elevated temperatures.
 FE bentonite (unreacted). The unreacted FE bentonite contained 94.2 wt.% montmorillonite with
 minor amounts of quartz (2.1 wt.%), feldspars (1.5 wt.%), cristobalite (1.2 wt.%), and an amorphous
 phase (1.0 wt.%) (Figure 10-5). The bentonite is slightly different from the Wyoming bentonite used in
 the IEBS experiment because the FE test used pure Wyoming sodium bentonite (Hansen et al.,
 2016).
 BCV bentonite (unreacted). The Czech bentonite had slightly less montmorillonite than the FE
 bentonite and contained minor amounts of mica, along with quartz, anatase, apatite, carbonates,
 and goethite (Figure 10-6).
 ESDRED-like low pH cement, ESD (unreacted). The unreacted ESD cement was composed mostly of
 calcium carbonate (89 wt.%) with minor silicon dioxide (11 wt.%). Due to the heterogeneity of the
 cement, the QXRD analysis may not be a true representation of the sample.
 HBT-1. The FE bentonite included in this experiment had a slight decrease in the smectite fraction
       (94.2 - 92.4 wt.%). The amorphous phase increased 1.0 wt.% to 2.0 wt.%.
 HBT-2. The smectite content remained unchanged after the experiment, but the clay fraction gained
 the addition of illite (1.2 wt.%). Goethite was expended and magnetite is present from the added
 redox buffer. The amorphous phase decreased from 1.9 wt.% to 0.5 wt.%.
 HBT-5. The FE bentonite experienced little change after four weeks of heating. There was a slight
 reduction (~ 3 wt.%) in smectite and a slight increase in amorphous material (2 wt.%) (Figure 10-5).
 However, all other changes may be attributed to natural variation between the samples (~1 wt.%).
 HBT-6. The six-week BCV + ESD cement experiment saw a slight reduction in the smectite phase and
 an increase in the mica and amorphous phase (Figure 10-6). Any other variation can be contributed
 to natural sample variation.
 HBT-7. The BCV + ESD cement reacted for four weeks and had a slight reduction in smectite with an
 increase in calcite, likely derived from the cement (Figure 10-6). Like the other samples, the other
 differences in mineralogy are due to sample variation.

       10.3.2.2    Cement XRD
 The unreacted ESD cement contained calcium carbonate and silicon oxide phases (Figure 10-7). The
 ESD cement after reaction in HBT-5 also contained calcium carbonate and silicon dioxide, along with
 tobermorite, feldspars, and halite. The feldspar originated from the FE bentonite, whereas halite
 precipitated from the aqueous fluid during experiment cooling. The cement surfaces from HBT-6 and
 -7 were similar to HBT-5 with the addition of tobermorite. Smectite was also present on the surface
 of the cement from HBT-7 from remnant clay on the cement surface.

 Figure 10-7. Cement XRD patterns of reacted ESD cement from HBT-5 compared to unreacted ESD
 cement. Peaks correspond to felspar (Fsp), silica (SiO2), halite (Hal), and calcite (Cal).

       10.3.2.3    Clay XRD
 The clay-size fraction (< 2 µm) was separated via density separation in DI water from a gently
 crushed portion of the reaction products from each experiment. The XRD patterns of the ethylene
 glycol-saturated, oriented clay fractions can be used to determine alteration to the clay mineral
 structure (i.e., peak position shifts), which can be used to calculate clay mineral expandability. The
 ethylene glycol-saturated peak positions are presented in Table 10-4 and the XRD patterns are
 plotted in Figure 10-8. The illite content was estimated in the glycolated illite/smectite using the
 NEWMOD calibration from Leupin et al. (2014). In the bentonite-only experiments, there was little
 change in the percent illite, and a slight decrease in expandability (1-3%). However, in the
 experiments that included Grimsel granodiorite or ESD cement, the percentage of illite increased (4
 and 5 wt.%, respectively) and alongside a larger decrease in the clay expandability (10-13%).
 Glycolated smectite (GS) peak positions for the < 2 µm clay fraction separated from the FE and BCV
 bentonites. Expandability and percent illite (%I) were calculated based on the difference in position
 of the 002 and 003 glycolated smectite peaks.

 FE and BCV bentonites. Expandability and percent illite (%I) were calculated based on
 the difference in position of the 002 and 003 glycolated smectite peaks.

 10.3.3 XRF
 XRF analyses for bulk rock oxide chemistry were performed on the unreacted starting material and
 the bulk reaction products (Table 10-5). In experiments HBT-1 and 2, the weight percent oxide values
 only indicated slight variations between the unreacted and reacted bentonite. Both experiments
 experienced a decrease in SiO2 weight percent compared to their unreacted counterparts and HBT-2
 also decreased in LOI (loss on ignition) compared to the unreacted bentonite. The weight percent of
 Fe2O3 increased likely from the addition of redox buffers to the experiments.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                    203

 In the experiment containing FE bentonite + ESD cement (HBT-5), the most notable difference in the
 bulk FE bentonite compared to the unreacted FE bentonite was an increase in CaO and decrease in
 Na2O. This increase in CaO was due to the presence of cement in the experiment, whereas Na was
 retained in the aqueous solution (Figure 10-3). The BCV + ESD cement experiments (HBT-6 and -7)
 compared to the unreacted bulk BCV bentonite also had a notable increase in CaO, likely due to the
 cement, with the additional oxides experiencing little change.
