       10.3.4    SEM-EDS
 Along with EDS images of the altered bentonite and cement, BSE images of the epoxy mounted ESD
 were made to examine the cement for reaction rims.
 HBT-1. The reacted matrix of FE bentonite was observed to have a foily, ‘cornflake’ texture. Minor
 quartz and plagioclase were also identified (Rock et al., 2024, Figure F-19[B]). A layer of Fe-oxide
 coated the LCS included in the experiment (Rock et al., 2024, Figure F-19[C, D]).
 HBT-2. The reacted BCV clay matrix consisted mainly of smectite and was locally more Fe-rich than
 the FE bentonite reacted in HBT-1, consistent with the higher Fe content in the unreacted BCV
 bentonite (Table 10-5). Fe-carbonates were identified in the bentonite matrix and at the LCS surface
 (Rock et al., 2024, Figure F-20): the dominant cation in the carbonate species was largely Fe with
 accessory Ca and Mg identified in some populations by SEM-EDS and is referred to here as siderite.
 As well as the siderite, minor amounts of feldspar and quartz were also observed in the matrix and
 are interpreted to be inherited from the unreacted material. There was honeycomb-texture Fe-
 saponite on the LCS along with the isolated siderite crystals (Rock et al., 2024, Figure F-20[C-E]).
 HBT-5. SE images of the reacted FE bentonite showed little alteration and maintained the foily
 montmorillonite texture. There was minor quartz and feldspars embed in the smectite (Rock et al.,
       2024, Figure F-21).
 The SE images of the altered ESD cement show a calcium carbonate surface with larger calcium
 carbonate crystals and calcium oxide on the surface (Rock et al., 2024, Figure F-22).
 BSE images of the ESD cement showed that a reaction rim of ~20 µm formed at the cement-
 bentonite interface. After drying the cement and fixing the sample in epoxy, little of the bentonite
 matrix remained adhered to the cement surfaces (Figure 10-9). EDS analysis of the reaction rim
 showed it to be enriched in Si and Al compared to the underlying ESD cement.

November 8, 2024                                                                                              205

 Figure 10-9. Image from BSE analysis of ESD cement reacted in HBT-5, in contact with FE-
 bentonite. A reaction rim was observed at the cement surface as well as isolated
 sections of adhered smectite.

 HBT-6. The altered BCV bentonite from the six-week experiment had montmorillonite with a foily
 texture along with minor quartz, mica, and apatite embedded throughout (Rock et al., 2024, Figure
 F-23).
 SE images of the rough ESD surface showed a calcium carbonate matrix with SiO2 and clusters of Fe-
 rich calcium carbonate. Portions of the cement were covered in a Fe-rich smectite (Rock et al., 2024,
       Figure F-24).
 The BSE images of the epoxy mounted ESD revealed a small reaction rim at ~15 µm formed at the
 interface with the bentonite. Cracking of the cement was also observed near the edges and
 remnants of the bentonite were seen on the surface of the cement (Figure 10-10).

 Figure 10-10. Images from BSE analysis of ESD cement reacted in HBT-6, in contact with BCV
 bentonite. Isolated section of the cement with smectite adhered to the edges.

 HBT-7. The BCV bentonite for the 4-week experiment had few alterations and contained minor
 amounts of quartz, mica, and iron oxide (Rock et al., 2024, Figure F-25).

November 8, 2024                                                                                               206

 The rough surface of the ESD showed a majority of calcium carbonate with SiO2. Apatite was
 observed on the cement surface, likely originating from the BCV bentonite (Figure F-26).
 BSE images of the epoxy-mounted ESD showed rough, cracked edges and a small reaction rim (> 10
 µm). Sparse bentonite was observed on the outer edge (Figure 10-11).

 Figure 10-11. BSE images of the cross section of a ESD cement chip reacted from HBT-7 showing
 the rough and cracked edges.

 10.4 Discussion
 10.4.1 Previous Bentonite-Only Experimental Results
 Hydrothermal alteration of FE and BCV bentonite. Secondary electron SEM imaging of loose powder
 mounts of the reaction products show the development of a foily texture in the fine-grained clay
 matrix, especially of the FE bentonite. Minor recrystallization of montmorillonite to non-swelling
 phases, such as muscovite, was observed in the QXRD results in experiments that included BCV
 bentonite. Characterization of the smectite structure using XRD analyses of ethylene glycol-saturated
 mounts presented a more detailed look at the alteration of the smectite to non-swelling phases
 during the hydrothermal experiments. The experiment with FE bentonite and no BCV (HBT-1) saw a
 reduction in expandability of ~4% versus ~1% in the BCV bentonite and no FE (HBT-2) experiment.
 The equal portions of FE:BCV in HBT-3 experienced a reduction in expandability similar to HBT-1 (FE
 only). The formation of illite-smectite mixed layers was not detected in the XRD patterns of the clay
 separations (Figure 10-8).
 Steel interface mineral precipitation. Mineralized surfaces of steel coupons that were included in the
 experiments were characterized by SEM. These images demonstrate the reactive environment at the
 bentonite-metal interface in these experimental systems. The alteration mineralogy is likely
 controlled by the bulk geochemistry as demonstrated by the differences in mineral precipitation in
 the different experiments with FE and BCV bentonite (Figure 10-12). The initial corrosion of steel
 material created relatively rough surfaces that acted as a substrate for direct mineralization,
 resulting in the growth of surface-bound minerals in the localized environment surrounding the
 metal.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                207

 Figure 10-12. a) LCS included in HBT-1 with Fe-rich smectite on the surface and b) HBT-2 LCS
 overlayed with siderite and Fe-rich smectite

 FE Bentonite. After HBT-1, containing FE-only, the LCS was covered with black, orange, red, and
 white precipitants. The formation of a Fe-phyllosilicate corrosion product, Fe-saponite, was observed
 on the steel coupon surface during SEM analyses. These reaction products were only observed in a
 thin (< ~50 μm) rind on the reacted coupons. Unaltered montmorillonite was observed along with the
 Fe-saponite. Other observed Fe-rich corrosion products include Fe-oxides.
 In the Wyoming bentonite-only experiment series described in Cheshire et al. (2018), a magnetite-
 like oxide layer developed at the outermost surface of the steel coupon. In addition to the
 crystallization of this oxide product, precipitation of Fe-saponite was observed. Fe-saponite forms a
 more reactive zone with a higher surface area than an unaltered steel surface. The Fe-saponite in
 these experiments likely precipitated directly on the steel as the solution interacted with smectite in
 the local environment surrounding the steel coupons, given the slight increase in aqueous Fe
 concentration from the beginning to the end of the experiment in lieu of the presence of steel. The
 clay matrix was not enriched in Fe like the clay observed at the steel interface. The combination of a
 low final concentration of Fe in the bulk system solution (> 0.50 mg L-1) and the presence of a new
 Fe-rich phase supports that the reaction at the interface was generally localized and did not
 significantly affect the chemistry of the bulk experimental system.
 BCV Bentonite. In the BCV-only experiments, HBT-2, abundant rhombohedral siderite with
 intergrowing smectite at the LCS surfaces was apparent in SEM imaging (e.g., Rock et al., 2024,
 Figure F-20[C]). Previous work identified the formation of a magnetite inner layer under siderite
 formed at the surface of LCS during alteration in the presence of bentonite/clay-rich environments
 (Romaine et al., 2015; El Mendili et al., 2015; Necib et al., 2017). Though not qualified in this report,
 magnetite may have formed in our experiments. Future characterization of a cross section of the
 steel coupons will make that determination.
 The transportation of the dissolved Fe species produced by the corrosion of the steel was limited to
 the clay-steel interface. This corrosion product formed a siderite-smectite layer, creating a
 transitional layer between the LCS and the bentonite groundmass. One of the suggested
 precipitation mechanisms to describe the corrosion of LCS to the precipitation of siderite is (El
       Mendili et al., 2013):
                                CO2 (aq) + H2 O (l) ↔ 2H2 CO3 (aq)                                 Eqn. (10-1)

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   208

                                 H2 CO3 (aq) ↔ HCO-3 (aq) + H+(aq)
                                                                                                 Eqn. (10-2)
 or
                                  HCO3 (aq) ↔ CO2-
 -
 3 (aq) + H
 +

                                                                                                 Eqn. (10-3)
                                    Fe (s) ↔ Fe2+       -
                                               (aq) + 2e (aq)

 2-
                                  Fe2+(aq) + CO3 (aq) ↔ FeCO3 (s)
                                                                                                 Eqn. (10-4)
 or
 -
                              Fe (aq) + HCO3 (aq) ↔ FeCO3 (s) + H+
 2+

 The carbon species (CO2, HCO3-, CO32-) in aqueous solutions are determined primarily by pH. At
 25 °C, CO2 is prevalent at low pH, HCO3- at circumneutral (5.9 < pH < 8.9), and CO32- at high pH
 (El Mendili et al., 2014). Based on the measured pH values during the experiments, the reaction
 most likely followed the bicarbonate reaction path, leading to the precipitation of siderite. The
 formation of carbonates through this reaction path would increase the pH of the solution through the
 formation of H+ (Eqn. (10-4), consistent with the gradually decreasing pH throughout the
 experimental durations after the first week of reaction time, where measured (for example, HBT-3
 and -4).
 SEM-EDS analyses of the precipitate at the LCS surfaces indicate that the siderite has Ca
 incorporated into its structure. The incorporation of Ca into the siderite structure for an Fe-Ca
 carbonate product (FexCayCO3) causes an in-step or spiral dislocation, similar to the manner of
 growth seen for calcite and may lead to a rounded type of the siderite crystal morphology
 (Matamoros-Veloza et al., 2020). This rounded morphology is evident in the SEM images of the
 siderite crystals at the LCS surface (Rock et al., 2024, Figure F-20).
 The dense Fe-mineral layer on the steel surface that forms during alteration may act as a protective
 corrosion product film because of a high relative thermodynamic stability and because the kinetic
 rates of dissolution of these minerals are many orders of magnitude lower than that of steel at
 circumneutral pH (Palandri & Kharaka, 2004). The formation of siderite at the steel surfaces in the
 HBT experiments may then have acted to limit the amount of dissolved Fe from the LCS and
 maintain a low Fe concentration in the bulk solution (< 0.51 mg L-1 observed during the HBT
 experiments), additionally mediated by the circumneutral pH.

 10.4.2 FY23 and FYY4 Results
 The EBS International experiment completed in FY23 and FY24 added ESD cement as a reactant
 with the FE and BCV bentonite and the synthesized Grimsel groundwater solution. These
 experiments ran for four or six weeks as opposed to the eight-week duration of previous bentonite-
 only experiments to capture the incipient mineral alteration processes in the solid reactants. The
 results are additionally compared to the previously completed experiments HBT-1 and -2, and to the
 results of experiments CEM-3 through -5 completed as part of the EBS R&D FY24 work package
 discussed in Section 10.

 10.4.2.1 Aqueous Geochemistry
 As with experiments reacting ESD cement in CEM-3 through -5, pH increased in HBT-5 as compared
 to the FE bentonite experiments (HBT-1) and the BCV + ESD experiments (HBT-6 &-7). During the
 first week of reaction time, however, in HBT-5 the pH initially decreased by 1.5 (from 8.5 to ~7, as
 measured at benchtop conditions) before increasing again to pH > 9. The BCV + ESD experiment
 experienced the same decrease, but both stabilized after ~190 hours and held a pH of ~6.6 for the
 duration of the experiment. The buffering capacity of montmorillonite through (de)protonation
 reactions at edge sites has some capacity to control pH, as seen in all the HBT experiments that

November 8, 2024                                                                                               209

 have very similar steady-state pH values of ~6. However, this capacity for pH buffering in HBT-5 may
 have been overwhelmed by the release of OH- from ESD cement alteration, as evidenced by the
 increased pH after the first week of reaction time.
 Increases in Al and Si observed in HBT-5 through -7 were also substantially greater than those in
 HBT-1 and -2 (Figure 10-2). The increased concentrations are not solely attributable to the addition
 of cement, since, while Al trends are similar to those of the CEM-series experiments discussed in
 Section 10 that included ESD cement with no bentonite reactants, the Si concentrations in HBT-5
 are substantially higher. This suggests another mechanism of increasing Si and Al concentrations in
 HBT-5. At a given temperature, increasing pH above circumneutral results in increased silicate
 solubility. At the conditions of the experiments, however, the in-situ pH in any of the experiments are
 not substantially above circumneutral: in-situ pH at 200 °C may range from ~5 in HBT- 1 and -2, to
 ~6.5 in HBT-5. The cause of the elevated Si concentrations in HBT-5 is then currently not identified.
 Future work will focus on ongoing microscale and bulk characterizations of the solid reacted
 products (FE bentonite and ESD cement), including EMP analyses of the major and minor mineral
 phases to evaluate preferential dissolution or alteration of the materials in HBT-5. Additionally, future
 analyses of dissolved inorganic carbon will aid geochemical models that calculate in-situ pH to
 develop a stronger understanding of the processes involved in barrier material alteration.
 The lack of sampling during experiments HBT-1 and -2 means that aqueous geochemistry trends
 over the course of the experiments were not recorded. The first and final samples of each of these
 experiments give some insight into the aqueous processes, however, and it is worth briefly noting
 here that the few collected samples from these experiments suggest that most elements analyzed in
 HBT-1 and -2 appeared to follow similar trends, if not match exact values, to samples collected from
 HBT-5, that also included FE bentonite. Similarly, the few samples collected from HBT-2 appear to
 follow trends analogous to those observed in HBT-6 and -7 that also reacted BCV bentonite. These
 initial results show reproducible impacts of each of the reacted barrier materials, especially the type
 of bentonite used (here, FE or BCV). Future work will focus on applying these results to model
 predictable outcomes for EBS stability based on the contributions of and interactions between
 different barrier materials.

 10.4.2.2 Cement-Bentonite Co-Alteration
 The cylinder of ESD cement that was reacted in HBT-5 with FE bentonite formed a reaction rim at the
 cement-solution interface during the four-week experiment. Chemical mapping through SEM-ESD
 showed most of the rim to be enriched in Si and Al (Figure 10-13): isolated locations of Ca-rich
 products in the reaction rim were also identified. This result is similar to that observed in the reaction
 rim formed in ESD cement reacted at 200 °C in CEM-3 (Section 9.4.3.4). The potential mechanisms
 for this alteration processes are discussed above: in brief, elevated temperature may result in higher
 Si to Ca ratios responsive to cement mineral dissolution as well as lower CO2 solubility in solution,
 increasing the potential for silicate precipitation as opposed to calcite formation through cement
 carbonatation processes. In HBT-5, the impact of co-alteration with FE bentonite is apparent in the
 appearance of Al in the reaction rim, which was not observed in the cement reacted with bentonite in
 CEM-3.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                    210

 Figure 10-13. ESD cement round post-reaction in HBT-5. a. BSE image of the cement mount in
 cross section, showing an area mapped for chemical analysis in the inset box. b. EDS
 map of Si counts mapped in from the area in the inset. c. EDS map of Al counts in the
 from the same area.

 The ESD cement cylinders included in the BCV experiments (HBT-6 and -7) for 4 and 6 weeks did not
 form a reaction rim like HBT-5. Bentonite adhered to the cement had increased Ca concentrations
 compared to the unreacted BCV bentonite (Figure 10-14). The SEM line scans performed on the
 edges of the cement indicated slight decalcification on the cement in contact with the bentonite
 (Figure 10-14). The XRF analyses of the reacted bentonite showed an increase of Ca in the bulk
 product along with a slight increase of aqueous Ca (~20-30 mg L-1) over the duration of the
 experiment. Decalcification is the loss of calcium through chemical reaction and is a degradation
 mechanism of cementitious materials. The decalcification of the ESD cement likely occurred from
 the leaching by soft waters, which are devoid of calcium, magnesium, etc., with a circumneutral pH
 (Faucon et al., 1998). Calcium hydroxide (CH) and calcium silicate hydrate (CSH) minerals in cement
 are important calcium-bearing hydrated phases that are susceptible to decalcification. The
 dissolution of CH minerals commonly occurs first, and only when CH is inaccessible or depleted does
 decalcification of CSH minerals occur (Chen et al., 2006). From SEM and XRD analyses of the
 cement, lime (CH) was rarely observed, compared to the observance of CSH minerals (i.e.,
 tobermorite). This was not the case in the CEM experiments at 200 °C and a decrease of Ca was not
 observed on the edges of the ESD cement mounts.

November 8, 2024                                                                                             211

 Figure 10-14. (a) BSE images of ESD cement round post-reaction from HBT-6 with bentonite
 adhered to the cement edge. (b). SEM-SE images of bentonite on the ESD cement
 surface from HBT-6. (c). EDS line scan of Ca counts along the red line showing a
 slight decrease of Ca at the edge of the ESD cement round from HBT-7.

 The identification of the secondary minerals is the focus of ongoing work characterizing the surface
 of the cement and reacted chips through Raman spectroscopy and SEM, as well as analysis of the
 reaction rim by EMP. Based on the CSH phases that formed abundantly in other cement experiments
 conducted at 200 °C and the chemical analyses to date, the reaction rim here is expected to be at
 least in part composed of CSH and C(A)SH minerals.
 Alkali elements in HBT-5 followed similar trends the FE bentonite-only experiment (HBT-1). The same
 observation can be made for HBT-6 and -7 to the BCV-only experiment. For all experiments, K
 increased throughout the experiment duration, but FE experiments exhibited slower rates and lower
 concentrations of K than observed in the BCV experiments. Calcium increased over the entire
 experimental duration at varying rates and Mg quickly decreased to below detection limits (0.1 mg
 L-1) within the first week of reaction time for all experiments. Overall, these trends suggest that K and
 Ca concentrations are greater in solutions that interact with BCV bentonite, while Na concentrations
 are greater in experiments with FE bentonite.
 From these results, it appears that the concentrations of alkali elements are largely controlled by the
 type of bentonite included as a reactant rather than the added cement at the experimental
 conditions. However, there are indications that the addition of ESD cement may be contributing to
 alteration processes occurring in the bentonite matrix. In CEM-3, which reacted only ESD cement in
 solution at similar experimental conditions to HBT-5 at 200 °C, the K concentration increased rapidly
 to > 40 mg L-1 within the first week of reaction time, while Na remained relatively conservative after a
 slight increase (to ~150 mg L-1) in the first few days (Figure 10-15). In comparison, HBT-5 exhibited
 reversed trends: K increased slowly over the experimental duration to ~10 mg L-1, while Na
 increased rapidly to ~350 mg L-1 before the concentration attained an approximate steady-state
 after ~2 weeks. HBT-7 experienced a trend similar to CEM-3, suggesting that BCV bentonite has less
 of an impact on the alkali elements in solution. The paired increase of Na concentrations with the
 evidence that K is being released from the ESD cement suggests that Na/K ion exchange occurred in
 the clays of the bentonite matrix reacted in HBT-5. This process potentially contributed to alteration
 of the Na-montmorillonite, observed in the decreased smectite content post-reaction (Section
       10.3.2.1, Table 10-3).

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   212

 Figure 10-15. K and Na concentrations from CEM-3, that reacted ESD cement with synthetic
 Grimsel groundwater at 200 C; and HBT-5, that reacted the same ESD cement with
 FE bentonite in synthetic Grimsel groundwater.

 The comparison between experimental results from HBT-5 to HBT-6 and -7 also highlights potential
 for Na/K exchange in smectite minerals during interaction between different bentonites. This
 suggests the potential for the FE bentonite to act as a sink for K and source for Na through cation
 exchange between clays as well as to the bulk solution. This could be the reason for the greater loss
 in expandability in HBT-5 (~10%) versus HBT-6 and -7 (~5%) since illitization is limited by K
 availability in the system, with greater K concentrations promoting more illitization (Mills et al.,
 2023). Future work describing the mass balance in each of these experiments can then be used to
 compare to reaction path models of EBS interaction. The results may also be applied to
 understanding the effect of aqueous phase equilibration on the EBS materials used in the HotBENT
 full-scale experiment, where BCV and FE bentonite will have had long-term interactions.

 10.5 Conclusion
 The completed experiments document the potential mineralogical and geochemical changes in the
 FE and BCV bentonite at 200 ºC and water saturated conditions (HBT-1 and -2). The main observed
 effects of heating for the eight-week period were the slight reduction in montmorillonite swelling
 capacity, the formation of carbonate minerals in the BCV-only experiment, and the lack of formation
 of silicate minerals such as zeolites. Low carbon steel coupons included in the experiments were
 variably corroded and provided a substrate for Fe-rich mineral precipitation. Fe-oxides and
 phyllosilicates were observed in the experiment with FE bentonite only. In experiments with BCV, Fe-
 rich carbonates (siderite) were observed on the reacted steel surface in addition to oxides and Fe-
 saponite.
 Completed analyses for these and future experiments using BCV bentonite will focus on the
 mechanisms that drive FeCO3 formation at the steel surfaces. Future experiments may focus
 additionally on how the pH trends caused by the addition of cement products affect this and other
 reactions in the FE-BCV bentonite mixture in a crystalline environment. Completing experiment sets
 at lower temperatures (90 ºC, the predicted temperature at the buffer/rock interface) will also allow
 insight into any threshold behaviors for the reactions interpreted to occur in our experiments,
 especially considering the lower stability of carbonate minerals with decreasing temperature,
 referred to as retrograde solubility.
 The results of the experiments including ESD cement (HBT-5 to -7) interacting with FE and BCV
 bentonites with a low-alkali cement (ESD cement) resulted in a bulk solution pH of ~9 (as measured
 at 25 ºC) in the FE + ESD cement experiment (HBT-5), elevated from bulk solution pH values of ~6 in

November 8, 2024                                                                                               213

 the other HBT experiments that did not react cement. Increased Na concentrations and depressed K
 concentrations compared to the anticipated contribution from the ESD cement also suggested cation
 exchange in the smectite phases. The reacted FE bentonite additionally had a smectite content that
 decreased by ~3% after hydrothermal interaction with the ESD cement. The reacted ESD cement
 after co-alteration with bentonite was observed to have formed a thin (~20 µm) Si-Al enriched
 reaction rim apparently comprised of C(A)SH minerals at the cement-solution boundary. The ESD in
 the BCV experiments (HBT-6 and -7; 4 and 6 weeks, respectively) did not form a reaction rim like
 HBT-5. Bentonite adhered to the cement had increased Ca concentrations and there were
 indications of slight decalcification on the cement in contact with the bentonite. The aqueous
 geochemistry behaved similarly to the CEM experiment of ESD cement-only at 200 °C, indicating the
 type of bentonite present influenced the bulk chemistry of the system.
 Future work will focus on extracting geochemical effects driven by each of the reactants (FE
 bentonite, BCV bentonite, steel, host rock, and cement) on the hydrothermal chemistry of EBS
 systems over time. Isolating the effect of each reactant will facilitate predictive modelling that can be
 validated by comparison to the short-term results of the HBT experiments discussed here, as well as
 with future results from the long-term HotBENT experiment. Future experiments will be designed to
 isolate any ambiguous processes with the goal of contributing constraints on reaction rates and
 products to geochemical databases characterizing in-situ EBS alteration over repository timescales.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   214

 11 UPDATED THERMAL-HYDROLOGIC SIMULATIONS OF FULL-SCALE
 HEATER EXPERIMENT IN OPALINUS CLAY
 11.1 Introduction
 Thermal-Hydrologic (TH) modeling of DECOVALEX 2023, Task C has now been completed. The work
 involved modeling of the full-scale emplacement experiment at the Mont Terri Underground Rock
 Laboratory (Nagra, 2019). The experiment was designed in part to investigate thermal-hydrological-
 mechanical (THM) processes resulting from emplacement of spent fuel and high-level nuclear waste
 in a geological repository in Opalinus clay host rock (Figure 11-1 and Figure 12-2). In DECOVALEX-
 2023, Task C numerical models were built to study the various processes such as bentonite thermal
 conditions and pore pressure changes in the Opalinus clay because of heating using experimental
 dataset.
 The Task C final report, which covers the work over four years, will be published in the near future.
 Part of SNL’s modeling work has been published in Hadgu et al. (2024). A Task C synthesis article
 has been submitted to Geomechanics for Energy and the Environment journal, and part of the work
 will be presented at the 9th Clay Conference, Hannover, Germany, Nov. 25-28, 2024. This report
 summarizes SNL’s progress in TH modeling since the FY23 report and summarizes completion of
 steps 1 and 2. The SNL Team did not participate in Step 3 modeling, which is on extended heating
 prediction for possible longer heating schedule of the FE experiment.
 Figure 11-2 shows a schematic representation of the experimental setup, with the experiment tunnel
 represented as a cylinder, and heaters placed in the tunnel with the centerline of the heater aligned
 to the centerline of the tunnel. The Opalinus clay is bedded and has anisotropic properties in
 directions parallel and perpendicular to the bedding plane. The bedding plane dips at 34˚ from the
 horizontal (Figure 11-3). Model domain of 50 m ×50 m ×50 m mesh was used for TH simulations
 with different mesh sizes applied for different simulations. One of the generated meshes is shown in
 Figure 11-4. Figure 11-5 shows representation of the experimental tunnel in the TH simulations.
 Step 1 is on modeling the heating phase of the FE experiment with changes in pore pressure in the
 opalinus clay host rock and comparison of model results with experimental data. A large
 experimental dataset was provided with temperature, pressure and relative humidity at various
 locations in the experiment covering a period of five years. In Step 1, modeling the experiment is
 represented using 3-D models. Model calibration was performed using the experimental data.
 Calibration TH simulations were conducted using PFLOTRAN (Hammond et al., 2014) numerical code
 and a refined 3-D mesh. For Step 1 simulations material and fluid properties that are part of the
 PFLOTRAN code (Hammond et al., 2014) were mainly used. For water properties, the equation-of-
 state from the International Formulation Committee (1997) was used. Output results mostly closely
 matched the experimental data.
 Step 2 focused on modeling the FE experiment starting from the ventilation phase and continuing to
 the heating phase. The objective of Step 2 was to continue to refine calibrations of the models
 representing the FE experiment. For this phase the SNL team added representation of the ventilation
 of the FE tunnel prior to backfilling followed by shotcrete lining of the tunnel and backfilling.
 Experimental data of the ventilation period were used to further calibrate the 3-D model and
 generate initial conditions for the heating phase. Calibrations of both the ventilation and heating
 phases in Step 2 refined the model generated in Step 1. Selected simulation results for the
 ventilation and heating phases are shown in Figures 12-3 to 12-5. (Figure 12-7 shows locations of
 selected observation points). Figures 12- 6 show predictions of pressure evolution at Observation
 Points 2 and 6 during the ventilation phase. Figures 12-8 and 12-9 show predictions of temperature
 and pressure evolution at Observation Point 6 during the heating phase.

November 8, 2024                                                                                               215

 Figure 11-1. The FE experiment at the Mont Terri underground laboratory.

 11.2 Step 1c Modeling
 The Step 1c TH modeling simulations were initiated using properties developed in Step 0. Properties
 were then varied during model calibration using experimental data. For the TH simulations the
 numerical code PFLOTRAN (Hammond et al., 2014) was used. Simulations were run for five years
 from the start of heating.

 11.2.1 Step 1c Model Geometry
 Figure 11-2 shows a representation of the experimental setup, with the experiment tunnel
 represented as a cylinder, and heaters placed in the tunnel with the centerline of the heater aligned
 to the centerline of the tunnel. The opalinus clay is bedded and has anisotropic properties in
 directions parallel and perpendicular to bedding. The bedding dips at 34˚ from the horizontal as
 shown in Figure 11-3. To include shotcrete around the walls of the experimental tunnel and to add
 mesh refinements, a new 50 m × 50 m × 50 m mesh was generated. The new mesh size has
 1,497,366 elements (Figure 11-4). A 30 cm thick shotcrete was included, represented with two
 elements in the radial direction, as shown in Figure 11-5. Solving the coupled flow and energy
 multiphase equations and the large mesh size required use of high-performance computing.

 Figure 11-2. Schematic diagram of model geometry for Step 1 simulations.

November 8, 2024                                                                                               216

 Figure 11-3. Model geometry for Step 0 (cross-section view of tunnel).

 Figure 11-4. Geometry and meshing used for Step 1c simulations.

 Figure 11-5. Representation of the experimental tunnel, Step 1c simulations

 11.2.2 Simulation Model Setup
 For Step1c, simulations start with the heating phase using the heating schedule given in Table 11-1.
 Representation of the experimental tunnel for the simulations is shown in Figure 11-5. Activities prior
 to the heating phase such as the ventilation phase have not yet been considered. Thus, the initial
 condition used for simulations is not the undisturbed state. Approximations were made to capture an

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 217

 initial condition that closely fits the condition at the beginning of heating. The following initial and
 boundary conditions were assumed:
 • The opalinus clay was assumed to be initially at a temperature of 16.5 °C and pore water
 pressure of 2 MPa.
 • Hydrostatic pressure was applied.
 • Anisotropy in permeability and thermal conductivity was applied because of the bedding.
 • The granular bentonite and the bentonite blocks were assumed to be initially at a temperature of
 18 °C with different saturation conditions, liquid saturation of 0.65 and 0.18, respectively. Those
 conditions match the initial relative humidity at each material.
 • The concrete was assumed to be at an initial liquid saturation of 0.1.
 • Diffusion Coefficient values of 2.0 x 10-9 m2/s for the liquid phase and 2.0 x 10-5 m2/s for the gas
 phase were used.
 • Boundary Condition: based on the heater power schedule given in Table 11-1, heating was
 started with Heater 1 only, with a stepped increase to the maximum power (W). Heaters 2 and 3
 were then added at Days 64 and 65, respectively.
 Table 11-1. Heating schedule for the three heaters (Task C Specifications).
 Date Power (W)
                   Heater 1                               12/15/2014                    500
                                                          01/13/2015                    1000
                                                          02/16/2015                    1350
                   Heater 2                               02/17/2015                    1350
                   Heater 3                               02/18/2015                    1350

 For Step 1c simulations, material and fluid properties that are part of the PFLOTRAN code
 (Hammond et al., 2014) were mainly used. For TH simulations water and steam properties Equation-
 of-State (EOS) from the International Formulating Committee (IF97, 1997) were used. Water density,
 steam density and enthalpy are calculated as a function of temperature and pressure. Viscosity is
 calculated as a function of temperature, pressure, and saturation pressure. Vapor diffusion is a
 function of pressure and temperature. The diffusion coefficient is calculated using the equation by
 Vargaftik (1975) and Walker et al. (1981).
 In PFLOTRAN heat capacity is represented as a constant value. The Task C provided relationship for
 thermal conductivity as a function of saturation, along with the default function in PFLOTRAN were
 used. The default thermal conductivity equation in PFLOTRAN is (Somerton et al., 1974) is given as:

                               𝜆𝑡ℎ = 𝜆𝑑𝑟𝑦 + √𝑠(𝜆𝑠𝑎𝑡 − 𝜆𝑑𝑟𝑦 )                                          Eqn. (11-1)

 where,
 • 𝜆𝑡ℎ = thermal conductivity
 • 𝜆𝑑𝑟𝑦 = dry thermal conductivity
 • 𝜆𝑠𝑎𝑡 = fully saturated thermal conductivity

November 8, 2024                                                                                                    218

 11.3 Step 1c Simulation Results
 Updated TH simulations were made for Step 1c. As described above, parameter values were varied
 to calibrate the model using experimental data. Selected calibration simulation results are presented
 below. The calibrated properties for granular bentonite, bentonite blocks and opalinus clay are
 shown in Table 11-2. The calibrated shotcrete properties were permeability of 2.0E-21 m2, thermal
 conductivity of 0.3 (dry)/0.5 (wet) W/m K and pore compressibility of 3.5E-12 1/Pa. Figure 11-6
 shows predicted distribution of temperature after 1800 days of simulation, where Figure 12-6.a
 shows a cross-section along the tunnel axis and Figure 12-6.b displays a cross-section perpendicular
 to tunnel axis, showing the effect of the anisotropy in the opalinus clay. In addition, mesh refinement
 and the presence of the shotcrete with the selected properties has affected results. Figure 11-6 and
 figures of distribution of pressure and saturation show that the selected domain size for TH
 simulations minimizes boundary effects.

 11.3.1 Results in the Bentonite
 The selected observation points (2,3,6,7 and 9) shown in Figure 11-7 in the granular bentonite
 represent the locations from where the results are to be displayed. Figure 11-8 and Figure 11-9
 show early time evolution of temperature and gas saturation, respectively. The plots illustrate
 processes dominant at early time at different locations close to Heater 2. Heating starts at Heater 2
 after 64 days of simulation time (Table 11-1), thus, temperatures are near constant during this
 period. Once heating is started temperatures rise at the observation points according to their
 proximity to the heater and their position with respect to the opalinus clay bedding (anisotropy).
 Figure 11-9 shows the respective gas saturation evolutions. The period before the start of heating is
 marked by gradual saturation of the granular bentonite due to flow from the opalinus clay. At the
 start of heating, points close to the heater see immediate drying up due to conduction and vapor
 diffusion. Points further from the heater show a sharp drop of gas saturation before rising. As with
 temperature, gas saturation is also affected due to position with respect to the opalinus clay
 bedding.
 Predictions of temperature and relative humidity, along with experimental data, are shown in Figure
 11-10 and Figure 11-11, respectively. The predicted results using calibrated properties are close to
 the experimental. Figure 11-12 shows the effect of shotcrete thermal conductivity on temperature at
 point 7. The data matched better when lower thermal conductivity values were used for shotcrete.

 11.5 Summary and Future Work
 The FE Experiment provided in situ heater experimental data supporting the study of nuclear waste
 disposal in argillite rock. The modeling analysis supported by field data resulted in better
 understanding of TH processes and pore pressure development. Comparison of modeling results
 with other DECOVALEX2023 Task C teams also helped refine prediction methods. Use of high-
 performance computing allowed solving the strongly coupled fluid and energy equations and the use
 of a large mesh.
 Calibration modeling for Step 1c using a 3-D mesh that includes shotcrete and experimental data,
 was reported in Matteo et al. (2023). Step 2 simulations which constitutes modeling of the
 ventilation phase followed by the heating phase are reported in this report. New calibrations using
 Step 2 modeling of both the ventilation and heating phases updated parameters developed in Step
 1c. The simulations described in this report assumed that the FE experiment can be represented
 using TH processes. There is a need to quantify the effect of neglecting rock mechanics on
 simulation results.

November 8, 2024                                                                                             228

 12 DEVELOPMENT OF MODIFIED BOEHMITE MATERIALS AS ANION
 SORBENT ADDITIVES IN EBS DESIGNS
 12.1 Introduction
 Activities in FY24 involved molecular modeling and experimental characterization of organically
 modified boehmite (glycoboehmite, GB) for the adsorption of anionic radionuclides, e.g. iodine, in
 EBS designs. Layered metal oxyhydroxides such as boehmite (-AlOOH) offer a platform to develop
 hybrid nanomaterials with modified interlayers for specific adsorption applications. Organic
 modification with butanediol expands the interlayer and increases the accessible surface area, result
 in new ion adsorption sites. As summarized below, efforts in FY24 resulted in two journal articles and
 several conference presentations. Additionally, a graduate student intern performed simulations of
 ion interactions with external surfaces of GB.
 Highlights from FY24 activities:
 • Molecular modeling validated with experimental characterization revealed details of the
 expanded interlayer structure of GB with intercalated butanediol molecules.
 • Batch sorption experiments and spectroscopic characterization showed that Ni-functionalized GB
 has a high adsorption capacity for aqueous iodide.
 Manuscripts submitted or published in FY24:
 • Kruichak, J.N., Bell, N.S., Greathouse, J.A., Weck, P.F., Rodriguez, M.A., Rosenberg, S.A., Juba, B.,
 Lam, M.N., Wang, Y., and Matteo, E.N. (2024) Anionic Contaminant Capture using Nickel-
 functionalized Glycoboehmite. J. Colloid Interface Sci., in review.
 • Greathouse, J.A., Weck, P.F., Bell, N.S., Kruichak, J.N., and Matteo, E.N. (2024) Structural and
 Spectroscopic Properties of Butanediol-Modified Boehmite Materials. J. Phys. Chem. C 128,
 3533.
 Conference presentations in FY24:
 • Greathouse, J.A. Molecular Modeling of Adsorption and Hydration Properties of Layered Minerals.
 American Chemical Society Fall 2024 Meeting.
 • Kruichak, J.N. Evaluation of Novel Materials for Anionic Sorption. Clay Minerals Society 2024
 Annual Meeting.
 • Winetrout, J. Molecular Simulations of Engineered Barrier Systems with Enhanced Anionic
 Radionuclide Sorption. Sandia National Laboratories 2024 Student Symposium.

 12.2 Structural and Spectroscopic Properties of Glycoboehmite from
 Molecular Modeling and Infrared Spectroscopy
 Classical molecular dynamics (CMD) simulations were combined with quantum calculations (density
 functional theory, DFT) to determine the interlayer properties of GB as a function of butanediol
 loading. Atomistic detail provided by these results is needed to determine the interlayer structure
 needed for synthetic control of GB material properties. Results from our published journal article on
 structural and spectroscopic properties of GB (Greathouse et al, 2024) are summarized below.
 CMD simulations were first used to determine the interlayer structure as butanediol loading was
 increased up to 2 diols per unit cell. As seen in Figure 12-1, models consisted of chemisorbed
 butanediol molecules with one alcohol group available to form hydrogen bonds with the adjacent
 boehmite surface. Following an initial expansion when butanediol molecules are first introduced, the

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  229

 layer spacing gradually increases as more molecules fill in the interlayer region. An additional
 expansion occurs at higher loadings as the butanediol monolayers become separated, resulting in a
 hydrophilic region in the interlayer. The green triangle in Figure 12-1 represents the experimentally
 measured layer spacing from powder XRD (Inoue et al, 1989), and red diamonds represent layer
 spacings from DFT geometry optimization.

 Figure 12-1. Results from CMD simulations of glycoboehmite structure at 300 K

 Comparison of experimentally measured IR spectra with those of DFT-optimized structures aid in
 determining the interlayer structure of the synthesized material. The C-H stretch (~2700 – 2950
 cm−1) and O-H stretch modes (~3100 – 3300 cm−1) confirm the presence of butanediol molecules in
 the synthesized samples. As seen in Figure 12-2, the O-H stretch modes at 3088-3092 cm−1 and
 3295-3297 cm−1 measured in bulk boehmite are red shifted compared to isolated aluminol groups
 due to significant H-bonding between AlOOH layers. The FTIR spectrum shows that the same O-H
 stretch mode is significantly broadened in GB, due to the reduction in hydrogen bonding.

November 8, 2024                                                                                               230

 Figure 12-2. Infrared spectra of GB simulated using density functional perturbation theory (DFPT)
 from the DFT-optimized structure (blue) and Fourier transform infrared (FTIR) spectra
 (black).

 12.3 Anion Sorption Properties of Metal-Exchanged Glycoboehmite
 Materials
 Extensive characterization of cation and anion adsorption was performed to develop a material
 specifically designed for iodide adsorption that is compatible with current EBS designs. A critical step
 in designing an anion sorbent based on GB is metal functionalization. The synthesis and
 characterization of metal-functionalized GB is outlined in Figure 12-3. Batch sorption studies
 determined that GB adsorbs Ni2+ in far greater quantities than other divalent cations, and that Ni-
 functionalized GB shows exceptional uptake of I−. Results from our submitted manuscript on anion
 uptake of Ni-functionalized GB (Kruichak et al., 2024) are summarized below.

 Figure 12-3. Illustration of the glycoboehmite (GB) reaction scheme, including postsynthetic metal
 functionalization.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                  231

 Material characterization using X-ray fluorescence, X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy, and elemental
 analysis were used to determine the amount of organic component (butanediol) and metal cation
 (Ni2+) in the functionalized material. The resulting chemical formula for synthesized GB is
 AlOOH(C4H8OH)0.15, where AlOOH represents the boehmite substrate and C4H8OH represents a
 chemisorbed butanediol molecule. Based on the formula, approximately 15 % of the surface hydroxyl
 groups in boehmite are replaced by butanediol groups in GB. An additional 0.26 mol% potassium (K)
 remains in the synthesized material based on the use of KOH as a mineralizer during synthesis.
 Quantitative analysis of divalent cation sorption in Table 12-1 indicate that cation uptake occurs
 through an unusual cation exchange process. Similar amounts of K+ are desorbed even though
 different amounts of divalent cation are adsorbed. These results also show that Ni2+ is preferentially
 adsorbed compared with Mg2+ or Ca2+.
 Table 12-1. Uptake of Mg2+, Ca2+, and Ni2+ by GB and accompanying desorption of K+ and other
 cations.
 GB material Metal uptake (mg/g) Potassium Aluminum Sodium
 release release (mg/g) release
                                                                          (mg/g)                              (mg/g)
 NiCl2 reacted Boehmite 0.32 (Ni2+)
        MgCl2 reacted GB                 1.92(Mg2+)                       9.46               0.08             0.52
        CaCl2 reacted GB                 3.99(Ca2+)                       9.30               0.08             0.53
        NiCl2 reacted GB             9.09(Ni2+)                           9.93               0.20             0.86

 Iodide sorption results shown in Table 12-2 highlight the significant effect of metal
 functionalization—particularly Ni2+—on I− sorption. Without pH adjustment, I− uptake by Ni-GB is
 accompanied by a significant decrease in solution pH, suggesting that a surface charging
 mechanism is responsible for the exceptional anion sorption. Both unreacted GB and Ni-
 functionalized boehmite also show good I− uptake at pH 7. We note that the surface area of Ni-
 boehmite (285 m2/g) is more than double that of Ni-GB (120 m2/g), so the surface normalized I−
 uptake of Ni-GB is far greater than Ni-boehmite.

 Zeta potential titrations (Figure 12-4) offer preliminary insight into the mechanisms controlling the
 adsorption of Ni2+ and subsequent uptake of I−. Upon Ni2+ functionalization, the GB surface charge
 becomes more negative, suggesting the presence of Ni hydrolysis species such as Ni(OH)+ on the GB
 surface.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                        232

 Figure 12-4. Average value and standard deviation of GB zeta potential as a function of pH.

 Additional characterization and molecular modeling provide insight into cation and anion adsorption
 mechanisms. X-ray (XRD) and infrared spectroscopy indicate that there is little change in GB
 structure after metal functionalization (Figure 12-5). Thus, potential adsorption sites for cations are
 likely to be in the interlayer region or at external surfaces.

 Figure 12-5. Powder XRD stack plots (left) and FT-IR spectra (right) for unreacted GB compared
 with metal-functionalized GB showing similar structure for all materials.
 CMD simulations indicate a slight increase in layer spacing for Ni-functionalized GB (12.2 Å)
 compared with unreacted GB (11.0 Å). These layer spacing values are in good agreement with XRD
 (11.7 Å). Accompanying DFT simulations also reveal a possible adsorption scenario in the interlayer
 region in which Ni2+ coordinated by alcohol groups directly binds I− (Figure 12-6).

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 233

 Figure 12-6. Snapshot from a DFT simulation showing Ni-I interactions in the GB interlayer.
 Hydrogen bonds are indicated by dashed lines. Color legend: Al, cyan; C, brown; H,
 white; I, yellow; Ni, grey; O, red.

 12.4 Summary and Future Work
 We have combined molecular modeling with experiment to develop modified boehmite materials for
 the adsorption of anionic radionuclides such as I− in EBS environments. Molecular modeling and
 infrared spectroscopy were used to investigate the structural and vibrational properties of
 glycoboehmite (GB) materials containing varying amounts of interlayer butanediol molecules that are
 chemisorbed on the basal surfaces of layered AlOOH (boehmite). Molecular modeling was also used
 to explore structural properties such as layer spacing, H-bonding, and interlayer diol geometry as a
 function of diol loading. Additional experiments reveal that GB adsorbs significant amounts of Ni2+,
 and that Ni-functionalized GB shows exceptional uptake of I−. Extensive characterization and
 molecular modeling provide preliminary insight into the surface charging and adsorption
 mechanisms underlying the observed ion uptake behavior.
 The following future work is planned:
 • Molecular modeling and experimental characterization to identify the sites and mechanisms for
 cation and anion adsorption. Molecular modeling will involve the interaction of metal cations and
 iodide anions with external surface sites (e.g., edges), to determine if interior or exterior surfaces
 are involved in ion binding. Modeling results will be compared with planned synchrotron X-ray
 absorption fine structure spectroscopy which will provide molecular-level detail of the
 coordination environment of adsorbed Ni2+ and I− ions. Structural properties of GB variants with
 different sized diol molecules will also be investigated.
 • Experimental evaluation of the sorption properties of GB under repository conditions. Laboratory
 characterization of sorbent performance for cation and anion sorption will include reversibility as
 well as the effects of temperature, pH, and competitor ions. Batch sorption and diffusion
 experiments will be coupled with microscale characterization (micro-CT and electron microscopy)
 with the goal of designing EBS materials that will effectively prevent the migration of both
 cationic and anionic radionuclides in repository environments.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                   234

 13 CHEMO-MECHANICAL CHARACTERIZATION OF CEMENT-ROCK
 INTERFACES
 13.1 Overview
 The long-term performance of cementitious materials at interfaces between cements and geologic
 strata (e.g., rock formations) is a critical safety component in many applications, including deep well
 storage (Newell & Carey, 2013; Hangx et al., 2016), boreholes for oil and gas extraction (Mangadlao
 et al., 2015), nuclear waste disposal (Ewing et al., 2016), and a broad spectrum of engineered
 barrier materials and structures (Ingraffea et al., 2014; Gaucher & Blanc, 2006). Cement mix
 designs and additives are chosen to achieve safety functions of structural performance, hydraulic
 containment, and contaminant retention at the interface with host rock. Failure of cements in this
 region can result from chemo-mechanical transformations at the cement-rock interface, induced by
 natural gradients in mineral assemblage, pore water pH, and porosity that promote mass transport
 and chemical reactions across the boundary. The important characteristics that may change spatially
 and temporally during long-term interactions are: porosity, solid volume, mineralogy, porewater pH
 and composition, hydration, mechanical properties (e.g., elastic modulus, ductility), and mass
 transport properties. For the proposed use of cement in potential borehole and mined disposal
 configurations, the long-term performance of the cements at the interface with host rock is
 uncertain. The characterization of chemo-mechanical changes at interfaces is needed: (1) for
 selecting appropriate formulation and construction of the cementitious materials for stable,
 long-term nuclear waste disposal (Gruber et al., 2022), and (2) to validate the mechanisms
 incorporated into models that are used to provide long-term performance estimates.
 Without a consistent way to characterize cement-host rock interfaces, it is difficult to compare
 cement characteristics on the micro and nanoscale and have a basis for comparison with simulation
 results. Thus, a methodology was developed to compare interface evolution under varying conditions
 with several different host rocks. The resulting data collected could then be used to calibrate model
 predictions of alteration zones, increasing the reliability of long-term estimates.
 The discussion below will briefly review these findings and outline the process for data analysis going
 forward for other interface studies. The discussion will also briefly introduce an additional interface
 project underway in collaboration with LANL.

 13.2 Objectives
 The overall objective for this project is to provide and demonstrate methodology for determining
 mechanistically based estimates of long-term changes in the chemo-mechanical properties of
 cementitious materials interfacing with host rocks relevant to potential nuclear waste disposal sites.
 This research (1) characterizes chemical and mechanical changes within the alteration zones of the
 cement interfacing with host rocks after controlled aging processes; (2) identifies structural features
 and chemical reactions that affect the fate of radionuclides at the interface of dissimilar materials;
 and (3) provides a basis to verify predicted changes in major mineral phase composition and
 distribution that affect the diffusion-controlled transport of tracer constituents using reactive
 transport modeling.

 13.3 Methods
       13.3.1    Workflow
 An approach for rock/cement interface characterization workflow was developed that facilitates
 comparison of geochemical speciation reactive-transport simulations with experimental results.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 235

 Experimental data is collected using a variety of instruments that characterize chemical and
 mechanical properties (Figure 13-1).

 the corresponding information obtained at each step. Optical Microscopy has since been added to
 the workflow, and an updated version of Figure 13-1 will be published in the next few months.

 To process the data collected from the instrument analysis, an analytical workflow has also been
 developed. This workflow incorporates technology provided on the instrument software, a machine
 learning program called AIVIA (Leica Microsystems, Germany), and other MATLAB developed codes.

 13.3.2 Sample Casting/Aging
 Carbonate rocks were obtained for this experiment from the Yamin Plain in Northern Negev, Israel.
 The rocks (oil shale, marl, and chalk) were analyzed for mineralogical composition via X-ray
 diffraction (XRD) analysis using the methodology in Gruber et al. (2022). Rock samples were
 sectioned in 1” cubes using a low-speed saw with diamond blades and embedded in epoxy using
 2” x 2” PVC cylinder molds, exposing only the top surface. After saturating samples, an ordinary

November 8, 2024                                                                                                           237

 Portland cement (OPC) paste was cast on top. The OPC cement paste was mixed using a w/b of 0.4
 and a 1% addition of MasterGlenium 7700 (BASF, Germany). The water for the cement mix was
 doped with heavy metal salts (lithium, cerium, chromium, and uranium) each to a concentration of
 500 mg/kg as analogue tracers for radionuclides. Samples were cured for 1 year at >95%RH and
 30C.
 The same rock material and doped cement samples also were processed and tested independently
 using standard EPA protocols. The purpose of these experiments was twofold: (1) examine the pH-
 dependent available content of major constituents at near-equilibrium conditions (EPA Method
 1313; EPA, 2012) and (2) measure the effective diffusivity of conservative and major reactive
 constituents (EPA Method 1315; EPA, 2017). For both experiments, elemental analysis was
 performed using inductively coupled plasma mass spectrometry (ICP-MS) and inductively coupled
 plasma optical emission spectroscopy (ICP-OES) (EPA, 2014). The results of these Methods for
 limestone and marl were previously reported by Gruber et al. (2022).

       13.3.3    MicroCT
 Aged samples were sent to Purdue University for MicroCT scanning and analysis, and were analyzed
 using a Zeiss Xradia 510 Versa X-ray Microscope, at an objective of 0.4X and an HE2 filter to
 minimize low energy X-rays. The goal of these preliminary scans was to identify a potential alteration
 zone depth (via grayscale density changes) and key features (large cracks, interfacial deposits)
 indicative of interfacial reactions. From this information, appropriate cutting planes for
 characterization were established.

 13.3.4 Sample Preparation – Sectioning
 After selecting a region of interest for characterization, samples were demolded and sectioned using
 a low-speed saw with a diamond blade. The interface for characterization was stabilized with an
 epoxy-attached stainless-steel plate, segmented from the thin section slide, and embedded in a 1.5”
 round silicon mold with epoxy. Then, samples were polished to specification for characterization.

 13.3.5 Sample Preparation – Polishing
 Samples were polished to achieve average surface roughness over three 50 µm2 area of <50 nm to
 meet requirements for subsequent nano-indentation. Polishing was carried out using a NANO-1000S
 benchtop polisher (PACE Technologies, USA) and ethanol as the lubricant. First, four grinding steps
 using progressively finer silicon carbide paper (320, 400, 600, and 1200 grit) were used in
 increasing intervals from ca. 30 seconds to 4 minutes, then three polishing steps using 9-, 6-, and 1-
 micron polycrystalline diamond pastes were applied in increasing intervals from ca. 6 to 10 minutes
 each. The success of this process was verified using surface probe microscopy (SPM) imaging on a
 Hysitron TI980 Triboindenter (Bruker, USA).

       13.3.6    SEM-EDS
 An FEI Quanta FEG 650 environmental scanning electron microscope (ESEM) (FEI Company, USA) fit
 with a Schottky field emission gun was used to collect backscatter electron (BSE) images and large
 area maps of major element composition using energy dispersive x-ray spectroscopy (EDS) across
 the sample surface.
 First, BSE was used to image the entire interface and select areas of interest for characterization.
 After performing Nanoindentation with a TI980 Triboindenter, the same areas were reimaged using
 the identical image collection specifications to capture chemical composition over precise indent
 locations.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                238

 13.3.7 Micro/Nano-indentation
 A Hystitron TI980 (Bruker, USA) was used to perform nanoindentation and microindentation on the
 cement in the interface sample. Areas of interest were selected and analyzed via SEM-EDS before
 and after indentation.
 For tribological (SPM) imaging and nanomechanical (nano-indentation) testing, a standard
 transducer with a diamond Berkovich tip was used. Nano-indentation was used to identify
 mechanically distinct phases and correlate the results with chemical phases identified using EDS.
 Specifically, grid nanoindentation was performed below the alteration zone and above the alteration
 zone.
 Reduced modulus and hardness were calculated through instrument analysis of the load vs.
 displacement curve using the Oliver and Pharr Method (Oliver & Pharr, 2004). The reduced modulus
 measures the combined elastic deformation of the sample and the tip and can be converted to
 elastic modulus (Shuman et al., 2007). Each indent curve is then visually evaluated, and malformed
 curves- indicating pore indentation, improper tip-to-sample contact, or sample cracking- were
 discarded.

 13.3.8 LA-ICP MS and MicroXRF
 Laser Ablation Inductively Coupled Plasma Mass Spectroscopy (LA-ICP-MS) (NWR 213 (Elemental
 Scientific Lasers, Bozeman, MT) coupled with NexION 2000B (Perkin Elmer Corporation, Waltham,
 MA)) and MicroXRF analysis for spatial gradients of trace species (e.g., dopants used as radionuclide
 surrogates) is under development.

 13.3.9 Image Segmentation and Analysis Programs
 Machine learning software AIVIA was used to perform object segmentation on BSE images. Programs
 provided with the previously described characterization equipment also were used in tandem with in-
 lab developed code (MATLAB) to further analyze the collected instrument data.

 13.4 Results and Discussion
 13.4.1 Oil Shale-OPC Paste Interface
 The results published in both the 2022 and 2023 milestone report remain up to date for the oil
 shale-OPC Paste interface. To summarize the previously reported results, after one year of aging, an
 oil shale-OPC Paste interface forms a two front alteration zone: a ~100 µm dissolution front leading
 a ~200 µm remineralization front, making a total alteration zone of 300 µm. A diagram of the
 identified zones is shown in Figure 13-2.

November 8, 2024                                                                                               239

 Figure 13-2. Diagram of identified zones in the oil shale-OPC Paste, overlayed on a BSE image.

 The alteration zone formed at the oil shale-OPC Paste interface showed a decrease in the
 mechanical properties of reduced modulus and hardness in the remineralization front region. This
 region also showed a depletion of calcium likely related to the dissolution of portlandite (CH), and an
 increase in the relative wt% of silicon, sulfur, and carbon. It was ultimately determined that this
 alteration zone mostly likely formed due to sulfate attack with calcium leaching – the oil shale in
 contact with the OPC has a relatively high content of sulfur-bearing organic material, as well as
 gypsum, compared to the cement which could drive concentration gradient-based reactions. The
 dissolution front region was characterized by its chemical gradients and increased porosity. This
 region signals the onset of the dissolution of the portlandite.

 13.4.2 Marl-OPC Paste Interface
 In the 2022 and 2023 milestone report, reference was made to the preliminary characterization of
 other cement rock interfaces, primarily marl and chalk interfacing with OPC Paste. The study of these
 additional interfaces is currently underway and has been refined to optimize the characterization
 equipment capabilities. Preliminary results on the formation of an alteration zone after one year at a
 Marl-OPC Paste interface are discussed below. Finally, the conclusion of this study will characterize
 the evolution of the alteration zone for both an oil shale- and marl-OPC Paste interface after four
 years of aging. These samples at these time stamps will give a more complete picture of the early-
 age reaction mechanisms impacting the chemical and structural changes of the cements at the
 interface.
 Though the following results are preliminary. For the marl-OPC sample, a clear alteration zone has
 formed, but seemingly through a different mechanisms than the oil shale-OPC paste. A BSE image of
 the interface is shown in Figure 13-3(A), with preliminary identified zones labeled. While distinct
 mechanical phases have not been segmented from the data yet, the probability density function
 (PDF) in Figure 13-3(B) shows a distinct decrease in the primary peak for reduced modulus values in
 the altered zone compared to the bulk cement. While the change in reduced modulus values is not
 unique to this interface type, the changes in chemistry are.

November 8, 2024                                                                                                 240

 b)

 Figure 13-3. (a) BSE image with preliminary regions labeled and (b) the reduced modulus PDFs
 from grids in the labeled regions
 The alteration zone of the marl-OPC sample showed some interesting changes in both the major and
 minor chemistry at the interface. Like the oil shale-OPC Paste sample, this alteration zone showed a
 decrease in calcium concentration and an increase in silicon and carbon concentrations. In direct
 contrast to the oil shale-OPC samples, the Marl-OPC samples showed a decrease in the sulfur
 concentrations in the alteration zone. Additionally, the Marl-OPC samples showed an increase in the
 concentration of potassium and sodium in the alteration zone. These results are shown in Figure
 13-4.

 Figure 13-4. Elements with changes in average concentration of elements between segmented
 regions
 Further analysis is in progress to analyze the cement phase chemistry present at the indent
 locations, to determine if a correlation exists between the change in mechanical properties and the
 mineralization/dissolution of phases. Additionally, image segmentation will be used to further refine
 results for accuracy of area coverage measurements and distribution of particles.
 For each of the interfaces described herein, a conceptual model is being developed to describe the
 reaction mechanisms and direction of the pathways that create the experimentally measured
 alteration zone.

 13.5 Experimental Comparison to Models
 As previously reported, Gruber et al., showed that when comparing these different carbonate rocks
 interfacing with OPC paste, oil shale was the most reactive, followed by marl, then limestone (Gruber
 et al., 2022). These long-term alteration estimates assuming saturated conditions are shown in
       Figure 13-5.

November 8, 2024                                                                                               241

 Figure 13-5. Model carbonation front predictions; from Gruber et. al. 2022

 Since these original long term prediction models were developed, there progress has been made to
 reflect the evolution of porosity over time within the simulations based on changes in mineral molar
 volume as a result of the contact at the interface. These “adaptive porosity” models can more
 accurately predict the depth of alteration by accounting for mineral dissolution and remineralization.
 The updated predictions for an Oil Shale-OPC Paste interface are shown in Figure 13-6.

 Figure 13-6. Changes in model predictions with updated adaptive porosity simulation.

 Based on these long-term scenarios, after 1,000 years, oil shale-OPC paste interfaces are now only
 predicted to be altered in the first 6 mm, compared to the prior model results which predicted an
 alteration of up to 16 mm. These updated models were resolved to a 1.5-year time frame and are
 summarized in Table 13-2 below.

 Table 13-2. Result Summary – Modeling vs. Experimental after 1.5 years of aging.

 Type
 Oil Shale Within ca. 300 µm of interface: 200 µm alteration zone: 200 m alteration zone:
 • Depleted portlandite depletion of Ca, decrease in both hardness and
 enrichment of Si, S, and C. reduced modulus; fewer high-
 • C-S-H/HG mixed changes stiffness phases such as CH,
 (e.g., CNASH increase) 100 m porous zone:
 depletion of Ca, and formation of a lower-
 • Ettringite increase enrichment of S and C stiffness CSH phase

 µm, then goes back to baseline porous layer due to equipment
 limitations

 The models have predicted, within experimental uncertainty, alteration front depths after 1.5 years
 of aging for an oil shale-OPC paste interface. Simulations for the Marl-OPC are currently being
 reviewed, and initial results indicate they have predicted the depth of alteration and porosity front for
 that interface as well.

 13.6 Hydrothermal Reactor Project
 The objective of this study is to utilize what was learned about characterizing the cement-rock
 interfaces and apply this to a new interface in an elevated temperature reaction. For this study, the
 experimental conditions were selected to reflect, i) a potential multi-barrier system for nuclear waste
 storage, ii) the reactions at the interface under the most “extreme” predicted conditions (fully
 saturated,120⁰C, and >10Bar) and iii) the gradient that forms as a result of the complex interactions.
 Several cement mix designs, as well as different temperatures and time steps were tested, to
 optimize the experiment and promote the most efficient characterization. Ultimately, a 10% silica
 fume replacement cement sample was used as the reference cement material, which was cast into a
 15 mm diameter by 20 mm thick disks and placed into the hydrothermal reactor with bentonite and
 synthetic argillaceous groundwater, then reacted for a series of timesteps at 120⁰C. Thus far, a
 reaction at 1 week has been completed and characterization of the gradient is underway. Also
 currently underway is the next round of samples, which will be reacted for four weeks at the same
 temperature and pressure conditions.

 13.7 Summary and Future Work
 The most important work completed this year has been expanding the application of the
 characterization and analytical workflows developed for the early stages of the cement-rock interface
 alteration as a function of aging. The addition of Marl-OPC interfaces to the data that includes the oil
 shale-OPC interface, and the added analysis of reaction type/rate changes using the four-year-aged
 samples, will inform and improve the reliability and assumptions for long-term prediction models.
 Successful progress has also been made in the development and execution of the Hydrothermal
 Reactor Project, which has been an ongoing collaboration with LANL, SNL, and Vanderbilt University.
 The information gained from these experiments will advance the understanding of the types and
 rates of reactions that occur when elevated temperatures are applied to the interface of these
 materials and the extent to which these variables impact the long-term durability of the cement.
 Over the next year, all currently ongoing experiments will be completed, and there will be multiple
 papers published. These papers will discuss the different cement-rock interfaces (Oil Shale-OPC and
 Marl-OPC) at 1.5 and four years of aging and the bentonite-cement interface at elevated
 temperatures.
